[scan 6]

## Die Psychologie der Hegelschen Schule — The Psychology of the Hegelian School

examined by Dr. F. *Exner*, Full Professor of Philosophy at the Imperial and Royal University of Prague.

Leipzig, Friedrich Fleischer. 1842.

[scan 8]

### Vorbemerkung. — Preliminary Remark.

This little work was begun with the intention of having it printed as a critique in a literary journal. I found, however, that I had little hope of establishing convictions in my readers about the subject under discussion unless I went into it in detail. This, together with the interest that a wider public continues to devote to the system examined here, led me to let the work appear in its present form. Had this been part of the plan from the outset, the arrangement of the whole would no doubt have become more organic, and the treatment of many a detail more thorough. I am, however, [p. I.IV] aware that well-tested thoughts often underlie the hasty exposition, and I am convinced that the little work, if it is of use at all, will be of use in its present form as well.

November 1841.

The Author.

[scan 10] A hard battle is at present being fought by German science. The Hegelian philosophy stands in the field with high-flying banners, boldly carrying its attack in all directions; the other philosophical systems, as well as theology and jurisprudence, and whatever is akin to them, oppose it with ever-growing courage, now that the first consternation is over. It does, to be sure, offer peace everywhere, and assures them that it is quite willing to let its opponents stand: but these believe that the peace offered is precisely the worst war, namely a half-concealed one. Truth, they say, is indeed conceded to them, but a truth of the kind that is really untruth; they are allowed to stand, but only by being declared sublated. Not for nothing do they feel the strength of two millennia in their veins, and they mean to show by the deed that they are there, expecting that the right to be so will then no doubt be conceded to them by their adversary according to her own principles. So the dispute takes its course; victory will remain where the truth is.

But where it is, that is of interest nowadays to every thinking person among us, even if he has no wish to swell an already much confused clamor with his own voice. Here, then, one side of the Hegelian system is to be considered which, lying further from the chief objects of the loud feud, the better bears an impartial discussion, and is also not likely to have received such discussion in any great detail as yet.

To test the correctness of a philosophical system by its consequences has from time immemorial been attempted and permitted. Among the [p. I.2] consequences must also be counted those parts of the system in which the principles and methods demonstrated in other parts come to be applied. It is especially desirable here if the developments are of such a kind, and carried so far, that they come up against the objects of daily experience and are meant to make these comprehensible. Here it is shown what the system is worth. Reality sets out its creations with very definite features; it lets nothing be argued away from it, and bends to no favorite system and to no passion of the beholder. False theory does not reach it; it stays hovering in airy heights, where it likes to clothe empty shapes in dark words. True theory penetrates into real things and makes them transparent to thought. Thereby it ultimately becomes practical, and is able to give helpful precepts by which desired ends can be attained through the transformation of the real. One part of philosophy of the kind described is *psychology*. In it, views come to be applied which were already established in other parts; but it is itself meant to explain the states of the soul given through experience. The moralist, the politician, the educator, and whoever else needs a thorough insight into the human soul, come to it for instruction. A psychology that proved incapable of affording such instruction would show that it had not solved its task; a philosophical system that produced an incapable psychology would necessarily suffer from essential defects.

The works we propose to consider first are: “Psychology, or the Science of Subjective Spirit; by K. *Rosenkranz*, Full Professor of Philosophy at Königsberg” (Königsberg 1837); — “Anthropology and Psychology, or the Philosophy of Subjective Spirit; by Dr. K. *Ludwig Michelet*, Associate Professor of Philosophy at the Friedrich-Wilhelms-Universität in Berlin” (Berlin 1840); “Outline of Psychology for Lectures, by Dr. *Joh. Ed. Erdmann*, Full Professor of Philosophy at the University of Halle-Wittenberg.” (Leipzig 1840.) — They constitute pretty well the complete psychological literature of the Hegelian school; an earlier work by *Mussmann* (1827) [p. I.3] is declared by this school itself to be worthless and forgotten; *Daub’s* “Lectures on Philosophical Anthropology” (1838), so far as they belong to it, contain nothing essentially different from the writings to be discussed, and have in part been used in them; any other separate work of the same school on the same subject is not known to us. The above works are based on the section in *Hegel’s* Encyclopedia that is headed: Subjective Spirit; *Rosenkranz* declares his book outright to be a compendium to that outline. It is therefore the psychology of the Hegelian school that we subject to examination in those works.

What we take to be the task of psychology has already been indicated. As so-called natural science teaches us to know the changes of the external world, so psychology is to teach us to know those inner changes which are called states of the soul. It must therefore exhibit the causes and laws of these changes. But it is a condition that it describe precisely every state of the soul it means to explain; for how should we know what it is speaking of if it does not say so clearly? Perhaps it is compelled to go far back and to refer in many ways to other parts of philosophy; perhaps it feels inclined to give us information not merely about the states of spirit but about spirit itself; but whatever it may give, and however high or low it may begin, it must end by precisely *describing* the states of the soul to us and *explaining* them from their causes and according to their laws. This is the least it has to accomplish. Or should our knowledge of the human soul content itself with less than our knowledge of the corporeal world? Then the exertion of our thinking would correspond poorly to the worth of its objects! But perhaps the ever-changing states of our inner life cannot be grasped in fixed concepts at all, cannot be precisely described? Whoever believes this must renounce knowledge of them; he may not write a psychology. Yet we all know that matters are not so bad. Daily experience has taught us that our thoughts are something other than our sensations; a rising wish something other than the reciting of a poem learned by heart; the [p. I.4] psychologists have long since depicted many states of the soul very aptly, and made more or less successful classifications of them; there is no reason to despair of the undertaking, however difficult it may be. Even if it is not possible for description to follow all the transformations of the soul, one can perhaps do as the mathematicians do, who, when they seek a curve, often first determine only individual distinguished points of it, say the points of inflection or of intersection. But *laws* of the states of the soul — are there such too? One can deny it if one does the same for the appearances of the external world as well; otherwise every reason that speaks here holds there too. One has to choose between causes and the laws of their operation, or blind chance. That human beings can be educated would by itself already suffice to decide us for the former; that there would be no science of the purely accidental, and hence, in the second case, again no psychology, and that only insight into the laws of those changes could lead to practically useful results, may be remarked in passing. So we hold fast, then, to the task of this science stated above.

But will the authors of the works named agree, or can they complain that they are being judged by a standard wholly foreign to their system? *Rosenkranz* calls it, on p. 215, a just demand of empirical consciousness to find itself again in the concepts of speculation; *Erdmann* declares in § 3 that his work comprises both empirical and rational psychology; the tables of contents contain the current names of the best-known states of the soul: thus the task of apprehending what is given is acknowledged. It is doubtful, however, whether the authors also consider themselves obliged to state the *laws* of the states of the soul. They do, to be sure, speak countless times of a necessity in the development of the moments of spirit, or, as we have said so far following common usage, of the states of the soul; and the lawfulness we mentioned is indeed a kind of necessity, even if under peculiar conditions; attempts at explanations in our sense also actually occur, which in fact acknowledge the validity of our demand: [p. I.5] Yet we will not conceal that their thought on this point does not wholly coincide with ours. The peculiar nature of this difference, and with it the decision of the question last raised, can however only emerge later. We have set out the reasons for our demand, and for the time being they may achieve what they can. But it must be repeated that description and explanation of the states of the soul are the *least* that is demanded here; whatever we receive beyond that we shall welcome most gratefully. With this the field of accomplishments has, it is hoped, been staked out spaciously enough.

The three works to be examined contain the same main thoughts; even whole sentences agree word for word; manifold divergences occur only in subordinate matters. We shall now consider chiefly the work of *Rosenkranz*, and draw the others into the consideration at the appropriate places. Further, we shall first investigate from the standpoint given above to what extent the works before us satisfy the principal tasks of psychology named; then we shall go into the peculiar standpoint of the authors themselves. It must also be recalled that, according to the fundamental view of the school, to be illuminated more precisely later, the familiar groups of states of the soul which are called sensation, presenting, thinking, desiring, etc., are developments of the human spirit appearing in a necessary gradation.

*The first part*, called anthropology, treats of spirit in its unity with corporeality, or of spirit as soul. It takes up more than half of the book, and discusses the determinacy of spirit by planetary life; the races of mankind; the temperaments, aptitudes and idiosyncrasies; the stages of life and the differences of sex; sleep and waking; the sensations; dreaming, presentiments, visions, animal magnetism; self-feeling together with the diseases of the soul; mnemonics, physiognomy and craniology. Most of the objects discussed here really do belong to that region, hitherto considered very obscure, where spirit and body have intercourse with one another. Many thinkers have declared them downright unknowable. Still [p. I.6] more may, before reading the works at hand, have been of the opinion — and we ourselves must count ourselves among them — that states which announce themselves as products of two factors cannot well be comprehended *before* their factors; that, therefore, anatomy, organic chemistry, physiology, etc. must first duly bring the body to light, and psychology the spirit, before one may venture upon those states with any hope of success. Our authors believe, however, that they have overcome all difficulties and seen through those states to the very bottom. It was, we hear, only narrowness, unskillfulness, that kept other psychologists from reaching this result. No doubt one will be eager to learn of the new discoveries. Since we shall have to return to these objects later, we will for now present only some of them.

In the human organism there are found several so-called systems; on the one-sided predominance of one or another of them rests, according to p. 34, the *temperament*. Now, as is well known, various numbers of those systems are counted, at present usually six: the reproductive, respiratory and blood systems as the vegetative ones, the skeletal, muscular and nervous systems as the animal ones. So there will presumably, one would suppose from the definition, also be six temperaments. But people generally speak of only four; how is this to be taken? *Rosenkranz* first ignores the skeletal and respiratory systems; thus four remain. But the muscular system does not usually play any role in the doctrine of the temperaments; he therefore combines it with the blood system, although the blood nourishes not merely the muscle but just as well all the other parts of the body. Now we have three systems; so we must split again. This can, to be sure, be done just as well with the nervous system as with the blood system, but we stay with the latter, with its veins and arteries, and so happily come out with four temperaments. — The matter can be managed differently still. *Michelet*, on p. 137, takes Galen’s four humors: blood, bile, black bile and phlegm, and so reaches the goal quite simply. Indeed, since one does not enter into reasons at all, one could, as history shows, have varied things in many more ways. There now follows [p. I.7] a detailed description of the psychical differences that are supposed to proceed from those physical ones, just as it is usually to be read. Only one new trait of character do we find there: the object of the melancholic is the past, that of the sanguine the present, that of the choleric the future; the phlegmatic is indifferent to time. That rounds itself off nicely; yet we have hitherto always been of the opinion that the melancholic is just as melancholic with regard to what is past and present as with regard to what is to come, and likewise every other temperament. Now one will expect the authors to demonstrate *how* the psychical differences set up result, and must result, from those physical differences; but on this they are silent. What, then, does absolute knowing, which sees through body and soul in their innermost depths, disclose to us here? A clumsy groping after what is given in experience and in other writings, an unfaithful apprehension of the empirical, and no explanation of it at all.

Quite in the same spirit other hitherto puzzling things are treated, such as the innate differences of talents, the idiosyncrasies, sleeping and waking. Idiosyncrasy, for example, *Rosenkranz* on p. 51 declares to be the immediate unity, or the immediate contradiction, or the indifference between the individual and objectivity, so that it is either sympathy or antipathy or apathy. The concrete cases, he admits, he cannot explain, but he can explain the matter in general. And how? “Our psychical rapport with the external world is more alive and more far-reaching than we know with our ordinary experience.” By this, then, physicians and educators may guide themselves in future when they wish to cure such states.

Waking is, according to p. 69, the act of judging: I am, the positing of the distinction of subject and object. Now since children are usually born awake, because crying, the author presumably imagines that they leap into the world with the judgment: I am. But why then does he later take so much trouble to exhibit self-consciousness as a far higher moment of development? According to the above definition we would rather have to believe that even the fetus already has self-consciousness, [p. I.8] were we not instructed on p. 67, from the concept of sleep, that the fetus is always asleep, and that precisely for this reason the human being, once born, assumes in sleep the posture of the fetus. With regret the writer of these lines noted here that he has a very obtuse sleep, since with him this really is otherwise. Remarkable, too, is certainly the explanation, described as “ingenious” on p. 16, where the lunar determinacy of man is under discussion, of the sureness with which sleepwalkers move about on roofs. They have, namely, besides the earth’s center also a moon center, by which they are kept in a certain suspension. If our astronomers and physicists recognized this truth, they would be compelled to reform their entire science philosophically! No doubt it is only the notorious changeableness of the moon that is to blame for many a sleepwalker’s falling down, and it is among the fine virtues of our earth to let no one fall upward.

Let us pass on to states which are more commonly claimed by psychology proper. Here we come upon a well-known group whose truest essence is supposed to consist in being determinacies of spirit through corporeality that are changeable and bound to the course of life. If one considers that earlier the permanent and the periodically changing determinacies were under discussion, and that the planetary determinacies, the differences of race and sex, and the like were counted among them, one will probably expect that the turn will now come to the countless bodily diseases that plague man temporarily and affect the spirit in many ways, and one will look forward to a good philosophical pathology. But that is not what is meant; it is the *sensations* that are under discussion, and their essence is supposed to be expressed in the above. Probably the author felt that some *differentia specifica* was still lacking; therefore he says on p. 73: sensation is spirit in its immediate identity with nature. But now, this is surely supposed to be the concept of all the states of spirit discussed in this first part; so at least it was expressly said on p. 9; instead of a narrower concept, then, we have an even wider one. Let us seek help on [p. I.9] p. 74, where it is said: sensation is the immediate existence of spirit; further: it is motion posited by affection of the organism; finally: it is motion posited by the spontaneity of spirit. *Michelet* adds on p. 243: sensation is “that in which the whole of nature appears as a dull weaving of spirit within itself;” it is the first glimmer of consciousness, where all determinations of natural being are determinations of the soul. Whoever can make use of one of these explanations may choose it; to us the master’s dictum, that sensation is the unsayable, the irrational, seems to find a brilliant confirmation in these efforts of his pupils.

If one sees from this with what precision this state of the soul is described, a sample may now also be given of the way in which *Michelet* explains it in detail. Ordinary thinkers might here put to themselves questions such as the following: How, then, do external things act on the sensory nerves? What changes do they produce in them? Is this action confined to the place where it is exerted, or does it propagate itself, and whither? How does the state of the nerve bring about the sensation? Why does this nerve produce colors, that one sensations of tone? and the like. *Michelet*, now, teaches as follows on p. 245: the soul needs organs in order to sense. We agree. He continues: the whole process is a syllogism; the organs are the *terminus medius* [middle term], through it subject and predicate, i.e. spirit and object, close together; that is how the thing proceeds! — When the master put forward the proposition that every thing is properly nothing but a syllogism, he hardly foresaw what fruitful applications his pupils would one day make of it. But we have not finished with this remarkable subject. A knowing which step by step follows nothing but the necessary development of the concept is also able to tell us why we have precisely five senses. The essential determinacy of matter, we read on p. 246, is gravity; the first sense is therefore the sense of gravity, *touch*. Strangely enough, it is conceded that this sense nevertheless senses not merely gravity but also cohesion, warmth, bodily shape and the like. But not merely subsisting, but also perishing [p. I.10] matter is sensed by the sense; for this it becomes active, it dissolves matter. Its perishing is twofold, “either the secret, quiet, gradual perishing of evaporation, or the real, actual one, through dissolution in water”; thus we have *smell* and *taste*. Hear it, chemists and geologists: there are two kinds of perishing of material things, evaporation and dissolution in water! Finally we also sense “the matter of things that is already spiritualized and yet remains ideal in its subsistence. The spiritualized properties of bodies are concussion or trembling, and light refracted by corporeality, or color. These ideal properties are, in relation to matter, the most abstract, the most remote from matter.” Thus result *hearing* and *sight*. With these spiritualized properties one is involuntarily reminded that some people also see and hear spirits, and one could almost comprehend the possibility. The individual senses are now considered more closely in turn; the sense of sight comes first. Of it it is said on p. 247 that seeing “grasps things as quiet subsistence in space,” and the other works join in in chorus. It is a misfortune when a distinguished man is often not exact with individual propositions in his writings; a misfortune which, as *Rosenkranz* himself confesses, befell *Hegel*. For the disfiguring little dot that such a man has carelessly tossed off in an unguarded moment turns up in every writing of his pupils, squeezed out at length and breadth, as an ugly blot. Or must one seriously remind our philosophers that they see not merely bodies at rest but also bodies in motion? What is bad, however, is the frivolity with which so important and difficult a subject as the formation of spatial presentations is here skipped over. The authors surely cannot possibly believe that they have solved these difficulties; do they, then, not know them, or does it please them to ignore them? But let us hear further. For seeing one needs light; light, however, we are told, is quiet expansion in space; it is the highest spiritualization of matter, “immaterial matter.” We will not ask what use a Cauchy, say, will draw from these propositions; but when it is asserted [p. I.11] that in pure light one does indeed see, but only one’s own seeing, we must greatly regret that no one has been willing to betray to us what color, then, seeing has. Seeing is, further, naturally also a syllogism; the *terminus medius* is the optic nerve, the extremes are spirit and color. Color and nerve, however, are themselves in turn mediated by a transparent medium, which yields a second syllogism. Now *Hegel’s* Logic teaches, however, that the *terminus medius* must always participate in the nature of both extremes; the application of this is as follows: The nerve, as middle term between spirit and color, is itself spirit, for a reason to be discussed later; but it is also color, or at least light, an inner organic light; only because the eye is itself light can it see, and the dictum of Empedocles, that we perceive the light outside us only through the light within us, is perfectly correct. — Certainly a remarkable discovery for our physiologists and physicists; it explains a well-known feat of Münchhausen’s. The man had better look out, or else present-day philosophy will rob him of all his wit. In the other syllogism, which precedes the first, the transparent medium is the middle term between the nerve and the color. It participates in the nature of color insofar as it is light, in the nature of the nerve insofar as it consists of the crystalline lens and the humors of the eye; or better: the nerve is inner light, crystalline lens and humors are light, and color is light modified by darkness; thus all three are light, and the rules of logic are satisfied. — There follows the explanation of hearing. Hearing is supposed to rest on time, as seeing rests on space; indeed, we are again assured in chorus that tone is nothing but “filled time.” This too is one of the unfortunate propositions of *Hegel* referred to above. That musicians often fill their time with tones is well known; others, however, are accustomed to do so with other things. Hearing is supposed to be the deepest sense and to make known to us the inwardness of bodies, their coherence. This is emphasized in particular by *Rosenkranz*, p. 76. But we very much fear that he himself, if one were to blindfold him in good Pestalozzian fashion, would only [p. I.12] poorly hold up. We urgently commend to physicists p. 253 in *Michelet*, where they will learn that solid bodies do not conduct sound as well as air does. — The next two senses are the practical ones, smell and taste; through them the subject is supposed to take into itself the matter, too, of the things sensed, and the one sensing is supposed to form an amalgam with what is sensed. Saliva, which is necessary for tasting, is declared to be a specific water generated out of the tongue, which the physiologists, who speak of special salivary glands and the like, may again take note of. — Touch, finally, is called the most perfect sense, because it embraces at once chemism and mechanism, the two main stages of nature, the former as heat, the latter as gravity. The third stage of nature otherwise maintained by the school, organism, seems to have been forgotten in the distribution of the senses. Moreover, just as light outside us is sensed through the organic light within us, so earth, air and water outside us are sensed through the earth, the air and the water within us. This may suffice to show with what fidelity the facts are here apprehended and with what acuteness they are explained.

From *outer* sensation *Rosenkranz*, p. 94, passes over to *inner sensation*, i.e. the primitively mental sensation, or the one excited through the spontaneous activity of spirit. Insofar, so he teaches, as this senses the mental in its universality, it is called feeling; if it senses the mental in its concrete individualization, it is either positive or negative or mixed sensation. Instead of positive and negative one can also say sthenic and asthenic. As examples of feelings the feeling of truth and religious feeling etc. are cited, as examples of these sensations, courage, fear and the like. We all know these states from experience, but we would hardly have recognized them from the above description, which after all claims to state their most proper essence. Inner sensation is supposed to arise through excitation. For that, two things are required: something that excites and something that is excited. What excites is here spirit; what is the thing excited? It is not to be found out. If we recall earlier explanations [p. I.13] and put them together with those given here, we must say: inner sensation is the immediate existence of spirit, which through spontaneous activity excites a sensation, i.e. its immediate existence; or it is motion posited through the spontaneous activity of spirit. Are we now instructed? Had it pleased the author to attempt a description of sthenic and asthenic sensation, he might perhaps have got on the track of the matter. Instead of that he cites only one example of sthenic sensation, revenge, and says it is sthenic “for I want to procure a right for myself.” Are we now to say that every sensation is sthenic which wants to procure its right for itself? That would hardly do. So perhaps the one which wants something at all? However, if sensations do always want something, then fear too wants something, just as well as revenge, and yet it is cited as an example of the asthenic sensations. Feelings have always been the cross of psychologists. Whoever knows how to get at them may flatter himself that he has found the right path into the science; whoever has not even made an attempt to penetrate into the secret of their origin and their transformations ought to learn modesty at this point. Our authors believe they are done with the main thing; only the concrete is supposed here and there still to require a further exposition, which, however, is properly no concern of psychology. They have given us the above definitions and, in addition, *Hegel’s* dictum about the dull weaving of spirit, and have highly extolled the latter as a particularly brilliant thought; what more can we ask?! For the present, hardly anything else than an honest confession as to whether it has pleased them here to jest with the reader.

According to p. 103 in *Rosenkranz*, sensation disappears with time, but its essential content remains. We thereby come to a main point of psychology. The *reproduction* of sense presentations speaks for their persistence; to demonstrate its necessity, however, is no easy matter; for that reason it has recently been urged again from various sides. Let us now hear the explanation that is offered us here. “The [p. I.14] spirit is ideal spatiality, which can harbor an infinite content within itself without that content needing to dispute its own place. In this boundless expansion, therefore, sensation, which vanishes away with time, is preserved forever.” “Every sensation scratches in its trace, and this, were it only a little scratch, cannot be smoothed out again.” Are these explanations? What would the somewhat hard-to-comprehend expansion of spirit avail if it pleased the sensations to go out again through the same gate by which they came? if spirit had no force to hold them fast within itself? But whence such a force? Furthermore, into what do the sensations scratch their traces, and do they not perhaps leave them behind empty, behind the hurrying foot, like the antediluvian animals? It is indeed also said that the sensations become moments of existent spirit, and for that reason, it seems, they are supposed to be imperishable. But how many feelings and convictions, which after all are also moments of spirit, a child has, which, once they are destroyed, the man is never again able to produce within himself! And is not the *coming* and *going* of a sensation, too, a moment, a state of spirit? and yet no sensation is always coming or always going. Here one must penetrate deeper, or one has said nothing. — All the outer and inner sensations together, it goes on to say, form a summary unity, which is the identical totality of them and the immediate subjectivity of spirit.

In what follows, a rich group of states of the soul is brought together under the name of *dream life*. They are all supposed to be characterized by the fact that in them subjectivity loses itself in indeterminate objectivity. If we look at the earlier determination of the concept, this has no other sense than: the summary unity of the sensations loses itself in the individual sensations; which seems to have no sense whatever. First, now, there is talk of *dreaming*. It is taken now as the unity, now as the mixing of sleeping and waking, both of which are obviously false. In *Michelet* the soul always dreams; in *Rosenkranz* spirit as embryo is dreamless. [p. I.15] This may rest where it is; a decision on it would probably require more than has been cleared up here. We should like above all to learn whence come the well-known peculiarities of dreams, their proverbial absurdity, and, by way of exception, again their uncanny sensibleness. We now read, pp. 109 ff.: In dreams, presentations relate to one another mechanically, their combination is contingent; but the possibility of a connection is also there. Further instruction is not given, but many known and less known experiences are — for how much has not been written on this subject! — and all sorts of remarks that read quite well, even if they do not advance scientific insight. *Michelet*, p. 167, finds the difference between dreaming and waking in this: that in dreams space and time are our own, and the positing of the categories depends on us. This is meant to say that in dreams our soul is active with a particular independence. Experience, however, would hardly correspond to this; rather, whoever recalls a thoroughly absurd dream of the past night feels at the same time that it was not his true, proper I that produced that state — an I which would, after all, have thought and felt quite otherwise — but rather that some alien power had taken possession of his spirit and forced such strange things out of it. It is true that in dreams spirit as a rule lets itself be determined only a little by the constitution of real things; it heeds neither time, nor space, nor categories; but does not the same thing happen to a certain degree in waking phantasizing as well? Certainly, answers *Michelet*; but this phantasizing is also a dreaming. Well, we believed that what was to be discussed here was dreaming proper, the activity of the soul during sleep; by letting things blur into one another one does not learn to distinguish them and to know them according to their peculiar character. — There follow *presentiment*, *vision* and *second sight*. They are declared by *Rosenkranz*, p. 122, to be a dreaming in the waking state. That might be well enough, if only we knew from the foregoing to some degree what dreaming and waking actually consist in: For a more precise determination it is said: presentiment [p. I.16] is a being-outside-itself of the soul which has the form of being-within-itself, of sensation; vision is a being-within-itself which has the form of intuiting being-outside-itself, inasmuch as the content of the presentiment condenses to a distinctness which is identical with common actuality and can therefore be confused with it; the unity of presentiment and vision is second sight, where presentiment appears in the form of common actuality. The last could be put in intelligible language roughly thus: second sight is a presentiment in which one believes one perceives a fact alongside other things that one really perceives; but how what precedes, in particular that “being-outside-itself of the soul,” could be replaced by words that have a sensible meaning, and without becoming entirely stale, we do not know. To explain those facts, there is talk of the probability of electrical influences and of an intensification of the common sense; riddles are solved by riddles. One honorable side of this work, however, we must acknowledge: sound common sense still has its little word to say in it too. Things have come so far — one must say it with deep regret — with many of our philosophers that on making this discovery one cannot help feeling a heartfelt gratitude, as for a valuable gift long since no longer hoped for. *Rosenkranz* declares the states discussed to be purely subjective; he looks with mistrust on the countless little stories of presentiments and visions; he reminds us how people are wont to remember only those that later proved true, and to forget the others, far more numerous; that the actual coming to pass of what was presentiently foreseen is frequently, like the presentiment itself, purely contingent, and the like. It is otherwise with *Michelet*. Whatever ever frightened children and alarmed confused minds, whatever fantasts old and new reported, perhaps also what jesters invented: of this a credulous serving is dished up here; this is put together in thoughtless earnest, slotted into the system, and scientifically explained. The explanation runs, pp. 178 ff.: “Presentiment and vision rend the veil of past and future life, leap over time and space, and conjure up into the presence of intuition objects which are not yet or are no longer.” [p. I.17] The necessity of this is cognizable *a priori*. The soul, the universal ideality of time and space, leaps over the conditions of the knowing of the understanding, inasmuch as everything spatial and temporal is ideally present in it at every moment. — Surely never has anyone tried to dispose of something significant with emptier words! But apart from the utter impotence, here coming to light, to say more than the most commonplace about those objects: have we not enough of the most narrow-minded superstition in the world at present, without absolute knowing itself performing midwife’s services for it? We too are convinced that there is much in those regions that deviates more or less from the customary course of events; but whoever knows what it takes to establish quite ordinary facts so firmly that they afford room and a secure footing for a strictly scientific treatment will admit that with those objects we are still a long way from having got that far. Under such circumstances it befits science to refer the importunate, with calm earnestness, to the future, to draw attention to the difficulty of the experiences still to be made and to the inadequacy of those made so far, and to warn emphatically against fraud and superstition. This is the only worthy conduct in matters of such practical importance. Whoever believes he knows where he does not know, and confirms and encourages where he ought to warn, must answer for it when, a short while ago, one of the most eminent scholars of Germany reproached the philosophers of certain schools with being “the stultifiers of the age.” —

To this group of states of the soul are finally reckoned also *acting in dreams*, *magnetic sleep* and *clairvoyance*. The first two states contain, according to *Rosenkranz*, p. 138, nothing that could not be comprehended from the concept of dream on the one hand and that of illness on the other. The concept of illness, however, he has up to this point remained owing us, and what that of dream will avail can be judged from what was said about it above. Two pages earlier the author himself considered it necessary to call to his aid the permeability of matter, which is supposed to let the dynamic and organic processes pass through it, and moreover declared that it [p. I.18] is still very doubtful what the far-seeing of somnambulists in magnetic sleep is actually like. Does this mean that the matter is comprehended, and comprehended from the mere concepts of dream and of illness? As regards animal magnetism, *Hegel* asserted that it had essentially advanced psychology in most recent times, and *Michelet* faithfully repeats it after him. For that reason we must consider its presentation more closely, which, however, we save for a later place.

To the dream life just discussed, *self-feeling* is opposed. The word says that what is in question is the feeling of one’s own I, and so the state shows itself to be most closely related to self-consciousness. The history of philosophy teaches what an important role the concept of this state has played; systems that continue to exert considerable influence begin from it as their basis. This alone already summons psychologists to proceed here with care and circumspection, and honestly to make their due contribution to the solution of disputed questions. For this it will be necessary that they tell us, first of all, what we are to think of under the I of a human being; further, what is actually there in spirit when the feeling of one’s own I is present; and out of what and according to what laws it develops. At the close of the doctrine of sensations, p. 102, it was said that spirit appears as a being-one, which, however, for the present is nothing but the totality of all the sensations posited from outside and from within, a summary unity; and that this is the immediate subjectivity of spirit. Against this there is nothing to object. It must be permitted to bring together a multiplicity under some name and to regard it as a unity. Of dream life it was said later that in it subjectivity loses itself in indeterminate objectivity. We remarked that, if the concept of subjectivity that had been set up were held fast, the statement seemed to have no sense. But already on p. 107 we suddenly read that in self-feeling all the content of sensing is taken back into the “simplicity” of identical subjectivity. If this is not a surreptitious substitution, it at least looks exactly like one. One knows the difference between empirical and pure consciousness, [p. I.19] and the latter does indeed point to a strict simplicity of the I. So we see quite well the goal that is being steered toward. And how is it reached? By substituting, in a seemingly most innocent manner, “simplicity” for the earlier “unity.” And one has good reason to do this very quietly; for a slight attentiveness on the reader’s part suffices to notice the enormous difference that obtains between simplicity and summary unity. It is not to correct the surreptitious substitution, but only to repeat it, when on p. 245 the self is declared to be the unity pervading everything particular in sensation; for a sum never pervades its individual summands. What, then, is self-feeling, and how does it arise? It is the feeling of that unity, and it arises with necessity. So much we are told, and with that this important subject is disposed of. Whence the unity? what the feeling? why the necessity? All this seems to the author not worth mentioning; he turns to the particular determinatenesses of self-feeling.

Whoever reads through these works with an earnest desire for instruction must be seized by a desolation growing section by section, which is only gradually alleviated by observing the droll variety with which, again and again, empty nothingness springs forth from the bursting envelope of vapor. — As the particular determinatenesses of self-feeling there are introduced *healthy* and *diseased self-feeling*. One had good reason to expect something else; for the pure I is, after all, opposed by the empirical one, and the author says on p. 107 that in self-feeling all particular sensations become predicates of the subject. But the predicates are surely that by which the subject is *determined*. However, *Hegel* had made that arrangement, and science may strike up a song of triumph for it, because he thereby conquered for diseases of the soul their systematic place. Healthy self-feeling as a mental state is, according to p. 143, the unhindered fluidity of all the acts of intelligence; it is the alternation of absorption and distraction, so that neither of these states attains extreme dominance. Diseased [p. I.20] self-feeling is, according to p. 144, the self-diremption of the same; according to p. 146 the essence of mental illness consists in a moment of the psychic totality fixing itself for itself, and thereby confronting the movement of the totality as something inorganic. We gladly admit that correct features of the picture of the health and illness of spirit are drawn here; but in what necessary connection do they stand with self-feeling? The unhindered fluidity of the acts of intelligence, the free alternation between absorption and distraction, are not states of self-feeling; self-feeling is not present in every one of them; it can merely come in addition to it; it is itself one of the acts that alternate. A historian can reflect for hours on a state of affairs of ancient Rome; he can pass over in full freedom of spirit to related states of affairs of past and present, compare them with that one, and transfer the results obtained to the future, without once in all this thinking of himself, feeling himself. Rather, when a noise from outside, or the sensation of hunger, or the observation that his memory has failed him somewhere, reminds him of himself, he feels disturbed and is often displeased. That inquiry is a perfectly healthy state, but it is no kind of self-feeling. The man sunk in gloom in his misfortune has self-feeling, because it is *his* misfortune that occupies him; mentally ill, on the other hand, he is because he *cannot get away* from his object. If another’s misfortune drove him mad, he would be mentally ill even if for days on end he had neither thought nor feeling for his own self. Among mental illnesses the author rightly reckons mania too; but it is found often enough in animals, to which he nevertheless does not grant self-feeling. Who is able to recognize again in such speculation the concepts of empirical consciousness? In general, the undersigned knows of no work of psychological literature in which the facts — one must say it openly — are treated with such great levity as in the present one. Is not the essence of mental illness stated, p. 146, to be that a moment of the psychic totality fixes itself for itself [p. I.21] and thereby confronts the movement of the totality as something inorganic? As its kinds there are then named: *imbecility*, *derangement* and *mania*; to which of these, now, does that definition apply? Neither to imbecility nor to mania, according to the author’s own presentation; thus, of the three, only to one, derangement, in which a more or less fixed idea appears. Does any trace of a scientific conscience still remain where one considers it superfluous to hold fast to a definition for three pages, and the generic concept is already forgotten with its nearest species? Of mania it is said, p. 147, that it wants to make the impossible possible, but senses the impossibility of its realization and is thereby brought to a senseless activity that exhausts itself to the point of fainting. If the sensation of having willed the impossible and of not being able to do it drove people mad, how few human beings would be mentally healthy! The paroxysms of the raving man by no means spring from the striving to make the impossible possible, but conversely, the latter sometimes springs from the former, of which it is neither a characteristic mark, still less the exclusive cause. One main kind of diseases of the soul is forgotten, or what the author knows of it is jumbled together with mania in such a way that both are disfigured beyond recognition. We mean the one that used to be called folly, and that here, as senselessness, *delirium*, is supposed to form a component of mania. There is frequently a complete dissolution of ordered presenting without a fixed idea and without mania; conversely, the *mania sine delirio* [mania without delirium] is maintained by distinguished practitioners, is also admitted by the author, p. 156, and one can find in every larger lunatic asylum madmen who, at least outside the time of their paroxysms, are free of *delirium*. The undersigned saw one such unfortunate who, when after having once been cured he felt the return of the malady, harnessed his own carriage and drove himself to the asylum, several hours distant. A system that knows the soul to its very ground cannot, of course, pass over in silence the well-known disputes about the cause and seat of diseases of the soul; as supreme judge it rather summons them [p. I.22] before its forum, in order to send them away instructed and satisfied. After the two parties have been duly reproached with their one-sidedness, the sentence on p. 145 reads: “that spirit in itself cannot fall ill follows from its concept, which is to be free subjectivity, whose unfreedom is only ever a relative one. Absolute unfreedom would entail the impossibility of redemption.” And now, *Friedreich* and *Heinroth*, you stubborn men, be quiet! But they will probably shake their heads and ask which of them, then, ever asserted the possibility of an absolute unfreedom, or denied the possibility of a relative one? And then perhaps the one bethinks himself and says: after all, I have been proved right; for it was said that spirit in itself cannot fall ill. On the contrary, the other then replies, I am right; for it says on page 144: the illness of the soul is the self-diremption of self-feeling, the contradiction of intelligence, which are obviously properties not of the body but of the soul. Would the author kindly tell us which of the two sentences he has actually pronounced? It is a peculiar habit of his, appearing more than once in this book, first to step in between contending parties with the air of the most sovereign majesty, and immediately afterwards to withdraw with a most submissive bow to both sides. — *Michelet* instructs us on p. 204, under the heading: Origin of Insanity, that the illness of the soul could be produced by a disease or an external injury of the brain. Quite right; but how, then? He continues: “But it is chiefly the sublated continuity and harmony of the disposition which brings forth this state.” That may well be; but what is that sublation, and how does it arise, and how does this state arise from it? The undersigned is convinced that no one at present is able to answer these questions completely; but it is not such an admission that dishonors science, but the conceit which pretends to know everything where it knows nothing, and only meagerly ekes out its life on the leavings of other people’s meals. For of what physicians and empirical psychologists have collected so far we find all sorts of things here; but from the very own absolute knowledge of our authors not the slightest addition, no new experience, [p. I.23] not even a fleeting ingenious remark; indeed, the coarse material, in no way soluble in the vapor of thought, drags them down to triviality. On the healing of illnesses of the soul *Rosenkranz* on p. 156 knows nothing to say except that some are incurable, others curable; the latter because spirit in itself after all always remains the real possibility of reason. How the former, the incurability of some, is compatible with this does not trouble him, and so to triviality is joined, already, frivolity.

But now we should like to convene a congress of psychologists, of experienced and thinking ones, so far as this is possible without being Hegelians, and lay before them the following riddle: What sort of state in man is it whose most proper essence consists in being the unity of dream-life and of self-feeling? And if they then racked their brains in vain, we would open *Rosenkranz* for them at p. 157, where they would have to read, not without shame: *habit*! For, as indeed every good riddle appears very simple once solved, once we are accustomed to something, it is no longer foreign to us, we count it as part of our self; and thereby subjectivity and objectivity at the same time pass over into one another, as in dream-life. With the same scientific acuteness one can say: our chestnut tree is essentially the unity of a rosebush and a gallows; for it is a plant, and one can hang people on it. — *Erdmann* gives on p. 59 another definition: habit is the state in which the soul already has what it is supposed to transform into something inner in sensation, and in which, conversely, what was supposed to be embodied is already found in the body. According to this one would have to see always a color to which one has once become accustomed, and make incessantly the finger movements which one has once brought to facility in learning a piece for the clavier. Does experience teach this? — As the kinds of habit *Rosenkranz* designates *hardening* and *skill*. Where, pray, does he place those which occur not infrequently and cause careful governesses anxiety enough, e.g. always turning up one’s nose or baring one’s teeth; is this skill or hardening? And now the causes [p. I.24] and modes of origin of these states! On p. 158 we read that the frequency of repetition makes the sensation so identical with the self that the latter no longer distinguishes itself from it in its feeling. *Michelet* names practice as the mother of habit. That much is known in every nursery; but how habit could and must arise through repetition, that is what anyone who wanted to tell us more than the most commonplace had to investigate. *Michelet*, to be sure, makes further special efforts; he says on p. 211 that “the indwelling of the soul in the body” produces habits, and on p. 214 they are, through “natural magic,” a “thoroughly magical relation.” About such an explanation there is nothing more to be said. —

Whoever finds what has been adduced so far sufficient for understanding habits will be glad to learn, in passing, that this comprehends not merely all bodily ones, but also the mental ones, up to the highest, virtuous self-control. So great is the superficiality here that no one has even taken the trouble to sort out the empirically given material to some degree: the purely bodily habits from the purely mental ones; both from those which adhere to spirit and body at once; further, those arising through arbitrary will from those which form involuntarily, and the like. With one bold grasp they are all gathered together, and the magic word “repetition” is the sharp instrument with one cut of which they are harvested into the barns of knowledge. Now, apart from the fact that the connection between the ground and what is grounded remains quite invisible, one ought surely to remember that logic calls proving too much a fallacy. For the world of experience one has always proved too much, and hence nothing, when one derives different appearances entirely from the same conditions. And surely it is something different to be accustomed to heat and cold, or to insult and disgrace, to walk stooped out of habit, and to be a scoffer out of habit.

The last section of the first main part discusses *mimic*, *physiognomic* and *craniological* expression. Here, then, there would again be things about which many would very much like to know something proper. [p. I.25] From a few points it will become evident what has been accomplished here. *Lavater* and *Lichtenberg* disputed about physiognomy; now, on p. 171, *Lavater* is right, and *Lichtenberg* too. The latter’s assertion that in this field nothing can be asserted with certainty except that virtue beautifies the expression and vice makes it ugly is acknowledged with praise on p. 172, but immediately afterwards the physiognomic meanings of the individual parts of the face, forehead, eye, ear, nose, upper and lower lip, chin, are expounded one after another with great seriousness. On craniology it says on p. 175: physiognomy admits of dissimulation, “but the hard bone must tell the truth, man cannot alter its shape at will; the surest thing, therefore, is to keep to the bone, this irrefutable, unambiguous symbol,” and so on p. 176 the dependence of the formation of the skull on the form of the brain, and of the latter on mental activity, is diligently shown in the familiar manner, and “its right” is vindicated for phrenology, and “their undeniable significance” for “the protuberances on the cerebrum and cerebellum.” But according to p. 177 it is again “impossible to relate the individual elevations of the skull with determinacy to the individual activities of spirit;” indeed, on p. 178 “it remains impossible for all eternity.” With these pronouncements and decisions the different parties can again be satisfied, for each finds what it wishes; whether the unprejudiced man who seeks instruction can be so too, the readers may decide. We only recall what we said above about the author’s conduct toward contending parties.

Here ends the consideration of spirit in its unity with the body, or anthropology; there follows the *second part*, *phenomenology*, the *doctrine of consciousness*. In this part *Hegel* is credited with a “ravishing mastery”; it also contains such peculiar things that it seriously calls for examination. *Michelet* departs here in the arrangement, in that the doctrine of consciousness, though in agreement with *Rosenkranz* as to content, does not form a separate part in his work, but is woven into the presentation of theoretical and practical spirit [p. I.26] *Erdmann* arranges things exactly as *Rosenkranz* and *Hegel* do. In three sections consciousness, self-consciousness, and universal self-consciousness or reason are discussed.

Of consciousness we receive several definitions; the most definite tells us that it is the act of spirit’s distinguishing itself from everything that is not I. Consciousness, it says further, is at first *sensuous certainty*, a grasping of objects in their singleness, through the senses; the objects are in time and space; the senses can deceive, but they need not. Sensuous certainty advances and becomes *perceiving consciousness*, in that the constant marks of things, the universal, are sought. Finally it becomes *consciousness of the understanding*, which finds the laws and assumes forces. — Here familiar facts are stated; all that is missing is what really matters, description and explanation. Or must one not ask what actually goes on in the mind when one says: it distinguishes, apprehends, seeks and finds, and the like? Of uncovering the causes and laws of these states there is no thought at all. But while we have no hesitation in granting the facts named, as such as are found in most people, though not in all, we nevertheless protest very decidedly against the gross falsification of experience which is offered to us immediately afterwards. To the consciousness of the understanding, it says on pp. 198 ff., the universal of things shows itself as the ground of what is individual in them; this universal further shows itself as its own thoughts, and thereby it is sublated for objectivity and has become identical with its subjectivity. — To what consciousness of the understanding does this show itself so? To that of the Hegelians; of others we know nothing of the kind. Now it would perhaps be a meritorious work if someone wrote a psychology of *Hegelian* thinking, of *Hegelian* consciousness, as there are monographs on all sorts of diseases; but where the talk is of the universally human consciousness of the understanding, let nothing be foisted on us that is not also usually to be found in a man of understanding who is English, French, or a German non-Hegelian. Or are these in the habit of assuming that redness, this universal property [p. I.27] of centifolia roses, is a *ground* why a rose standing right in front of them has precisely forty petals? When a new law is discovered in a body, a new force for chemical effects, is the body in the habit of suddenly vanishing from their workbench and appearing in their mind as a new increment? To the authors such objections may seem very trivial; but we remark that all metaphysics has as its final aim to explain such trivialities, and that the most splendid theory, if it stands in contradiction with the constitution of the most wretched blade of grass, crumbles into nothing before it. This point is to be pressed as a striking example of the boldness with which facts that can be refuted by anyone are here invented and publicly proclaimed with undoubting confidence. Of glossings-over, to be sure, there is no lack.

We read further: when the consciousness of the understanding has come to the insight that the objective is the subjective, it knows that, in presenting the objects, it presents itself; consciousness has become self-consciousness. For the object of self-consciousness is the I, and this is the identity of subject and object. — Once again an enormous surreptitious substitution, which is all the more astonishing since it not only repeats the above falsification of experience, but also betrays a misunderstanding of a philosophical concept whose historical interpretation, at least at present, ought no longer to present any difficulty. Whoever has self-consciousness presents his I, hence an object; but which? The same one that he himself, the presenting subject, is; in this sense the I is the identity of subject and object. Now what does the author say? Whoever has self-consciousness presents houses, trees, animals, etc., he further presents to himself that these are himself, and the identity of these objects with the presenting subject is the I. — It is also false that self-consciousness first develops out of the consciousness of the understanding; rather, it is already actually present wherever this, or even only what was above called perceiving consciousness, has set in, for it arises at the same time as they do. It is not possible to present external objects as external, as objects, without a presentation of oneself, the subject, [p. I.28] to which they are external. — The author continues: but because the object, as not-I, is the whole of objectivity, self-consciousness sinks back down to consciousness, and there arises the contradiction of simple identity and manifold external objectivity. — We know well that people, overwhelmed by the force with which external things press in upon them, only too often forget themselves, so that their self-consciousness becomes mere consciousness of external things; but we are unable to discover a contradiction in this, any more than when someone, absorbed in reading, forgets to observe the weather. We also know of contradictions in the concept of the I, but quite different ones from the above, which is merely the troublesome consequence of the complete falsification of that concept that we have censured. — This contradiction, *Rosenkranz* says further, produces the drive to equalize objective and subjective, and from this there arises first of all *desire*. It is the drive to sublate objectivity. The object is the negation of the subject; enjoyment, as the satisfaction of desire, is the negation of this negation, hence the return of self-consciousness into itself. The subject, as living and self-conscious, has moreover the certainty that the object cannot prevent its negation. — This is a tangle of nonsense! Let the strong expression be pardoned until it is justified; it is in place in a weighty matter. The contradiction is contrived, it is nothing, and so it can produce nothing either. If the objects are, as was asserted, identical with the subject, they cannot contradict it; if they do the latter, as was likewise asserted, they can never become identical with it. But this error lies deeper; we shall have to speak of it again later. Enjoyment is no negation of the object. If a food, or the longed-for sight of a painting, is enjoyed, food and picture remain for the mind, after the enjoyment as before, something external; they become neither nothing nor a component of the soul. So experience says loudly enough, and only very confused thinking can contradict it. The universal, as opposed to what is sensible in the objects, is indeed, strictly speaking, a thought, and to that extent something subjective; but whoever asserts this must not raise sensation, too, [p. I.29] to a universal, as *Rosenkranz* incautiously does in his doctrine of perceiving consciousness and the consciousness of the understanding. More than that: sensation, too, is something purely subjective. But behind thought and sensations lies what is sensed, the sensible, like the “well-founded, enduring earth” beneath what walks upon it; and whoever, with *Hegel*, lumping this together with sensation, declares it to be the irrational and rejects it as a nullity, may easily transpose all objects into the subject; but no thinker of any note will follow him in this for long, and no practical man even for a moment. Pointing to this spot, *Schelling* needed only to pronounce his word “the positive” to see a host of seekers turn to him full of joyful hope; here *Herbart*, long before *Schelling*, began a struggle to which recognition will not always be denied. But what are we to say to the assertion that people regain their forfeited self-consciousness through the satisfaction of their desires, if not that, by the testimony of experience, they just as often lose it thereby? And what, finally, to the monstrous view that the subject, as living and self-conscious, has the certainty that no object can prevent its negation, i.e. that every desire of man must find its satisfaction? If this is true for the adherents of *Hegelian* philosophy, we do not know whether we should extol them as the most blessed of men or lament them as the most unblessed; for who can decide whether a man all of whose desires, wise and foolish, were satisfied would be the happiest or the most wretched? With regard to the rest of the poor children of men, however, this assertion needs no refutation. Holding fast to erroneous speculations in glaring opposition to experience can at times arouse respect, because the power of thought makes itself known thereby; but where the energy of thought does not come forth unceasingly and indubitably, where frivolity even leaves its unmistakable traces, there we turn away with indignation. — But how, then, do people come back to self-consciousness? No one, after all, can consume the whole world, nor would he probably wish to; while he [p. I.30] enjoys one thing, infinitely many others still remain unenjoyed. But with them there remains the contradiction between objectivity and subjectivity, the sublation of self-consciousness. *Rosenkranz* does not trouble himself about such trifles, but strides rapidly on toward a new main point.

Self-consciousness, he says, must become manifest to itself in its own concept; self-consciousness must confront it as its otherness, i.e. *in other human beings*. Why this? Presumably because *Hegel* said so; any other reason is neither given nor to be discerned. But the other self, we learn further on p. 210, must prove itself as a self, which it does not do through mere human shape, since it could, after all, also be an ape. And how will it have to furnish this proof and demonstrate its human nature? Guess it, whoever has the courage! Must it perhaps show its earlobe, which no ape has? No. Must it speak, since language is found exclusively in man? No. Must it act, practice works of love, magnanimity, gratitude, which surely is human above all else? Not that either. Must it prove its genius through masterpieces of poetry, of music, of sculpture? Nothing of all that; it must — *fight* to the death. I cannot, says *Michelet*, know the other as an identical self-consciousness so long as he remains a sensuous thing over against me. The sensuous husk hinders the contact of spirits and must be cleared away; self-consciousness seeking recognition therefore aims at the *death of the other*. — Let one be astonished, if, having come this far, one can still be astonished at anything. In English schools it happens that the newly arriving boy must first of all box, if he does not want, as the lowest, to take the place farthest from the fireplace; at German universities something similar was found at times: it has been called knavery and brutishness. According to our philosophers it is the only possible way of putting one’s human dignity, one’s innate likeness to God, into practice. Alexandrian physicians performed vivisections on human beings in order to track down life; according to our philosophers one must cut open the bodies of men in order to find spirit. *Rosenkranz* speaks here, [p. I.31] as almost everywhere, more soberly; according to him man must fight in order to show that his corporeality counts for him only as the instrument and sign of his self, roughly as nowadays one fights a duel in order to prove one’s honorableness. — The outcome of the struggle can, as we further learn, be death; but this “dry negation” does not satisfy spirit. But the one may also shrink from the struggle; then he becomes the bondsman, and the other is his lord. The bondsman must work; thereby he acquires culture, and through it he comes to the grasping of his self, to self-consciousness. The consequence is his voluntary emancipation by the lord, or revolt; from both there results the mutual recognition of self-consciousness, in which individual difference is sublated and the community of knowing oneself as identical has taken its place. — But had the slave, we ask, lost self-consciousness, then? Self-consciousness, we were taught, is the act of distinguishing oneself from everything that is not I. The weak man, the peace-loving man, whom brute force made into slaves, have they become newborn children who know neither themselves nor others? And if, as we have heard, enjoyment restores self-consciousness, does the slave not at least enjoy his daily food, which keeps his wretched life going? Then he could, after all, eat and drink his self-consciousness back again, and would not need the toil of culture! This would surely be consistently argued. But our author has unawares fallen into a small confusion of concepts; in the place of the bare self-consciousness from which he started out, and to which in itself neither higher culture, much less any dignity as yet, adheres, the *Kantian* concept of the consciousness of innate human dignity has crept in. This can be lacking or be lost in a state of extreme bondage, but this too *need* not be; it is acquired through increasing culture and can win the recognition of the master. Or does the author seriously believe that slaves have no self-consciousness in the proper sense? Does he really believe that North American or other slaveholders take their slaves for apes, for non-humans, for beings without self-consciousness? [p. I.32] Then they would probably not punish the teaching of them. Can he cite from the whole domain of history a single example of a slavery in his sense? Not even poetry offers one; for even Shakespeare’s Caliban is too good for it, and his master no less. But the human dignity of the slaves, too, just as according to the testimony of history it is very often felt by the slave, need not always go unrecognized by the master, even though self-interest lets the better insight exert no influence on his actions; and just as little does the slave’s revolt always have as its consequence that the master recognizes his personal dignity. Further, if acquired culture can procure for the slave the recognition of the master, it can do so before any struggle as well, and the struggle’s necessity, which already proved earlier to be arbitrarily asserted, falls away for this reason too. This account is therefore senseless if the concept of self-consciousness is held fast; while the substitution of the other concept adds confusion to the senseless. — It is to be regarded as a concession made to experience that the struggle for life and death is not demanded for the present as well, as a necessary point of transition to mutual recognition, but that it is said: the struggle gradually transforms itself into a spiritual dialectic, and with us the state guarantees the independence of each individual from the outset. Let it be remembered, however, that what is under discussion here are developments of spirit which one and all proceed with inner, absolute necessity; the development of the mutual recognition of self-consciousness through struggle is supposed to be one of them. Now on the one hand this guarantee by the state would be a new piece of evidence against the asserted necessity of the struggle, which, if later it could be made superfluous by the state, could earlier be made so by a great many other things that were indicated above; on the other hand the state can indeed guarantee actions, but not the underlying state of the soul. How, pray, would the author like the undertaking if a state wished to guarantee its subjects the life of dreams, clairvoyance, memory, the understanding? —

[p. I.33] Some remarkable applications of the *Hegelian* concepts of right we cannot pass over in silence. According to *Rosenkranz*, p. 217, usurpers must prove their *entitlement* to the independence they have arrogated to themselves through *struggle*; likewise slaves, when they emancipate themselves by force. According to *Michelet*, p. 480, “the coercion which slavery exercises is a right against the first, still more barbaric violence of manslaughter, and this right is also the right of heroes to found states.” If, then, we say, someone, in order to behold his self in the other, has a mind to strike him dead, but, since the other cannot defend himself, merely makes him a slave, then he has a right, and is a legitimate master. According to p. 527, “the historical justification of slavery is not excluded, in that a people which has not yet attained the concept of freedom, and so still stands at the stage of barbarism, may admittedly also be held temporarily still unworthy of freedom. In this situation are, in particular, the Negroes.” — However much the school may strive to veil its outrageous principle: might is right, it cannot prevent its becoming visible here and there in all its hideousness. And it is this doctrine of self-consciousness, now, in which a “ravishing mastery” is ascribed to *Hegel*. What is true in the whole matter? That legally ordered social relations develop slowly, mostly out of confusion, strife and acts of violence. But the subject is not a purely psychological one; it belongs to the philosophy of history, which besides the psychological element has a great many other elements to consider, if it is to achieve true insight and not merely a couple of good notions. Were psychology to discuss this subject, it would, in order to be systematic, also have to take up all the other states of the soul which occur or tend to occur after self-consciousness, the distinguishing of oneself from everything that is not I, has developed. It would have to show why these boys choose these games, those boys others; how some become hunters, others nomads, artisans, artists; how they become husbands or confirmed bachelors; how migrations of peoples and colonies arise, etc. False [p. I.34] it is that what is at issue in that condition is self-consciousness; that this is lost and regained in the manner indicated. An important, widespread historical fact has been lifted out of its place and set in another, where it does not belong, in order to give it a false luster; and there it has been worked up in glaring contradictions with history and with the content of the concepts put forward.

This part is concluded by the doctrine of *rational self-consciousness*. The first section had developed the consciousness of the understanding, which recognizes the universal as what is proper to objects, and therefore recognizes these as something subjective. In the second, the origin of universal self-consciousness, of the mutual recognition of self-conscious beings, was shown. “The one subject knows itself, through the struggle for recognition, as identical with the other self.” With this, now, the third section teaches, the opposition of subjectivity and objectivity has vanished; we have attained the identity of universal subjectivity and objectivity, and this constitutes rational self-consciousness. “What is tremendous in this standpoint is that the subject says to the world: thou art mine!” We offer our congratulations on this result! Only may the man on the tremendous standpoint, when he is hungry tomorrow, also have something to eat, or else he might easily take a tremendous fall from his height. One misgiving we permit ourselves to express: How are we to think the identity of the true selves? Are they all One self? Against this we shall protest just as we did earlier against the subjectivity of external things; moreover, *Rosenkranz* himself, p. 219, declares this identity to be, in the first instance, only a community. But if this is the case, then each self is nevertheless something external for every other, hence an object over against the subject; the identity has vanished again, and we have shared Penelope’s labor. — In rational self-consciousness, it is said further, there is nothing which it could not also find outside itself as reality, and nothing is outside it “that could contradict it.” — Why this modesty? Let the author just say it outright: “Nothing is outside this self-consciousness which is not also in it! [p. I.35] That is how it must run, if objectivity and subjectivity are identical, and consistency is no boasting. Let him reveal to us, then, how matters really stand with terrestrial magnetism, what progressive motion our sun has, what lands there are at the South Pole, and whatever else torments the inquisitive; he will lack neither rational self-consciousness, nor we gratitude. — This consciousness now suddenly also knows all the *Hegelian* categories and applies them with skill. How it comes to this, we are not told.

The subjects of the first part compelled the authors still to consult experience; what they spoke of could often be pointed out in reality. With the doctrine of self-consciousness they have risen into the pure height of empty thought; there they speak like dreamers, and their talk floats about like fog.

*The third* part of the work of *Rosenkranz* bears the name “*Pneumatology*,” the doctrine of spirit. Spirit distinguishes itself into *theoretical* and *practical* spirit, and thereby two sections arise. In *Michelet* these form the second and third main parts, into which, as was already said above, what in *Rosenkranz* and *Erdmann* makes up the second main part, the doctrine of consciousness, is interwoven. Three chapters exhaust the doctrine of theoretical spirit; they treat of *intuiting*, *presenting* and *thinking*. We must consider them more closely.

The exposition of intuiting begins from *feeling*. One would hardly have expected this here again, since it was already discussed above under the sensations. There we saw at the same time how lacking it was in any intelligible statement of its nature, and still more in any attempt at a scientific explanation of it from its conditions. There, p. 94, feeling was called inner sensation when it relates to the universality of the mental; here, p. 242, it is the immediate unity of soulfulness and consciousness; or, according to p. 244, also spirit in the implicitly rational totality of its existence; or [p. I.36] also the whole of spirit as its own material; or again the dull weaving of spirit within itself; or, according to *Michelet*, p. 262, the material-like weaving of spirit within itself, insofar as it presents itself under the form of time, a content in dull unconsciousness, etc. *Rosenkranz*, p. 243, does indeed want the feeling of which he speaks here to be distinguished from the one considered earlier, where the transition from the outer into the inner, and vice versa, had supplied the principal moment; but if one looks at the examples adduced, here love for a woman, there patriotic feeling and the like, one does not find how that distinction is maintained by the author, or could be maintained. *Michelet* has actually given it up, and in this has improved upon his predecessor. What is certain is that through this new discussion of the feelings we gain not the least insight into their nature. — To feeling there is added, according to p. 245, *attention*. This is surely one of the most important psychological subjects. We demand it of the child and of the adult; its direction is followed by thought, feeling, will, deed, the whole man; the educator and the statesman must know for which things they may expect it, for which they can demand it, how it is to be stimulated, how high it may be strained so that it does not end in feebleness and disgust. Now what is it? It is, we hear on p. 245, that act of intelligence whereby the latter gives itself the direction toward itself in its feeling. But intelligence is no astronomer pointing his telescope; the mythological personification of the faculties of the soul has been rejected by our author, and very rightly so; what sense, then, does that giving of a direction have, i.e. what goes on in the mind when, as we say, it gives itself a direction toward something?
