Further, when a mathematician attentively considers an equation in order to find its roots, is it his feeling, then, toward which his intelligence is directed? Let us hear further how attention comes about. “I must,” it says on p. 246, “continually give myself a push within myself in order to keep myself in this direction of my intelligence.” Before such wisdom criticism falls silent. — The distinguishing, mediated by attention, of the content originally [p. I.37] existing in feeling is further called *intuiting*. What kind of mental act distinguishing is remains, here as before, unsaid. Naturally one recalls that what one ordinarily intuits is not so much one’s feeling as paintings, houses, landscapes, in short external things; one therefore asks how the above is meant. The answer we read on p. 248: “In directing myself upon myself, I grasp my feeling and throw it out into space and time.” If we ask further, in astonishment, how this throw is possible, we hear: “through the infinite elasticity of our self.” The writer of these lines confesses that he blushed with shame when he read this. For he does after all count himself among the philosophical public, before which one may venture to babble like a child, while close beside it the natural sciences deliver their well-ordered discourses with convincing earnestness. And so the child is often enough stood in the corner by the men, too, and, as circumstances require, also gets the rod now and then. — The whole view of intuiting is incorrect insofar as intuiting is derived from feeling. As an example it is adduced that whoever hears a song first feels it and then, through attention, though perhaps in a very short time, hears key, measure and melody out of the feeling. But put, instead of the song, the letters of a word, the digits of a composite number: where then is the feeling? Intuiting does indeed presuppose a material, and this can, to be sure, but by no means must, be accompanied by a feeling. — *Michelet* and *Erdmann* too have the content of feeling thrown out, “projected,” by intelligence. But they also present the matter in yet another way. Intelligence, they say, receives a multitude of sensations different from one another; these are external to it, and therefore they are so to one another as well, i.e. they stand in a spatial relation among themselves. The common experience that tones do not form triangles, ellipses and the like, as colors do, refutes this theory sufficiently, however. But with what right does *Erdmann*, in the Remark to § 96, reproach *Kant* with having taken the forms of intuition as subjective, when he does the same in the Remark to this §? For there he says that external things receive the form of spatiality only [p. I.38] thus, and by virtue of the fact, that and how intelligence determines them. He evidently does not think it necessary, in a philosophical work, to hold fast to a thought from a § to the Remark appended to it.

The second chapter treats of *presenting*, which consists in spirit’s positing the content of feeling within itself, in the infinity of its ideal space. It presents itself at three stages, as *recollection*, *imagination* and *memory*.

*Recollection* is a making-inward of intuition. Through inwardizing, condensing of the content of feeling, in which a multitude of detailed determinations are effaced, the image takes shape out of the intuition; it is the extract of the intuition. But it passes away, because it is something individual, yet it is preserved in the depths of intelligence, where it has determinate being, though not existence. It is then reproduced through the renewed intuition, compared with it, distinguished, supplemented and corrected; it becomes mobile, a new content over which free subjectivity rules with unconditional sovereignty; and in this distinguishability, mobility and freedom the image is: *presentation*; an image which, having become wholly free of the process of intuiting, has been made inward. Here nothing less than everything is lacking that belongs to the scientific treatment of these states; only the facts are put together crudely and in part quite untruly. No man possesses an unconditional sovereignty over his acquired presentations; on the contrary, everyone is at times deserted by recollection, to his annoyance. Why do the detailed determinations of intuitions vanish? *Michelet* says, p. 285: when the universal presentations have arisen, the individual images vanish more and more from consciousness, because they have become useless. But how often do we retain the most useless things indelibly in recollection without our will, while the most useful vanish despite all effort. According to *Rosenkranz* the images pass away because they are individual. How does the former follow from the latter? What does it mean that the images that have vanished from consciousness have, in the depths of intelligence, determinate being indeed, but not existence? [p. I.39] What do the words mean: inwardizing, condensing, mobility of presentations and the like?

To recollection is joined *imagination*. It is first reproductive, in that it calls forth presentations without the stimulus of intuitions, through the free power of intelligence. It is able to conjure them out of itself at will. Further, it is productive phantasy, which creates new images or lays a peculiar content into those already acquired. Finally, as semiotic phantasy, it creates signs. With this a sluice is opened, and over thirty pages flows a broad stream which amply sets before us, on phantasy, iconism, language and writing, what has long been known. —

This section is highly characteristic of the system. In the first place it becomes perfectly clear, as had already appeared earlier to the attentive reader and is confirmed by everything that follows, that the “absolute science,” the “newest philosophy,” which fancies itself so far exalted above its predecessors, has nothing better to give us than *Kantian* or rather *Wolffian* psychology. For the latter is characterized by its putting together groups of states of the soul and giving them a name and a nominal definition, without investigating more precisely what goes on in the soul, and on what occasions. Thus our authors, as though the preceding exertions had tired them, now bring hosts of familiar names and nominal definitions: recollection, reproductive, productive and semiotic imagination, symbolizing, allegorizing and poetizing phantasy, a faculty of presentation and of designation, the various kinds of memory, understanding, judgment and reason, and similar words that are current like old small coins, without anyone much troubling about their stamp and their content. Secondly, however, we see that we receive far less still than *Wolff*ian psychology, namely a word-hodgepodge so thoughtless as *Wolff’s* plain understanding never made itself guilty of. This becomes clear from the following. In connection with reproductive imagination, *Rosenkranz* comes to speak of the laws of the reproduction of presentations. He first says [p. I.40] on p. 260: that presentations tend to enter into a continuity with one another, by virtue of which they are reproduced in connection; and indeed this connection is coexistence in space, succession in time, similarity, contrast, the relation of the whole to its parts and of the cause to its effect. He declares it true, p. 262, that one presentation “as it were” awakens others. But *laws*, so it says on p. 263, laws association does not have; moreover, those enumerated would be incomplete, since all the categories could lay claim to the dignity of being such laws. About the completeness of the enumeration given we will not quarrel; in the most ordinary works on psychology it is at least usually better ordered: but why are there no laws of association? Because “law exists only where the manifold yet presents only the One, to which multiplicity and contingency must submit,” whereas “association already in the word points to a free, incalculable grouping”; or because, as *Michelet* says, p. 291, “the connection is a different one in each individual.” Here the reader may call out to himself with the old hero: Endure, my heart, harder things still hast thou endured! Since the wind has blown over the earth it has whirled together many a snowstorm, and massed, chased and scattered many a bank of cloud. But no one will find it probable that any two such processes, two snowstorms or two drifts of cloud, have ever been perfectly alike, alike down to the number of flakes and vapor droplets, down to all the motions of each such particle by itself and together with all the others. And now let our philosophers make the physicists believe that in those processes not every least change is subject to strict laws, because they are different each time, because the word “storm” points to an incalculable, hence free, grouping, because law exists only where the manifold yet presents only the One; if they cannot do this, let them strike out their above propositions with the blackest printer’s ink, and spare their science contempt and scorn! But how do they conceive the origin of reproductions? According to *Rosenkranz*, p. 264, they are produced by “free subjectivity, [p. I.41] which is the power over all its presentations.” This means nothing other than that presentations reproduce themselves through their own power, without any cause at all; for we heard above that subjectivity is the summary unity, or, as it was later called by a surreptitious substitution, the identical unity of all presentations. That, then, is the heart of the matter. Hitherto we knew, in psychology and ethics, a much-disputed transcendental freedom of the will; now we also have a transcendental freedom of all reproductions; and this at least is an entirely new thought, without precedent in the history of our science. When men who flatly call millions of facts occurring daily within them and around them causeless and lawless are just as flatly declared, by those who work in the so-called exact sciences, incapable of any scientific investigation of reality, we must confess that at this point it is no easy thing to contradict. For that assumption, if it does not shatter every possible circle of thought, at any rate does so to the actual one of the whole educated world; and if it should ever be made, this could only be done on the strength of the most pressing, irrefutable arguments, and not on the strength of frivolous remarks which the commonest experience refutes.

But we have not yet uncovered all the bad things that here press unwelcome upon the reader; contradictions of the authors with themselves remain to be mentioned. The reproductions are lawless according to *Rosenkranz*, and yet he himself asserts that continuities of them are formed. Even granted that they arose, as he thinks, according to all the categories, how do they square with that lawlessness? How does the experience, conceded by him and to be had every second, that one presentation awakens others, square with it? According to *Michelet*, p. 286, arbitrariness reigns in reproductions, but it is not an infinite one. If, however, it is finite, hence limited, then something limiting must be present. This will act according to the peculiarity of its nature, and so we have a law. This is the reason why every cause acts according to laws, and lawlessness is necessarily synonymous with causelessness. But *Michelet* does still more. On p. 282 he says that [p. I.42] reproduction takes place through forces of attraction which like or similar perceptions exert upon one another. How can the two thoughts of lawlessness and of the action of attractive forces be united? Hardly otherwise than by someone who is simply not thinking writing them down together in a book.

How, further, does our authors’ immediately following doctrine of memory square with their free arbitrary will of reproduction? If I am to be able to learn a language, to make myself understood to others through language, then I must be able to count on it that I myself and others continually connect the same thing in the mind with the same name. Is this lawlessness of reproduction? — And so we believe we have justified our charge of a thoughtless jumble of words.

*Memory*, the last and highest form of presenting, our authors declare to be the grasping of things in names. They thus restrict it to the retaining of names and their connection with the objects designated by them. This runs counter to all usage, the common as well as the scientific, and since it happens without justification, it appears as nothing more than a caprice. Unfortunately we miss in it the charm which otherwise makes such things bearable, at least in women. Nor is it to be seen why the connecting of names with things should form an entirely peculiar mental state, a higher one than the reproductions discussed earlier. We do indeed connect things with names, but just as much outlines with colors, music with dances, poems with melodies in our mind, and, psychologically considered, all these processes are alike. Where, then, is memory’s claim to preference? Our authors find it in the following: W. v. Humboldt and other students of language have made the observation that a close kinship frequently obtains between words and the objects named by them. The absolute philosophy likes to make use of views which cause something of a stir, and since it always knows somewhat more than other people, it asserts that names are [p. I.43] essential to things, that they and the presentations of objects designated by them are one. “The presentation is in spirit only as name, and the name only as presentation.” Against this one might now object that memory sometimes also retains wholly senseless words, through which nothing is presented. “But in the case of an abracadabra,” we read in *Rosenkranz*, p. 303, “intelligence alone is the power of holding together.” Here Wolffianism stands once more in fine bloom. Memory and intelligence are two workers who have introduced a division of labor between themselves. If the word “house” comes along, memory receives it and assigns it its presentation; if the word “abracadabra” comes along, memory says: You are no concern of mine, go to intelligence! and sends it away. This is called: making states of the soul comprehensible. Further, one might object against that essential unity that different languages name the same object with different names. Our philosophers feel the contradiction that arises here, but they find it easy to resolve it. *Rosenkranz* says, pp. 303 ff.: The contradiction that names are essential to things and also indifferent to them, the latter of which is attested by the plurality of languages, resolves itself in that spirit gives words their meaning, and thus transforms them, as something external to it, objective, through the power of its subjectivity. In *Erdmann*, p. 107, we read: the contradiction that the word is the thing as it is present in the realm of presentation, and yet at the same time is only contingently connected with the presentation, resolves itself through intelligence’s impressing the words upon itself and noting them; and in *Michelet*, p. 360: The foreignness of spirit and sign is absolutely sublated by mechanical memorizing. How do our readers like this resolution? It is just as good as if one were to say: It is indeed a contradiction that black is white and also is not; but it resolves itself if one only impresses black properly upon oneself. We believe, rather, that it will thereby only come to consciousness all the more strongly, and we are convinced that all people whose thinking has not been stunted by a false philosophical system will forever take the presentation of the sound which the written word [p. I.44] “tree” designates, and the presentation of the genus of plants designated by it, for different things.

*Thinking* is the last form of theoretical spirit because, as *Rosenkranz* teaches, it is also the first, and cannot resolve itself into a still earlier one. Are the concepts of empirical consciousness, pray, to be found in this speculation, i.e. does experience teach that thinking is the first form of theoretical spirit? But let us see in what the essence of thinking consists. In that spirit gives words a meaning, it transforms them, as something external to it, objective, through the power of its subjectivity. This vanishing of the opposition of objectivity and subjectivity is thinking. — How so? Thinking would thus be nothing but a fixing of meanings for words; and objectivity, which earlier was the sum total of the entire external world, would now be only the sum total of our words? So it is. For words are one with presentations; presentations, however, are the things, according to what was said earlier about the unity of the subjective and the objective; therefore words are the things. Hence we hear further: thinking is the negation of presenting; it no longer presents the thing, but is the thing. And *Michelet* teaches, p. 362: In memory the presentation has stripped off its merely subjective existence; in the sign it is objective being; for the sign is the thing itself, and spirit, which is just as much the thing, as the thing, which is just as much spirit, is thinking. We have already stated our opinion of this view, and can here only wonder a little at the spirit which, having already arrived in rational consciousness at the unity of subjectivity and objectivity, gets no further through the whole subsequent series of developments than to find words, now, for this unity as well. — Thinking is, according to *Rosenkranz*, 1) a having of thoughts, e.g. in proverbs; 2) it is intelligence itself, e.g. in the thought of end and means, which, like those of cause and effect, nature, custom, etc., is strictly simple; 3) it is autonomous; — according to what logic, one wonders, can this trilogy have been set up? — It has laws which are not [p. I.45] arbitrary but identical with our self. These are the categories, e.g. essence, ground, universal, particular and individual, etc., of which, on account of their simplicity, it cannot be stated how they differ from one another. The system of the categories is the logical Idea. Because the moments of the subjective concept are the concept, the judgment and the syllogism, subjective spirit is also concept-forming, judging and syllogizing. So *Rosenkranz*. *Michelet* teaches: Thinking turns away entirely from sensuous individuality, it takes it into no consideration at all, it draws out of things only its own determinations, the intelligible universal, which it has lent them and now demands back. It is 1) *understanding*, the having of the categories, of the necessary unities of the understanding, under which the material of cognition is subsumed; further their application to that material in discovering, classifying, describing, experimenting; finally the law of induction and of experience. 2) *Judgment*, the application of the laws and principles gained through the understanding. 3) *Reason*. This no longer goes through abstraction to the universal, but, proceeding from the interior of the subject, it descends from the universal to the particular. Its products are the Ideas. The Idea is most of all the thing itself, the most objective of all in things, with the stripping away of everything that belongs merely to matter. For cognition reason makes use of the Ideas, only of the Ideas, and so penetrates to the presuppositionless principle of the All, which is just as much the very essence of the soul as of things. According to *Erdmann*, there belong to thinking as moments theoretical reason, which perceives the laws of the objective, practical reason, which gives them, and comprehending reason, as the unity of both, or absolute knowing. Through it objectivity is sublated and has become spirit’s own determinateness. — One sees that the main thoughts of our authors are alike; about the deviations in detail, not insignificant to them, they may settle matters among themselves; for us they are of too subordinate an interest. On the whole we see the weariness in bringing forth new formations, already remarked above, continuing; the expositions for the most part present a sight like [p. I.46] those rooms in houses where worn-out pieces of furniture from several generations back are stored together. Classifications and schematizations of every kind; of scientific insight into the nature of psychical appearances — nothing.

The doctrine of *practical* spirit, which now follows, concludes in *Rosenkranz* and *Erdmann*, as the second section of the doctrine of spirit, the third main part, which in *Michelet* it forms exclusively. Theoretical spirit, in having come as far as thinking, has thereby become *free*. The freedom of intelligence, *Rosenkranz* teaches us, consists in this, that its being determined by the content thought is just as much its determining, i.e., if we look back to what came earlier: because intelligence thinks in the categories, but these are not arbitrary and yet are identical with the self, in thinking it is at once the determining and the determined. Thinking, it says on p. 312, is the free self-determination of the subject. So besides the transcendental freedom of the reproductions we now also have a transcendental freedom of thinking. Thinking, we read further, is also already willing; which was expressed earlier thus, that thinking is impossible without willing, and conversely. Stop! we cry, thinking is not willing, as everyone knows who knows German; and that the two assertions: thinking is willing, and: thinking is not possible without willing, are only different expressions of the same thought, that is an untruth which we will not let ourselves be saddled with so easily either. Let the author see how he arrives at a willing; by this road it will not do. Earlier we read: By virtue of the freedom of my intelligence I can determine myself to a content which is just as much mine as it is in being, i.e. I can will. We understand what the author is after. Willing, by the testimony of experience, is usually directed at changes in the external world, and this mark he would gladly see in his concept. We grant him that intelligence, as thinking, determines itself to a content which is its own, i.e. that the thought which it thinks is its thought; but that this content should thereby also already be in being, i.e. an actual, external object — from that may [p. I.47] God preserve us. His beautiful world could not endure an hour longer. The concept of willing, then, which the author gives us, we can by no means acknowledge as corresponding to empirical consciousness. Let us see what *Michelet* offers us. Theoretical spirit, he says on p. 75, knows itself in the end as all actuality, as the substance of things. That we have already heard and not believed. These things, he continues, are the false, that which is to be sublated, in whose place spirit has to put itself; thus it is practical spirit or will. But, we must remark, this false thing, this thing to be sublated, has after all already been sublated by thinking; why do you plague spirit any further? And now it is even to put itself in the place of the objects, it is to leave its body and become a stone, a plant! Spirit, as theoretical, has consumed objectivity; but it seems it cannot digest it, and so, as practical, it brings it up again. In such talk, surely, nothing whatever of the concept of willing is to be found. Let us go on to *Erdmann*, who gives us the following instruction on p. 124. In the sublation of objectivity by thinking two things are implied: “1) that spirit, which knows objectivity as its own determinateness, must know it as a contradiction not to exist objectively as well.” On the contrary, we say, that was precisely the contradiction earlier, that there was an objectivity which was not subjectivity; to escape this, the subject took the objects into itself by thinking. If it now even wants to become an object itself, it will only tear open again the wound just healed, and wantonly prepare for itself an unbearable existence. The author continues: “2) it is implied in the above that objectivity, as its own, can no longer offer any resistance to spirit, but must let it into itself. Thus it has now become the capacity to transform itself independently into an objectivity; it is practical spirit or will.” So then, we say, spirit has become a Proteus who transforms himself endlessly. Only he is at the same time omnipotent, for he can do everything he wills; nothing can resist him; indeed, everything he wills is already actual in his willing it, and simply through his willing it. The Hegelian philosophy has not seldom [p. I.48] shown a desire to enter public life; but what, pray, would it judge of a statesman who undertook to govern a country with its convictions, those just expressed? Ordinarily it is customary to regard it as a necessary requirement of wisdom that a man know exactly what he can do and what not, and that he beware of willing what he cannot do. — The concept of willing is, to be sure, not without difficulties; in order to grasp it properly one must understand a little psychology. But never has a thinker so distorted it into a monster and robbed it of all intelligibility and truth as has happened here. — Naturally willing too is free, transcendentally free, and this is the third freedom of this kind. Will spirit, with so many freedoms, still be in a position to have a coherent thought, a consistent course of action? We very much fear that under such circumstances it is all over with its understanding as with its character.

In three divisions the stages of development of practical spirit are now presented. *Rosenkranz* treats in the first chapter *practical feeling*. We thus come to speak of feeling for the third time. Theoretical feeling, it says on p. 316, must particularize itself, i.e. become individual, determinate feelings; through this the subject senses a lack which is tormenting and which urges spirit to sublate its limitedness through their positive negation. This is the stimulus, practical feeling in its universality. — Again empty nominal definitions, for we do not know what sort of states the feeling of a lack, the being-urged of spirit, really are. — *Michelet* says, pp. 244 ff.: practical feeling is a judgment; every sensation becomes practical feeling when I relate it to the ought in me, to the non-realization of the sensuous will in an adequate manner. According to *Erdmann*, § 129, practical feeling is not a judgment, but the mere feeling of the ought. With that we have evidently gained nothing. The *stimulus*, *Rosenkranz* teaches further, particularizes itself in turn into *drive* and its various forms. Drive is the nature of the living subject striving toward self-preservation. Satisfaction and [p. I.49] non-satisfaction of the stimulus yield pleasure and displeasure. — We doubt that these feelings arise only in consequence of a stimulus, of a felt lack; it might, for instance, be hard to discover in the pleasure which a lovely melody, unexpectedly heard, awakens in me. *Michelet*, p. 450, declares pleasure to be the final peak of an activity of the soul which, through certain changes undertaken, has sublated the state of disharmony and produced harmony. If, however, pleasure is a peak, then we want to know what shape this peak has and of what material it consists. Pleasure also not seldom passes suddenly into disgust, where, then, the final peak receives a further addition, which need not itself be the final one. — Our literature has dozens of so-called empirical psychologies which give just as good, very often better, explanations of these subjects. In general, the writings examined here stand in a twofold relation to those older ones. On the one hand they live off them, in that they credulously take up and adopt their schematizations and nominal definitions; on the other hand they proceed against them with a disregard which one is otherwise accustomed to show only toward the most senseless views. Whether pleasure can arise only from displeasure, both only in consequence of a preceding desire — these are old disputed questions gladly treated by earlier writers. Our authors deliver their opinions on them with such careless superficiality, as if a dispute had neither ever taken place nor could take place among reasonable people. If, moreover, we have not known hitherto what feeling is, our concept becomes still more confused by stimulus and drives now also being listed for us as forms of practical feeling.

The second main form of practical spirit, according to *Rosenkranz*, is *disposition*. The animal stops at the opposition of pleasure and displeasure; man, through his consciousness, makes his feeling an object to himself. The identity of feeling and self-consciousness is disposition. Already earlier, when feeling was under discussion, the author claimed a certain “looseness of designation” for it; it seems he wants it to be granted him for disposition and other names as well. [p. I.50] At least it is not to be seen how this name comes to that definition in particular, and conversely. At the same time we see that we need not always take the much-used word identity strictly; for identity of consciousness and feeling is here the consciousness of a feeling. — Disposition, we read further on p. 322, is *desire*, in that the self-conscious subject, whose practical feeling is excited by an object, seeks to attain the enjoyment of it through its annihilation. — Again we meet an old acquaintance, of whom we have heard many strange things, though not who he is. Here we learn that desire is a form of disposition, or of feeling bound up with self-consciousness. This, however, is just as much contrary to usage as it is unintelligible to us, who still do not know what feeling is. But that we are already used to; one question, on the other hand, we must put here. Desire seeks, we were told earlier and are told again just now, satisfaction through the annihilation of the object; according to p. 324 it seeks to sublate the being-asunderness of subject and object, and to make the object, as one enjoyed, i.e. posited negatively, into the predicate of the subject. How does this square with desire appearing here as a form of practical spirit, which has not to consume the object, to transform it into subjectivity, but, conversely, to objectify subjectivity, to transform it into the objects? It is true that it is also said in an earlier place: desire is the will determined by a presentation. Transformed into a presentation, the object has become imperishable; the will, then, can no longer, like drive, aim at its complete annihilation, but wants only to transform the substantial into something accidental; it wants to have the object. But the transformation of the substance of the object into an accident of the subject surely seems the same as the subjectivizing of objectivity, all the more so as the author will hardly bring himself to declare what he calls objective spirit to be an accident of subjective spirit. How, further, it comes about that objects become imperishable through transformation into presentations — that many a one would surely like to know; with this art one could [p. I.51] earn handsome prizes. What, after all, can the author be thinking when he says that enjoying drive annihilates its object, enjoying desire does not? Were he speaking merely of the drive for nourishment, it would be false, but one could at least think something by it. It is also a curiosity that he denies desires to animals. It seems that the *Hegelian* school, as it has so much else of its own, has also an animal world of its own; the common animals at least, which we other people observe, give utterances from which we infer the existence of desires in them with roughly the same certainty as in our fellow men. —

Secondly, disposition has the form of *inclination*. This has as its constitutive principle propensity, a particularization of drive, and indeed an abiding tendency of it; it is a concrete determinateness of propensity. In distinction from desire, which seeks to realize its supremacy over the object, inclination strives to preserve the existence of the object, and enjoys without the destructive restlessness of desire. In distinction from propensity it is changing, while propensity remains. And so the author really holds, p. 328, that the propensity for wine-drinking may well pass from an inclination for French wines into one for Rhine wine, but that the propensity for wine-drinking as such persists, according to the irrefutable dialectic of concepts. Experience refutes this irrefutability. How, moreover, can inclination be distinguished from desire by seeking to preserve its object, when just before the very same striving — to preserve, only to have, and not to destroy — was predicated of desire? How, further, can inclination enjoy, when enjoyment is supposed to be the sublation of the object, and it preserves its object?

The *third* form of disposition is *passion*. In it, says *Rosenkranz*, p. 323, the subject wants to enjoy the content of its feeling as desire and inclination at once; it makes itself the predicate of the object and wants at the same time that the object become its predicate; it gives itself up to the object and recovers itself back out of it. What precious expressions for so plain a subject! On p. 331 it says that passion is the [p. I.52] summit of the practical subject’s deepening into itself. Formerly it was thought that passion deepens itself far more into its object than into the subject. Its essence is further stated to be that it is not an intensified inclination, but an inclination combined with affect. Of *affect*, however, it is said that it is a feeling, a sensation, in which the subject as subject loses itself; that it is transient, but, when combined with an inclination, becomes persistent. We reply: a persistent affect in the proper sense of these words does not exist; no human being could endure it. Nor does affect make inclination into passion, at least according to what is understood by these words in the German language. A man of business may be accustomed to play a game of whist every evening for recreation, and to flare up easily while doing so; one may then ascribe to him an inclination to this kind of recreation and affects connected with it, but by no means a passion. — *Michelet* has a quite peculiar notion of the origin of the passions. On p. 487 he says: knowing is the highest, noblest drive of the human spirit; in it the subject has given up its individual being-for-itself against the object and has become adequate to the universality of the object. — One may, in passing, be permitted to be a little surprised that knowing is supposed to be a drive, as also that in it — since it is, after all, especially on the *Hegelian* view, so closely related to thinking — the subject gives up its being-for-itself against the object and becomes adequate to the latter, whereas in thinking an opposite process takes place, objectivity being transformed into subjectivity. But *Michelet* continues: “the determinate content of the object is itself expanded into universality, namely through knowing, but has thereby taken possession of the entire activity of the subject, and fettered it to itself. Thus passion comes to be. Now the subject is no longer ruled by the universal, but by a determinate content; it becomes a slave.” Herewith the essence, the dialectical necessity, and of course also the only possible manner of the origin of passion has been shown! — The states discussed so far in the doctrine of practical spirit, feeling, [p. I.53] affect, passion, desire, inclination, propensity, etc., have been treated, in part excellently, by *Kant*, *Maass*, E. *Schulze* and others. Even if scientific insight into the nature of these processes is lacking, we nevertheless receive a wealth of true and subtle observations, which contribute essentially to their characterization. Had our authors given no more than those men, they would deserve no thanks; as it is, however, they give us contorted phrases instead of plain speech, blurred hints instead of firm features of character, and, instead of faithful observation of nature, not seldom manifest falsification.

In the last chapter *Rosenkranz* speaks of *happiness and arbitrary will*. The naturalness of spirit strives after the satisfaction of all desires, inclinations and passions, i.e. after happiness. The multitude and the conflict of the passions calls forth reflection, to the effect that through choice among the desires the greatest and most lasting enjoyment will arise; the decision is arbitrary will. But it is devoid of reason and delivers man up to the restless urge of the desires and to boredom. A criterion for reflection is necessary which goes beyond the relativity of enjoyment. It is the *freedom of spirit* in its objectivity. In family, society, state, art, religion, science, practical feeling attains the form adequate to it, which can justify itself as rational and reconcile itself with the universal of thinking. Thus practical spirit becomes really free, the doctrine of subjective spirit ends, and that of *objective* spirit begins. — This chapter could with equal right stand in any other book, for it contains no psychological investigations. Resolution, arbitrary will, reflection, freedom are merely named. Yet it would be worthy and becoming of psychology to show how the presentations of morality and right, virtue and vice, the praiseworthy and the shameful, how resolution, deliberation, practical principle, character and similar objects are formed. French thinkers have put forward the assertion that morality is a matter of opinion and custom; what civil society, in which we grow up, and the persons who bring us up, consider honorable and praiseworthy, [p. I.54] that we too consider to be so, and no disposition has a worth in and for itself. German philosophers of the eighteenth century contradicted this with indignation, and saw in it a desecration of the holy. In the nineteenth century other German philosophers return to that view. The freedom of spirit in its objectivity, teaches the *Hegelian* school, is the absolute criterion for the arbitrary will of subjective spirit; i.e. the sum of views on right and wrong which has taken shape at any given time in the family, in civil society, in the state, is the objectively valid standard for the action of every individual within them. This view stands worthily beside the one discussed above: might is right.

Toward the end of his book *Michelet* is considerably more detailed than *Rosenkranz*; in particular, determinism, indeterminism and freedom of the will are examined more closely by him, as also by *Erdmann*. According to both, the will is undetermined. The animal must obey its drive, says *Michelet* on p. 493, whereas the will of man is capable of complete indeterminacy; it can posit the drive as its own or also not. And on p. 505: the will is the determining factor of the determining factor, of external circumstances; arbitrary will is the contingency of the will. The will, according to *Erdmann* § 156, has the possibility of realizing all its determinations; they all count equally, the will is at their point of indifference or *aequilibrium*, and must itself give the decisive turn; thus it is undetermined. According to both authors, however, it is also determined, and determinism and indeterminism are, according to *Erdmann* § 160, each the truth of the other. Finally we read in § 161: In truth the will is to be thought as undetermined in its determination, and determined in its being undetermined, i.e. the free will. What a pity that *Leibniz* did not live long enough to let himself be so thoroughly instructed! *Michelet* reaches the same goal differently. Happiness, on p. 512, is the totality of the individual satisfactions. In it every individual satisfaction is only a means, a moment, but the universal is the end. The will therefore wills the universal. This is at the same time its own nature; it accordingly wills [p. I.55] itself in every satisfaction, and thereby it is free. “Freedom is the will that wills the will.” — This is a rather clumsy little conjuring trick. Universality is the vessel with the false bottom; on top lies happiness as the totality of the individual satisfactions, underneath the nature of the will in its unity with thinking. A slight pressure, and the cup appears filled alternately with the one and with the other, the one is the other, and the will wills itself. The author comes nearer the truth when he declares the free will to be drive raised to rationality; but this fruit hangs too high for the ease with which he is pleased to reach for it to attain it. He then goes on to speak of the person, of its other, the thing, and of their being joined together, property, which forms the transition to objective spirit as the completed realization of psychological freedom. Here we find nothing psychological any more.

—

Thus we have, then, accomplished the first part of our task, and have looked through the works before us with a view to how far they contain a faithful description of the mental states given in experience, and in addition a demonstration of the causes and laws of their origin. We have traveled a long road, at the end of which perhaps only a few readers may wish to accompany us. But what is unpleasant for the reader was still more so for the writer. Who can, without reluctance, talk through a long series of objects, at each of which he must carp, cavil, reject? There were also passages there of so glaring a kind as would otherwise probably cause one to shut a book without further ado and forever. But here we are dealing with the products of a school which stands there pretentious and influential. A censure expressed in general terms is usually considered sufficiently refuted by a contradiction couched in equally general terms; therefore it was necessary to go into detail and to show step by step of what kind that which is offered is. We have found [p. I.56] that of so many states of the soul not one is described even remotely with the precision that is claimed by genuine natural inquiry; and natural inquiry psychology must surely be too, if it is to attain to science and if by nature one is not arbitrarily to understand merely the corporeal, but the empirically given in general. We have found the descriptions of the simplest mental states unintelligible, through unnaturalness and bombast of expression, so that, if the familiar names did not come to his aid, the reader would guess only with difficulty, or not at all, what the authors are speaking of. Not only are the characteristic features frequently missing, but quite contradictory ones are put together; pure fabrications, finally, distort reality, and create here and there veritable monsters, themselves too bad for a fable, since they are not merely impossible but also absurd. If such is the state of the description of the objects treated, a comprehension of them from their causes and laws was in any case impossible. Instead of this we find, at the place where the previous treatment of psychology generally endeavors to discover laws, the assertion that there are none. This monstrous proposition, were it generally acknowledged, would make our science impossible; as it is, it destroys only the hope that the *Hegelian* school will ever achieve anything deserving of thanks in this field.

What has been said so far is not suited to give the reader a faithful picture of the works discussed. We wished to do nothing but apply to them the standard of genuine natural inquiry; for that purpose we had to consider the treatment which the facts receive here *in isolation*. But it is precisely their *connection* in which the school places its worth and its peculiarity. Were we to end here, it would accuse us of having entirely overlooked its most important thoughts, its real view and intention, and the reader, even if he agreed with us in all of the above, could suspect that we had picked out the less good for our censure and suppressed the better. We wish, however, to establish firm convictions. Therefore we shall now attempt to present the standpoint of the authors in its peculiarity; we shall [p. I.57] examine with what consistency they have proceeded from it, and whether what they have achieved can give us a compensation for everything that, standing on our own standpoint, we have so far missed in their performances.

The standpoint of our authors is *speculative knowing*, their method the *dialectical* one. Every concept, e.g. A, shows on closer consideration that it has its opposite, not-A, in itself; thus it turns over into the latter, just as the latter turns into the former; but thereby both show themselves to be identical. The necessary turning over of every concept into its opposite is the dialectical method; insight into its unity with its opposite is speculative knowing. If B is the name of the unity of A and not-A, then this too now turns over into its not-B, and the unity of B and not-B is C; in the same way C develops into the higher unity D, this into E, and so on, until the series of developments has arrived at some Z which, turning back into A, closes the whole into a circle. Here every later moment of the development is a higher one; for it contains the earlier ones within itself as sublated, and is thus richer than they. A proof of the correctness of this method was attempted here and there, but was usually held to be unnecessary and impossible. The method proves itself in its application. Take any science whatever, lift out the concept of its object and examine it closely, and it will in fact turn out that it contains its opposite and passes over into it. Therefore this method is not one brought to the objects from outside; it is immanent in them; it is the course which they themselves take. *Hegel*, with spirit and acumen and rare perseverance, has strung determinate objects on the empty circular thread of developments drawn above; they all flow into one another like waves, and the motion goes round and round without end. What we called A above is immediate empty being; the principal moments of the development are the following: *being* passes over into *essence*, this into the *concept*, which becomes the *logical Idea*. The latter freely releases from itself *nature*, which rises up through the stages of *mechanism*, *chemism* and *organism* [p. I.58] to *subjective* spirit, which, having become *objective* spirit, ends in *absolute* spirit. The last is now our Z above; it lowers itself again to empty being, and thus completes the circle. Between every two of the principal moments named here intermediate links are inserted. The works we have discussed treat a segment of this circular line, subjective spirit, as it emerges from the next earlier principal moment, nature, and passes over into the next later one, objective spirit.

We have herewith gained the basis for a critique such as this school is accustomed to demand; namely, one that takes its standpoint not *outside*, but *within* the system to be judged itself. We do not, to be sure, wish herewith to have acknowledged this demand; it takes consistency for truth, and is one of the many false assertions which gain admittance solely through the boldness with which they have recently been repeated often enough. Yet consistency is important enough never to be overlooked by criticism. Our business now is to investigate whether the moments of subjective spirit put forward by our authors really develop out of one another with necessity and in accordance with the dialectical method they have adopted.

First, however, we must also bring up a subject which has long occupied us, namely the conformity to law in the developments of subjective spirit. Laws such as we claimed above in the name of natural inquiry the authors, as we have seen, do not acknowledge; nevertheless they assert a conformity to law, namely that of dialectical development. To comprehend spirit in its development, says *Erdmann* § 4, means to recognize this development as necessary; this is done through the dialectical method. And now we ask: How is the dialectical method related to the genetic method? We mean it thus: a plant comes to be before our eyes. For this a seed is necessary, which draws all kinds of substances from air and earth to itself, the warmth of the sun usually helping in the process. Seed, those substances, the warmth of the sun are the conditions of the plant’s coming into being; they are also the material which must be consumed in order that out of it [p. I.59] the plant may come to be; they are, finally, the ground from which the existence of the plant proceeds. Whoever demonstrates to us those conditions and the manner in which the plant comes to be out of them shows the genesis of plants, and a scientific method which revealed this genesis of things would be the genetic method. Are we now to assume that the moment A, out of which the moment B develops dialectically, is the material, the condition, the ground out of which and through which B in fact comes to be? *Erdmann* replies from § 4 that the dialectical method is essentially different from the genetic; in § 5 he remarks: the individual stages of nature exist side by side, the stages of development of objective spirit occur one after another; and in § 162: the dialectical transition from comprehending thought to willing does not mean that the two cannot develop parallel to each other. So teach the other authors too, and the school has never believed that essence comes to be out of being in time, that the logical Idea is the material out of which nature is formed, that mechanism is consumed and disappears through chemism, thinking through willing, and the like. *Rosenkranz* even instructs us that in the series of developments A, B, C, both A and C are the ground of B, because the concept of ground is one in which everything is contained and which is contained in everything. Thus we cannot doubt the essential difference which the school makes between the dialectical and the genetic method, and the former shows not the temporal and causal, but purely the conceptual connection. Accordingly it would be possible that certain developments which, according to the dialectical method, run from A to Z, occur in reality from Z to A, or in any other sequence conceivable according to the rules of combinations and permutations. But now the temporal and causal sequence of changes is of the highest importance for us; there therefore appears in the *Hegelian* philosophy, which, after all, like every philosophy, would like to make reality comprehensible, a gap which seems to diminish its worth very greatly. Yet a misgiving arises. The dialectical method is supposed not to be external to things; it is supposed to present the course of things themselves; [p. I.60] would it not thereby be a genetic method? One recalls, further, that *Hegel* and his school, in the philosophy of history and in the history of philosophy, present the developments that actually took place as dialectical processes, and thus make genesis into dialectic. One recalls also how the school has sometimes endeavored to keep for itself the name of the “newest,” in the belief that the latest in time is the most perfect according to the dialectic; and a “Genesis of Spirit” we have read from it in print. What, then, becomes at all of the asserted essential difference between dialectic and genesis? Here is a very obscure spot in the system, to which we earnestly draw attention. It constantly tempts one, where the developments of reality keep pace with the dialectical one, to say: that is quite natural, for the genesis of reality is precisely the dialectic of the concept; but where they diverge from one another, one remarks: that again is quite natural, for the genesis of reality is, after all, *not* the dialectic of the concept.

We return to the doctrine of subjective spirit. For it the consequence follows from what has been said that it is useless for any practical application. Neither he who wishes to practice pedagogy, morals, politics, nor he who wishes to treat them scientifically, can learn anything from it. For what does it help him to know that some mental state B is conceptually the negation of A or the unity of A and not-A, if he does not learn from what and how that state develops, and hence what is to be done and what avoided if one wishes to bring it about or remove it in oneself or in others? This consequence is so decisive for the worth of the system that we do not doubt its friends will try to dispute it and to present the dialectical process as a process of the real as well. That is why we have just drawn attention to the express contradiction which the school itself raises against such an attempt, as well as to the acknowledged absurdity of the assertion that the lower moments of that dialectical series of development are in fact consumed and used up by the higher ones; and we now add the following. All three works before us include, in the series of the dialectical [p. I.61] moments of development of subjective spirit, somnambulism, mental illness, and passion as well. Now either the dialectical development is at the same time the factual one, and then every human being on his way from the cradle to the grave must necessarily be at least once a *clairvoyant*, once insane, once exclusively ruled by a passion; or, if one is willing to give experience its due and deny this, then the dialectical development is not the factual one. We let everyone choose as he pleases, but we hold to the express decision of the school and abide by the consequence drawn above. We shall find further confirmations of our opinion in the works before us later on. It accordingly appears as an oddity when in *Rosenkranz* and *Michelet* a feeble attempt nevertheless occurs here and there to explain a mental state genetically. That it has nowhere succeeded we have shown in detail above. It seems that the authors were not quite clear in their own minds about the relation of the two methods, and unconsciously succumbed to the demands which sound thinking makes on psychology. Their readers, to be sure, will gain little understanding through such a procedure, especially since in other places a lawful connection of the factual states is again expressly denied. *Erdmann* is consistent on this point; his exposition is purely dialectical, without any admixture of the genetic. — We said at the outset of these investigations that it would become apparent later in what sense the authors take our demand that the laws of the states of the soul be demonstrated; this, we hope, has now been done. Let us now consider the application which they make of their dialectical method.

Here we must not overlook the modesty with which *Rosenkranz* declares in the introduction, p. XI, that here, like *Hegel* himself in his lectures, he has frequently been forced to set up “innocent trichotomies”; namely such as give not so much an organic, that is, dialectical unfolding of the material, as rather a synoptic exposition of it. So honest a confession deserves respect; yet may the school [p. I.62] not forget that, like bold gamblers, it has staked its whole fortune on one throw, on the dialectical method; that from the application of this method comes all its knowledge, about this method itself and about everything else, and that outside it there is none.

Spirit is declared to be the *negation of nature*. We must investigate what nature is, where in its concept the necessity of a transition to its opposite lies, and whether this opposite really is spirit.

Nature is the negation of the logical Idea; as such it is asunderness with necessity and contingency. For the logical Idea is the absolute unity of all differences, the complete annihilation of their self-subsistence, freedom; asunderness, however, is opposed to absolute unity, as necessity and contingency are to freedom. Spirit, now, is identity, i.e. the non-self-subsistence of differences; it is freedom inasmuch as, as *Michelet* says, its differences are not differences that are, but differences that are just as much no differences; a being that is just as much sublated; or, according to *Erdmann*: inasmuch as its essence is posited in being-within-itself. And because nature is the only mode of asunderness, spirit is its negation. Spirit, *Michelet* continues, is active; it is only insofar as it is active; activity is its substance. It assimilates nature to itself both as moving, inasmuch as, as the life-principle of the body, it determines the latter in accordance with its own unity, and also as cognizing. But nature’s becoming idealized is also its own deed. The divine, i.e. logical, Idea, which is the inner essence of nature, seeks to free nature from its externality; asunderness takes itself back more and more into its center. So in the solar system; most of all, however, in the animal organism. In this way nature also generates spirit, which is its result. Or, since this is a one-sided relation of spirit to nature: spirit is the first, that which posits nature, but also the process of the sublation of all externality. As such the divine spirit continually generates the other, its conditions, nature and finite spirit, out of itself, in order to swallow them up again. It is love, which wants to reconcile the other with itself, and therefore eternally generates it. [p. I.63] That which reveals and that which is revealed are identical in it; spirit is only the manifestation of itself. The modes of its revelation, however, are 1) nature, the “petrified intelligence”; 2) finite spirit, with the twofold determinacy of indifferent asunderness and of ideality that is for itself; 3) infinite spirit, God, for whom nature has lost its externality. Into his unity nature and finite spirits are taken up. Everything generated by him is or becomes he himself; this is the eternal begetting of the Son in Christianity; God’s love for the world is his incarnation.

So much, and far more besides, *Michelet* is able to tell us about spirit right in the introduction. About the concept of nature given here we will only remark that, in Spinozistic fashion, extension is posited in place of the extended. But does its opposite now lie in this concept? Certainly, for asunderness is thinkable only as the negation of within-one-another. — So the concept of asunderness turns over into that of within-one-another? Not in the least. Space with its infinite divisibility has occasioned many investigations; thus thinkers have brought manifold concepts into connection with it. This can also happen with the concept of within-one-another; just as it is already contained in the former, it can also be linked with it in thought in manifold further ways, so that thinking passes over from the former to the latter; but *it itself* eternally remains a different concept, and never does the former become the latter. — But supposing one were permitted to say: because the former includes the latter, it passes over into it; would one now have the concept of spirit as the negation of nature? Is the empty concept of within-one-another so constituted that it holds in its hollow, withered body the essence of spirit with the exuberance of its states, the essence of the lowest as of the highest spirit, together with the miracle of creation and the mysteries of religion? How would this ever be possible? Nothing of all this is to be found in it; everything is begged and obtained by stealth. Spirit, it is said, is ideality and freedom, i.e. absolute unity, the sublatedness of all differences, and thereby the negation of nature. But the logical Idea was earlier declared to be just the same; it, then, [p. I.64] is the negation of nature. And so it must be, too, if, as was asserted earlier, nature is the negation of the logical Idea. But with this one would have gone not forward but backward; and one *wants* to go forward. It is above all absolute spirit which has long hovered before one as the goal that must be reached at any price. Therefore that absolute unity must now be synonymous with within-one-another, although in the latter there lies at most a sublation of those differences which lie in asunderness and its kinds, but not of all possible differences; therefore that unity must suddenly put up with the name “spirit,” although it was otherwise called the logical Idea, and under the shield of the new name whatever serves the purpose is introduced into the old within-one-another; therefore *Erdmann* substitutes for within-one-another the more convenient being-within-itself, which already presupposes a self, hence an I, a spirit. Spirit, it is also said, is thinking, hence the identity of subjectivity and objectivity, and thereby, too, it is within-one-another. We already know this identity, in which spirit becomes wholly identical with its objects, except for a trifle, irrational sensation. Rather as if a mathematician were to say: five is quite as much as twelve, except for a trifle, namely seven. Out of such miserable material one does not make a science; one might sooner build bridges out of dry drift sand.

Spirit, so we heard, results from nature; but it is just as much its ground. This is comprehensible if, as *Rosenkranz* asserts, the concept of ground is in everything and everything is in it. It also becomes comprehensible when one considers that the development goes round in a circle, where, therefore, every moment is just as much before as after every other. So we grant that spirit is the truth of nature, as soon as it is conceded to us that nature is just as much the truth of spirit. Here, however, we come up against a peculiar idiosyncrasy of the school. It will not concede the latter; it stresses the one, and passes over the other in silence. But the dialectical method knows, as we hope, no privileges; it exercises its compelling power in the same way at all stages of development. Therefore it is only another surreptitious substitution [p. I.65] when *Hegel* says of the logical Idea that it resolves and freely releases nature out of itself. The logical Idea has no more to resolve than any other moment; nature is the negation and the truth of the logical Idea, as spirit is the same for nature, indeed empty being for absolute spirit, and everything just as much the other way round. The school will resist such assertions, for they strip away the whole glittering veil of mist which clothes the wooden scaffolding of the dialectical method. But they follow with necessity from this method itself, and fall only with it. Spirit, so we are told, is thinking. Just so *Spinoza* says that bodies are extensions, spirits thoughts. But he did at least have one substance, even if only One, to which extensions and thoughts adhered as attributes and modes; our school has nothing whatever of this kind. It has extensions without anything extended, thoughts without anything thinking. And these extensions and thoughts are at the same time just as much no extensions and no thoughts; for nothing *is*, everything only *becomes*. This becoming, finally, is itself no proper becoming; for the dialectical method is not the genetic one, and what it presents is not the real genesis of things, not becoming proper.

Up to now the talk was of spirit in general. But the school knows a *series of spirits*, and it means to speak here only of the first member of this series, of subjective spirit; namely, of spirit as it shows itself in the individual human being. How does it arrive at it? *Michelet* instructs us that spirit proceeds from the death of individualities. For death is the negation of the individual, hence of the asunderness of nature, and spirit as thinking is likewise the negation of the differences of nature. — So we thus have a third pretender to the honor of being the negation of nature; logical Idea, spirit and death all make this claim. But is it true that the death of a plant or of an animal sublates the asunderness of its constituent parts? No, one has merely, quite quietly, slipped the negation of asunderness into the place of the negation of individuality, i.e. of the individual. But if from the above premises [p. I.66] a conclusion followed, then according to ordinary logic it would run: therefore spirit *is* death; but not: therefore spirit *follows* from death. *Erdmann* brings out another thought. According to him the process of the genus is the highest thing that nature produces. In it an endless conflict presents itself, inasmuch as the genus does not attain reality, since it produces only specimens, while the specimen in turn succumbs to the genus. Spirit, now, as thinking, is the actual unity of genus and specimen, that is, of the universal and the individual; therefore spirit proceeds from the process of the genus. *Michelet* too hints at this view by drawing attention to the double sense of the word “Erkennen” [to know], which means just as much thinking as copulating. We feel no desire to enter upon a critique of this view; let it merely stand here as a notice for our readers. *Michelet* continues: this spirit is no longer bound to bodily asunderness; it is universality, the genus and substance of everything individual; its ideal, simple truth, from which all individuality and determinacy first emerges; it is the universal substance of the earth; the universal soul of nature; what magic has called the earth-spirit. The awakening of this soul is the becoming of the individual souls. Man is this soul of nature come to consciousness, its being-for-itself and self-apprehension. The material world is not present beyond and outside the universal soul as a self-subsistent existence; spirit is rather the truth of nature; it does indeed have matter as a limit in itself, but as one which it has posited for itself. The individuality of spirit is the being of universal spirit in material existence; every individual is a determinate materialization of the universal world-soul. Therefore the body has no power against spirit. Spirit is the true essence of man, which has in nature, the body, only the mode of its external appearance. The connection of body and soul must not astonish us, since matter is only a moment in the development of spirit. With this the question of the seat of the soul is settled at the same time. The soul is everywhere and nowhere for the body. It is not in the body, [p. I.67] but rather the body is in it; in it all material differences are sublated, and it is the bond that holds them together. It has no substantiality for itself, and the body still less; it is only the spiritual substance and activation of this spatial asunderness, hence that which posits place for the body. It is subjective spirit, which is finite, limited by nature as something external to it, from which it has not yet quite got free. But its destination is infinite spirit, for which nature has lost its externality. This destination it reaches by idealizing its body, overcoming its foreignness, and thus bringing the bond between body and soul, which is God, more and more to consciousness. In the being-taken-up of nature and of finite spirit into infinite spirit the individual must recognize his highest goal, his highest bliss.

How do matters stand here with the dialectical method? Spirit, we are taught, is the negation of nature. Very well, if it seems necessary to you, then negate nature and have your spirit. Quite right, say the authors, we have spirit. But it is not yet the right one; this negation is not yet a negation proper. Well then, we reply, this negation is a failed thought; throw it away and negate better. Oh no, they say; this failed negation we call subjective spirit, and we negate it again, and what comes out we call objective spirit. Now, then, do you have the negation of nature? Not yet; but we negate it again, and go on in this way until everything we have seen, heard and read of spirit and spirits in this world has found its little place; then we conclude and negate properly. — This is method, and dialectical method at that. — The concept turns over into its opposite; but like a bad tumbler it falls flat on its belly ten times, until on the eleventh it succeeds in coming right round and back onto its feet. *Hegel* himself has given the most striking example of this in his subjective logic. There the concept at the very beginning falls apart into parts; then, by virtue of the dialectical method, it must gather itself together again into unity. But it does this like a clumsy peddler who wants to tie up his bundle, and does not get done with it [p. I.68] until all the forms of judgments and syllogisms that have been set up since Aristotle are happily accommodated, namely at the end of the Logic. We hold, however, that one either negates or does not negate; that therefore the whole series of spirits which there negate nature and yet also do not negate it, these intermediate stations between a concept and its negation, these half-, quarter- and eighth-negations, in which the concept, like a runner in a dream, strains incessantly and yet gets no further — we hold that they are scientific absurdities, invented in order to adorn, after a fashion, the naked body of the dialectical method with what one knows not through it but from elsewhere. The fault here indicated in the application of the dialectical method we consider a principal defect of the school.

The earth-spirit, the world-soul, the union of finite spirit with infinite spirit we will pass over in silence; phantasticality and Spinozism have often been seen together before. But *Spinoza* teaches consistently that to every modification of the divine extension there corresponds a modification of the divine thinking, and that therefore every body is ensouled. What does the dialectical method say in this regard? It grants a subjective spirit only to the human body. Even the animal soul is, according to *Michelet*, only a principle of unity bound to matter, the active but mortal Idea of the body. As a plurality, *Erdmann* thinks, subjective spirit must appear, because nature, according to its concept, is an asunderness, a plurality of individual things. But as surely as a stone is a natural thing, an asunderness, and as surely as spirit, in the first instance subjective spirit, is the dialectically necessary negation of nature: so surely must the stone, too, have its subjective spirit, and one cannot deny it this, unless one asserts that the dialectical method congeals in the process of crystallization. We do not see how here the consistency of the system can be brought into harmony with experience, or the assertions of our authors with one another. They do, it is true, assume that spirit proceeds in the first instance from death, or from the process of the genus, both of which are to be found only in organisms; but then [p. I.69] spirit would surely have to be found at least in plants and animals. One could, to be sure, imagine that, since the dialectical process presents not a genetic but only a conceptual connection, a spirit might stand over against the mineral body, as over against the plant and animal body, as its negation, without exactly dwelling in it as a soul dwells in the body. But apart from the fact that our authors by no means assume this, why then is its negation so intimately linked precisely with the *human body* that, when my body climbs into the express coach and makes a journey, my soul must accompany it, whereas there it is supposed to be so entirely different? *Michelet* does say that every human body is only a materialization of the world-soul, according to the principle that not only is every earlier moment of the great series of development the ground of the later one, but also the other way round; but for that very reason the whole of nature too, stones and plants included, is a materialization of spirit, and all its parts have an equal right to be ensouled. The dialectical method has here evidently given way a little out of complaisance toward experience.

Here too we find the intermediate stations between a concept and its negation. Subjective spirit is, it is true, among all spirits the nearest negation of nature, but it does not, after all, negate nature in the first instance, but *organic* nature as an intermediate link. This, however, gives us occasion for another remark about the method, which we do consider important. The organism, it is said, negates mechanism and chemism, which it contains within itself as sublated, i.e. organic nature negates inorganic nature. This is no doubt correct; but of what kind is the negation here? Does it strike the whole concept of inorganic nature, or only one part of it, while letting the other subsist? *Hegel*, it is true, declares all those barbarians who think that a concept could consist of parts; we nevertheless profess this barbarism without fear, and count on the assent of all logic except the *Hegelian*, and of a sufficient number of thinkers. We confidently assert that the school indeed [p. I.70] *speaks* of concepts as *simple*, but in fact *treats* them as *composite*. So in the case above and in countless others. Suppose now that a concept A consists of the parts a, b, c; then one can sublate either all of them, or one, or two, which gives seven possible cases. Assuming, then, that one refrains from any addition of new parts, whereby the cases could increase to infinity, there are seven negations of the concept A. By virtue of the dialectical method it must now turn over into its negation, but into which? One sees that, if concepts really are composite, the method lacks any necessity of progress in a determinate direction. Here, then, the asserted simplicity comes in excellently handy; for of a simple concept there is indeed only one negation. But if someone, while asserting the simplicity of the concept A, nevertheless treats it as a composite one, he will let it pass over into whichever of the seven possible negations seems most suitable to him, and declare this transition the only possible one; to someone else another will seem the only possible one. The evil increases. Of B as the negation of A it is affirmed that it is not merely the sublation of A as negation, but also this sublation in the sense of preservation, conservation of A. So B is, after all, only A augmented by one determination, or it stands to A in the relation of the particular to the universal. This “metaphysical negation,” as distinguished from the merely logical one, now greatly lightens the business of dialectic; for one can let A pass over at one’s convenience into its proper negation or else into a subordinate concept. Here again, to be sure, the method seems to lack any necessity of a determinate progress; for I can determine A by α, but also by β, γ, etc., and so pass over to the most diverse particular concepts. *Hegel* helps out with the assertion that every universal has only One particular; or better: that there are only two particulars, the universal and the particular; if nature here and there behaves otherwise, this only shows its impotence to hold fast to the rigor of the concept. It is certainly [p. I.71] an original turn to lay the blame on the objects when the thinker’s concepts will not fit them. The evil reaches its peak through the following. In general every concept B is the negation of every other concept A, because it is just another one, hence not A. If, therefore, it is only required in general to pass over from A to its negation, then, whatever A may mean, any concept one pleases can be put in the place of B, provided only that it is other than A itself; and with this room is given to the most unrestricted arbitrariness. In this sense the *Hegelian* school may call its knowledge an absolute one. In fact we are convinced that in the circumstances noted here lies a principal occasion of the arbitrariness which is found to be so astonishingly great in many works of this school, as well as of the strife that has broken out in many ways within it.

After subjective spirit has been reached in the manner described, it is now the proper task of the works before us to know it scientifically. It is activity, nothing but activity, and indeed thinking. Experience, however, as is well known, shows in the human spirit much that is otherwise not taken for thinking, e.g. perceptions, activities of phantasy, feelings and the like. It shows further that spirit forms itself, develops, that, beginning from very simple stirrings, it advances under the influence of education and other favorable circumstances to more composite, higher states. Our authors make use of this and attempt to form out of those states a continuous series which begins with the lowest and ends with the highest, which is supposed to be precisely thinking. Now dialectic comes in. They declare that all these states are already thinking, spirit, but still at lower stages, hence not yet the right, proper spirit; that each succeeding state is always the negation of the preceding one; that thinking is the last, the highest negation in the series. Likewise, they say, every subordinate state, because it is spirit, is already the negation of nature too, but not yet the right, proper negation, because not yet thinking. In short, here too we have a series of intermediate negations between [p. I.72] nature and its proper negation, thinking, into which all the states of the soul that experience shows, or that psychologists have hitherto taken up into their registers, must be capable of being ranged. About this manner of putting the dialectical method into application what is needed has already been said. *Rosenkranz* complains, p. 7, of the difficulty, given the transition of all mental states into one another, of giving exact definitions of them. This we comprehend very well; it cannot be other than extremely difficult to say of a thing what it is, after one has declared that it is just as much everything else as what it is. And of what kind the definitions are that have come about here, we have shown in detail above.

The dialectical method naturally strides along in trichotomies, and so we find three main stages of the development of subjective spirit. It is noteworthy that already here the absolute necessity of the method becomes uncertain; our authors do not agree on those main stages. Let us first consider *Michelet’s* account. The *first* main stage of mental development is spirit as *soul*, i.e. spirit in its being bound to nature, in its unity with the body. Why so? “Since the development of spirit on the ladder of its activities to perfected freedom is only the final goal of our considerations, whereas its beginning is rather the unfreedom of spirit, the whole first part of our science presents this being-bound of spirit to nature.” So says *Michelet*, p. 71. But we are not here to be told little stories about what happens here or there; that a beginning is made here with spirit as soul, we see; but *why* is it done? Why must the concept of nature pass over first into that of the soul? The negation of nature is spirit; this is not soul, not unity of spirit *and* body; what, then, is the soul doing here? One sees that we have an intermediate negation, a negation of nature that is as yet none. And why does one proceed so?
