Because one knows from *experience* that the spirit of man is bound to his body, and that newborn children, to outward appearance, are not much more than mere [p. I.73] bodies. Do the facts become comprehensible by one’s hoodwinking the method? and is the method itself, which is to prove its truth precisely through its application, justified in such a way? And this soul, what sort of thing is it, then? It is the unity of body and spirit, hence the unity of asunderness and of the negation of asunderness. Such a unity is otherwise held to be a logical absurdity; the speculative method, however, asserts such things, and that the soul is one of them we are assured very earnestly. Assurances, however, are not reasons; the speculative method is, as we said, to prove itself through its application, not through assurances. It is also said that the soul is the being-bound of spirit to the body. But of spirit we heard that it proceeds from the death of individuals; yet it is not dead bodies that are ensouled, but living ones. A new proof that the dialectical method is not at the same time the genetic one, and that that proceeding is meant to present only the conceptual, not the factual connection. How, now, does it follow from that conceptual connection that this soul is nevertheless bound to the body, the living body? *Michelet* says, p. 72: “spirit here determines the body, and the body spirit.” These are well-known facts, which, however, one ought not to narrate but to prove dialectically. If, further, they determine each other, then they are two; as such they are not one in all eternity; how, then, are they to yield a unity conceptually? It seems they are two and one, just as one happens to need them. — May the dialectical method try to resolve all these difficulties for us.

The soul overcomes what is natural in it, tears itself loose from it, and now relates itself freely to the nature standing over against it, in order to try its idealizing upon it. This idealizing is, as we saw earlier, thinking, the transformation of the objective into the subjective, and so subjective spirit at its *second* main stage is *theoretical* spirit or *thinking*. — Where is the dialectical method here? The soul, so we are told, is unity of spirit and body, its negation therefore non-unity, spirit become free, which is thinking. We [p. I.74] must unfortunately contradict. The negation of the soul as the unity of spirit and body might at most be the non-unity of spirit and body; this concept, however, is very different from that of spirit become free. But if one wanted to put the latter in place of the former, then the concept of the body become free would certainly have an equal claim. And why, then, must the concept of the soul pass over into its negation, theoretical spirit? Because, we are told, that concept is a contradiction, which cannot persist, wherefore further development must ensue. The contradiction we do see, but we do not understand the inference. Since when does one use contradictions as premises in order to derive errors from errors? We must give them up as bad, useless thoughts, and begin afresh, unless they had shown themselves to us as necessary and irrefutable; then we should perhaps have to deal with them otherwise. Of that contradiction, however, we were only told that one wished to begin with it, namely with the soul; for a demonstration of its necessity we have asked in vain. What, further, are we to understand by that being-detached, being-become-free of spirit from the body, provided that it continues to form an opposition to its earlier being bound *to*, being poured *through* the body? After all, no thought walks about on the earth without a body. — While subjective spirit has already become theoretical spirit, it at the same time continues to endure as soul. For the thinking man is continually an inhabitant of the earth, belongs to a certain race, a determinate sex, age, etc.; in short, spirit even then still has the manifold determinacies that make it soul. From this too we learn that the dialectical method is not the genetic one.

The *third* main stage of spirit is *practical spirit*; it is willing, the faculty of desire. As theoretical spirit transformed objectivity into subjectivity, so now practical spirit puts its subjectivity out in the place of objectivity. — Where is the dialectical method here? According to it one might expect that the third main stage would be the negation and unity of the [p. I.75] two preceding stages. Of this, however, nothing is to be read; it is at most the negation of the second main stage. But it is not even that. The putting of subjectivity in the place of objectivity does indeed form an opposition to the putting of objectivity in the *place* of subjectivity; but, as is well known, that is not how thinking was defined. We thus have no pure negation of the second main stage, but a concept that negates thinking roughly just as any other concept differing from it, e.g. that of the circle, would do. But supposing that concept were really the negation of thinking, would we then have with it the concept of willing and thus of practical spirit? This question we have long since answered in the negative. The pure negation of thinking is not-thinking; this leads to no faculty of desire. But the author knows that man does not merely think but also wills, and in all psychologies there is talk, besides cognition, of a desiring as well; therefore he too must speak of it. To do so before he treats thinking did not, presumably, seem advisable, since there is no willing prior to all thinking. So it had to happen later, and then, to be sure, willing too had to be made the negation of thinking. So much the dialectical method must put up with, and something more besides. For subjective spirit, one would think, cannot after all become more, even at its highest stage of development, than spirit, i.e. thinking, theoretical spirit; here, however, it has become more, if indeed the later stage is a higher one. Experience must often enough put up, in this work, with coming to harm through the dialectical method; so let the latter too accept being brought to shame here by the power with which the former forces itself even upon its despisers.

*Rosenkranz* and *Erdmann* follow their master in forming the three main stages. According to them too, spirit at its first stage is soul; what we remarked earlier in this respect against *Michelet* holds against them too. The former, however, now further maintains that spirit is either bound to the body, as soul, or free of it, as spirit. The latter, on the other hand, think of [p. I.76] the matter as when a bird frees itself from the lime-twig. It first draws the viscous element out into threads; in this state it is loose from the twig, and also not loose, until it tears these too and flies away. This intermediate station in the liberation of spirit from the body they call *consciousness*; for them it is the second main stage of subjective spirit, and consists, as we have already heard, in distinguishing oneself from everything else. Rejecting the intermediate station, we here agree with *Michelet*. But if we could not comprehend there how the negation of spirit’s being bound to the body yields the concept of theoretical spirit, we see even less where that consciousness is supposed to come from. As distinguishing oneself from everything else it does indeed negate spirit’s being bound to the body; but only in the way every concept negates every other. The child already distinguishes itself from all external things, and so has consciousness, before it yet distinguishes its spirit from its body; when it has arrived at the latter, it in turn distinguishes its spirit not merely from its body but from the whole of nature; and not merely from nature, but also from all other spirits. Thus consciousness would be not merely the negation of being bound to the body, but also of being bound to the whole of nature, to all other spirits. However, *Rosenkranz* assures us that it is just the negation of the soul and nothing more, and this assurance is his dialectical method. *Erdmann*, on the other hand, gives a special proof besides. According to §. 62 ff., the soul is the individual according to the moment of universality, insofar, namely, as the soul is spirit, hence thinking; the body is the individual according to the moment of particularity; thus the individual, divided within itself, forms a contradiction. Such a contradiction cannot subsist, and the continued sublation of the difference between bodily and psychical existence yields — the *life-process*! It is the identifying of body and soul. But identity of body and soul is habit, according to §. 59, and when it has become complete it is *death*; man lives himself, or habituates himself, to death. However, the identity of universality and particularity is also consciousness, hence that process is also the becoming-conscious, and so the soul, by virtue of the dialectic lying in the contradiction, [p. I.77] necessarily passes over into consciousness. — Who would have dreamed of that? Habit, death, consciousness are one, identity of the universal and the particular. This is so remarkable a dialectic of thought that we have no hesitation in calling it drivel.

At the *third* main stage, finally, spirit is really *spirit*, and therefore of course *thinking*. In consciousness it still stands as subject over against objects, and is accordingly not yet wholly freed from nature; but in having become thinking, it has taken up the whole of objectivity into itself, brought it into unity with subjectivity. Except, of course, we add once more, for a trifle, the irrational sensations, with which, however, the reason of absolute philosophers does not concern itself. Now, however, one is evidently in danger of getting more than three stages of development, for theoretical spirit passes over into practical spirit. This, however, the method does not permit. How is this to be remedied? Very easily. One makes the doctrine of theoretical and practical spirit into a single chapter, and then has, after all, only three in all. Thus the dialectical method in the end even puts up with receiving orders from the copyist or even from the bookbinder. One inconvenience, moreover, still remains: the third chapter has only two subdivisions, subjective spirit as spirit only two stages of development, the theoretical and the practical. But the method demands trichotomies. Has it suddenly gone lame? — Practical spirit follows here from theoretical spirit roughly just as in *Michelet*. Spirit, which as theoretical spirit has sublated objectivity and now knows it as its own determinacy, must know it as a contradiction not to exist objectively as well. Thereby it is compelled to introduce itself into objectivity, and this, as its own, can offer it no resistance but must let it into itself. So now spirit has become the capacity to transform itself independently into an objectivity; it is practical spirit, will. That is to say, then: consciousness had to become theoretical spirit because it would be a contradiction if there were *an* objectivity outside spirit; theoretical spirit [p. I.78] must become practical spirit because it would be a contradiction if there were *no* objectivity outside spirit. But this has already been discussed earlier, and what we objected before against *Michelet* also belongs here. Thus already in the first main stages the dialectic appears as a method of arbitrariness, which admits of agreement only so far as the obedience of the pupils to the plan prescribed by the master reaches.

Referring back to an earlier remark, we ask how it comes about, then, that certain developments, e.g. those of theoretical and practical spirit, run alongside each other in fact, while dialectically they lie in one series; whereas others, e.g. all the moments of theoretical spirit, form only one single series both dialectically and in fact? Should this not be worth an explanation, or can none be given dialectically? — Further, we see that *Erdmann* throughout bases the emergence of the later moments on the presence of contradictions in the earlier ones. Now supposing — what, to be sure, is not proved by our authors — that the earlier moments with their contradictions were irrefutable: then they would certainly have to be developed further; for as they are, one could not keep them. But what would the development profit us if we only ever came to new contradictions? This is here admittedly the case. From contradiction to contradiction thought is driven, and nowhere finds rest. Not even in the last member of the dialectical series, absolute spirit, in which all differences, even those of subjective, objective and all the other spirits, are supposed to be sublated; this is rather the very greatest contradiction. For if it contains everything within itself as sublated, it nevertheless contains it within itself, and it is no more sublated in it than mechanism in chemism, theoretical spirit in practical, subjective spirit in objective, which, as is well known, despite the sublation persist quite vigorously. For this sublating also means as much as preserving, whereby everything just as much is not as is, and therefore also just as much is as is not; and by the fact that absolute spirit must again lower itself to empty being, [p. I.79] it itself proves that it has no rest but swims along in the dialectical whirlpool. So we are not rid of the oppressive contradictions after all; we roll the stone of Sisyphus, we are Dante’s damned, who are whipped about restlessly by an eternal whirlwind, the insoluble contradictions. Is it really worth while to pursue such a work?

The three main stages of development cited have in turn their intermediate stations, or each of them resolves itself into a series of developments formed out of continued subordinate and superordinate trichotomies. We must see how they justify themselves through the dialectical method, and the latter through them. First we consider the developments of spirit as *soul*. Here too our authors differ from one another, and indeed in very manifold ways.

The first main stage of the soul is *spirit in its immediate unity with naturalness*. At this stage every determinacy of the body is at the same time one of spirit. Now the body lives on the earth, and this is a part of the solar system; hence, according to *Rosenkranz*, a subordinate stage of the soul is: the determinacy of spirit by the planetary life of the earth. The determinacies of spirit here are its “moods.” The lowest and hence the first stage of the development of spirit in its becoming out of nature is solar life, or the solar determinacies of spirit, the moods that the change of the seasons and of the times of day brings with it. — We would gladly have learned how, then, from the concept of the organism, or of death, from which, after all, the concept of subjective spirit is supposed to result, it is precisely the concept of the *solar* determinacies of spirit that arises first. However one may search through the former, one will nowhere discover in them the concept of the sun; whence, then, should it occur in a concept that is the negation of them, the negation proceeding from them? But we recall that *Michelet*, p. 87, maintains that of all the heavenly bodies it is only on the earth that spirit has come to existence. We have already more than once had to lament the secretiveness [p. I.80] of this author; here too he conceals from us the ground of this assertion, which is certainly very interesting to many readers. Presumably he now holds the same view of organisms, since spirit results from them. Accordingly, its telluric existence would already lie in the concept of the organism. Likewise one could say that in the concept of the earth, as a part of the solar system, the concept of the sun already lies, and so this is already contained in the concept of the organism, from where it passes over into that of subjective spirit. So one could say, but how can it be proved? The solar determinacy of spirit is followed in *Rosenkranz*, as the second stage of development, by the lunar determinacy. Where is the dialectical method here? Where is the contradiction in the concept of solar determinacy which compels one to pass over to the lunar? The moon is the negation of the sun, but not one whit more than the earth, Venus or Sirius are. And which, now, is the third stage of spirit, the unity of the solar and lunar determinacies? It is the telluric determinacy. How so? is the earth the unity of sun and moon? Oh no, that is not what *Rosenkranz* means; here he does not even attempt to demonstrate a dialectical connection; *experience* shows that man depends on sun, moon and earth, and this empirical material is “presented in a perspicuous survey”; the trichotomy is an “innocent” one. *Michelet* and *Erdmann* reach further back and give us the cosmic, sidereal and planetary determinacies of spirit as the first trilogy. It needs no demonstration that it is just as “innocent” as the former.

The combination of the three members of development discussed yields, in *Rosenkranz*, the first member of a higher trichotomy, namely, as was said, the determinacy of spirit by the planetary life of the earth. As the *second* member, there stands over against it the determinacy of *racial difference*. With what dialectical necessity does the latter develop from the former? The author does not consider it necessary to go into this; he gives the subordinate trichotomy of racial determinacy: the black, yellow and white race. Now others, to be sure, count more than three [p. I.81] races, but the method demands a trichotomy, and he obtains it by taking together, under the name of the yellow, all races that are not black or white. Accordingly, the concept of the black race would proceed dialectically, with necessity, from the concept of the telluric determinacy of spirit; the yellow race would be the necessary negation of the black, and the white the unity and truth of those two. That this is so, *Rosenkranz* himself does not maintain; the trichotomy is therefore not a dialectical one but an “innocent” one. — The *third* member, or the unity of the planetary determinacies and the racial differences, is the *natural determinacy of the individual*. It splits into a trichotomy whose first member is temperament. This in turn dissolves into a trichotomy; but since there are four temperaments, the choleric and the melancholic are brought into one unity under the title: the temperaments of opposition, and the dialectical method is rescued once again. Accordingly, the sanguine temperament would proceed necessarily and immediately from the white race, its negation would be the temperaments of opposition, and the unity of all the phlegmatic temperament. Over against temperament stands *aptitude*, namely as a sense for something, as talent and as genius. So again, the sense for something would proceed from the phlegmatic temperament, talent would be the necessary negation of that sense, and genius the unity of the latter and of talent. Finally, *idiosyncrasy* is the unity of the temperaments and of aptitude, so that genius passes over conceptually into apathy, whose negation is antipathy, while the unity of both is sympathy. It would indeed be strange if with this the essence of all the states named were comprehended, if genius, for example, were really nothing but the unity of the sense for something and of talent; but we should do the author an injustice if we thought he maintains this. He demonstrates no dialectical connection; the trichotomies are all once again “innocent” ones. *Michelet* and *Erdmann* let the racial determinacies pass over not, as *Rosenkranz* does, into the differences of temperament, but into the nationalities, which the latter too already intimates; [p. I.82] the unity of the differences of race and nationality is, however, with *Michelet* the difference of sex, and with *Erdmann* it is the local determinacies through ethical communities to which the individual belongs by nature. From the difference of sex there develop, with *Michelet*, the aptitudes, whose necessary negation is the temperaments, whereas with *Rosenkranz*, as we have seen, the aptitudes conversely formed the negation of the temperaments, while these proceeded from the diversity of races.

If, going by the conduct of the authors themselves, we can see in all this only “innocent” trichotomies, there nevertheless remains a connection of the whole development which passes itself off as the dialectical one, namely a stepwise progress from the most universal, the cosmic or at least solar determinacies of spirit, down to the most individual, the idiosyncrasies. It has already been said that the school represents dialectical progress as a transition of each concept into its negation, but also, on occasion, into its particular. Both transitions are taken as identical by means of the artifice that the negation preserves the negated concept within itself and is therefore the richer concept, which is just what is also the case with the particular as against the universal. Nevertheless they are not really identical. The school will certainly not grant that spirit is the particular, while nature, whose negation it is, is the universal; rather, it here reverses the relation. But the double sense of the dialectical method evidently makes its application a great deal easier, and so it is supposed to be present in the above account as well. In the first place we must remark that the above series does not even present a continuous transition from the more universal to the particular, since the differences of sex and of temperament are not one whit less universal than the diversities of race and nationality. But supposing this fault did not occur, we should, in the second place, still have only a logical arrangement of those concepts, but not a dialectical transition, a demonstration that each later one proceeds with *necessity* from the one next before it. Our authors declare themselves incapable of accomplishing this — in fact, and *Rosenkranz* in so many words as well — for they do not even attempt [p. I.83] it for the most part. Perhaps it will be thought that such an attempt is unnecessary, and that it suffices to show that B is the particular of A; the transition, it will be said, is then self-evident by virtue of the dialectical method? So indeed it might be, were it not that this method must first prove itself correct precisely in its application to determinate concepts. Thus, through the series of moments of the development of the soul that has been set up, neither are these moments comprehended from their dialectical connection, nor has the dialectical method found its confirmation through a special application.

The moments of the development of the soul are not at an end with this; rather, all those discussed so far together form the first member of a higher trichotomy, which with *Rosenkranz* and *Erdmann* is not yet, but with *Michelet* is already, the highest for the soul. The further course is this: from the abiding constitutions of spirit just mentioned, in its being bound to the body, the authors pass over to the changeable ones, e.g. age, the alternation of sleep and waking; from there to those states in which the soul already appears freer from the body than hitherto (e.g. dreaming, presentiment); the conclusion is made by the states in which the soul finds itself as ruling over the body, and thus in a unity with it mediated by the preceding developments (physiognomy and the like). This is the master’s plan; in the particular arrangements, however, the pupils diverge from one another, as a glance at the tables of contents shows, in so many ways that the arbitrariness of the method censured above, and the lack of a rule by which progress in a determinate order would be secured, is thereby amply confirmed. Without going into all the details of these accounts, we shall single out only what seems especially noteworthy.

*Rosenkranz* makes the transition, p. 53, from the abiding to the changeable determinacies of the soul with the simple remark that besides the former the latter also occur. This is evidently no dialectical development. Enumerating them, he first discusses the stages of life, namely youth, maturity and old age. From them he passes over to the difference of sex, which [p. I.84] presents itself as man, woman, and the sublation of the difference of sex in the process of the genus. It is, however, no changeable determinacy, since a woman does not become a man; for that reason *Michelet* already lists it among the abiding qualities. The third member of the trichotomy is sleep and waking, with its subordinate members, being awake, falling asleep and awakening. It needs no proof that the “innocent” trichotomies continue here. — *Michelet* and *Erdmann* make the transition in another way, namely by continuing the particularization of the universal. Now if the individual particularizes itself further, what will come of it then? The stages of life, they answer. But why precisely these? Because experience presents them; this, to be sure, is not said, but neither do we find any other reason given. Thus we see how the method which, according to *Hegel*, takes absolutely nothing from outside, constantly goes begging to experience. — Sensation has a different position with each of our authors. With *Rosenkranz* it follows immediately upon the changeable determinacies of the soul, and so seems meant to be the unity of these and the abiding ones, although he does not venture to assert this expressly. With *Erdmann* it is a far later member in the development of the soul; with *Michelet* it no longer belongs to the soul at all, but to theoretical spirit. *Rosenkranz* divides it into outer and inner sensation; a third member is lacking. Here, then, the dialectic has contented itself with a duality. The outer sensations are of five kinds; but they are brought down to a triad, in that sight and hearing are placed under one common name, smell and taste under another. Here *Erdmann’s* dialectical development is precisely the opposite of the one which *Rosenkranz* sets up; what is the third member here, the unity of the two preceding ones, is the first there, and vice versa. — Sensation, according to *Rosenkranz*, is first sensation in itself, then sensation in its difference from itself, i.e. the particular kinds of it. Here, then, we again have a progress from the universal to the particular. The third member, however, is the immediate subjectivity of spirit, i.e. the totality [p. I.85] or summary unity of all sensations. With dreaming, similarly, there is talk first of the universal form of dreaming, then of its particular modifications; the third member is the symbolism of the dream, or its relation to objective reality. We see, then, that the third member of the dialectical method need not necessarily be the negative unity of the two preceding ones; it may also on one occasion be the merely summary unity of the many set up in the second member, on another something else at will. Self-feeling is first healthy, then sick; here the second member is not the particular, but the negation of the first. The third is the restoration of the sick to health. A unity of those two, admittedly, in a certain sense, in whose place, however, the transition of the healthy into the sick could stand with equal right. —

*Rosenkranz* seems to set up a genuinely dialectical trichotomy when, after having depicted the immediate unity of spirit and body in the abiding and changeable determinacies together with sensation, he now passes over to the struggle of spirit with its corporeity, and finds this in *the dreaming soul*, *self-feeling* and *habit*. For self-feeling is the negation of the dreaming soul, while habit is the negation of self-feeling, the unity and truth of those two. Instead of many, we will choose this one trichotomy as an example, because it is one of the best, and look more closely at the dialectic that lies in it.

The disunion of the mental and the bodily, so he assures us, p. 106, *must* be the next moment. This disunion, to be sure, we had expected to find at the second main stage of spirit, consciousness; but the author still needs several mediating stages, intermediate negations, in order to accommodate his material properly. It also seems to us that sensation, already discussed at the earlier stage, could with good right demand to be taken for a disunion of spirit and its corporeity, since in it a considerable difference, e.g. between the sensation of color and the optic nerve, is unmistakable. Indeed, [p. I.86] *Michelet* no longer assigns it to the soul, but to theoretical spirit; but our author has not been pleased to pay heed to things of this sort. What, then, is that disunion, that struggle of the mental and the bodily? It is, first, the life of dreams, or the dreaming soul. Strangely enough, the dream is, on p. 106, the unity of sleep and waking, but on p. 109 the mixing of both states. That would be a naive interpretation of dialectical unities which we could put up with. *Erdmann* declares it, §. 29, to be an “apparent” identity of those two. Many such *apparent* unities we believe we see in the absolute science; that it should itself admit them — this suicide was, admittedly, not to be expected. In return, however, dreams, universal as they are, count with *Erdmann* only as “contingent appearances,” a counterpart to *Hegel’s* impotence of nature to hold fast the concept. Now why is the life of dreams a disunion of body and spirit, and how does precisely this develop, as the first stage, out of what immediately precedes it, immediate subjectivity? The answer is on p. 106: “spirit, as that which is distinguished in itself from its corporeity, is now: 1. the dreaming soul.” A grounding, a development, is nowhere to be found. And what is this dreaming soul? In it “the subjectivity of spirit is dissolved into an indeterminate objectivity, and through such dissolution the essence of objectivity, which is itself to constitute the opposite of subjectivity, is destroyed;” the sense is: the subject ceases to know or to feel itself, to have itself as a unity, and instead of this, objects which are not real, or not actually present, are taken to be real or present. Where in this concept is there anything of the body, of the struggle between corporeity and mentality? The kinds of the life of dreams are: the simple dream, dream-waking, and sleep-waking. The latter we will pass over for now; they lie in the dark, where everyone can see what he pleases. A better-known object are dreams proper; does that concept of the life of dreams fit them? Many, no doubt, but many not. We sometimes dream, even if only for a short time, with the clear consciousness of ourselves [p. I.87] and of many of our circumstances; here, then, subjectivity is not lost. If in doing so we do not present to ourselves all our circumstances, e.g. that we are now dreaming, the same holds in waking too. Further, we not infrequently dream without taking any apparent objectivity for real, as e.g. when we compose poetry in a dream, so that after awakening we are able to recite the poem. Further, the dream has yet another characteristic, which our author does not forget, namely that it takes place only during sleep; how does he arrive at this characteristic, starting from the concept of the life of dreams? Without doubt not at all, except with the help of experience, by taking from outside. Surreptitiously obtained, then, is the first member of the trichotomy, because it is set up without any grounding at all; surreptitiously obtained too is the meaning given to it for the objects of experience. — This first member must now pass over into the second, into its negation. Why? Because the soul is spirit, but spirit is a self that is in itself for itself; because according to its concept it is self-conscious, and it therefore involves a contradiction that it should lose its subjectivity to objectivity. We will not ask again how this contradiction, this impossibility, could after all befall it; but this much is clear: we are told that the life of dreams must pass over into its negation, because otherwise we should not arrive at thinking spirit; and in saying so one forgets that the necessity of arriving at the latter follows only from the proven necessity of all the earlier stages of development that are supposed to lead to it. But if appeal is made to the development already given of the three main moments of spirit, as soul, consciousness and spirit proper, then we appeal to what we objected above against this development. But let us grant that the life of dreams must pass over into its negation; *which* negation is meant by this? The life of dreams consists in this, that subjectivity loses itself, while an objectivity remains; yet not a real one, but an apparent one. Are we now to say that the subject, turning over into the negation of this state, loses itself in objectivity, but in a real one? or that objectivity loses itself and subjectivity remains, but an apparent one? or that the apparent objectivity [p. I.88] loses itself in subjectivity? or that neither of the two loses itself? All these things, and far more, form negations of the life of dreams, and we do not know into which of them it will pass over. But our author knows, for he knows that he is aiming at thinking spirit. So he tells us expressly that *self-consciousness* forms the negation of the life of dreams. With this, however, we may expect that spirit will now quickly pull itself together out of the life of dreams and stand there as self-conscious. Instead of this we read on p. 107: “being-for-itself, as *immediate*, is admittedly not yet to be taken as self-consciousness, but only as the *feeling* of oneself in one’s bodily and mental condition.” So the author still needs mediations, although we already have so many of them that self-consciousness, if it were to appear now, would have no cause to fear coming to light in indecent immediacy without an honorable vanguard. But where then would self-feeling, which is given by *experience*, find its place? *This*, therefore, must be the negation of the life of dreams. In it objectivity — of its *apparentness* there is no further talk — goes back again into subjectivity and becomes predicates of the subject. Is that the correct concept of self-feeling? In pure self-consciousness a man presents to himself not his predicates, which are not he, but only himself; just so, in self-feeling proper, he feels only himself. That is why the author, p. 143., declares it to be the unity which mediates within itself the opposition of objectivity and subjectivity. But this is not the definition given above, since there that opposition is not mediated, but only retained in another form, that of subject and predicate. Thus, then, by negating something or other in the concept of the life of dreams, dropping a superfluous characteristic, apparentness, and then twisting and altering whatever one pleased, one has arrived at the concept of self-feeling. Accordingly we see that the necessity of a negation of the first member of the trichotomy is not proven, and that the concept which is put forward as the second member has been obtained only through the most manifold arbitrariness. It must not be overlooked that, just as [p. I.89] self-feeling is not the only and necessary negation of the life of dreams, so conversely the life of dreams is not the only negation of self-feeling from which, as its opposite, the latter could proceed. The subject can lose itself not merely in predicates which present an apparent objectivity, but also in many others, as e.g. when a thinker becomes absorbed in his object and thereby forgets himself. It is therefore equally arbitrary to let self-feeling follow precisely upon the life of dreams, or to let the life of dreams precede precisely self-feeling. — The dialectical method finally obtains the third member of the trichotomy by arguing thus: because the concept of the life of dreams necessarily passes over into its negation, self-feeling, the life of dreams is properly self-feeling; for that very reason self-feeling is properly the life of dreams; what is there in truth is therefore the unity of both: *habit*. On this subject we have already spoken at length further above. That in one and the same state self-feeling should be present and at the same time lost, we shall never believe the author, and if similarity is sameness, and a cricket is a griffin, then he may call that state habit too. The arbitrariness here is so glaring that neither *Michelet* nor *Erdmann* was willing to follow him; instead they assign habit another position in the developmental series of the soul, in which, to be sure, they agree among themselves just as little as with *Rosenkranz*. So stands it with this genuinely dialectical trichotomy.

We have seen that *Rosenkranz*, in connection with the life of dreams, also discusses a dream-waking and a sleep-waking. The same states, and some others besides, ordered differently from those, are treated by *Michelet* under the name of the *magical life of the soul*, by *Erdmann* as the *natural double life of the individual*. We have already remarked earlier that *Hegel* lays weight on animal magnetism as on a phenomenon through which psychological research has received an important impulse; *Michelet* says, p. 48: “the Hegelian categories of reason, which go beyond the Kantian categories of the understanding, have, together with animal magnetism, simultaneously in science and [p. I.90] in deed freed spirit from the bonds in which the understanding had confined it.” Likewise it was remarked above that our authors believe that, by virtue of their dialectical method, they have completely penetrated into the mysteries which, for other thinkers, still lie in the states where spirit and body act together. The way in which they treat these is highly characteristic of the whole spirit of their works and of the dialectical method in particular; we will therefore describe it. In doing so we follow *Michelet* first.

He links on to the concepts of sleep and waking. The aim of this book, so we read, pp. 161 ff., is that spirit should become master over corporeity and natural life. To that end it must oppose itself to the latter and enter into struggle with it. The sharpest opposition of spiritual and natural life is the relation of waking and sleeping. What comes first is sleep; in it there sets in for man the unconsciousness of his natural life, a dull weaving of spirit, where all differences are thrown through and into one another in the tangle of natural life. In waking, his particularity raises itself into universal spiritual consciousness. Both sides are justified; since neither is to be displaced at the expense of the other, but only one is to be subordinated to the other, they strive to combine with and to interpenetrate each other. This happens first in dreaming. — One sees that the dialectical method here forgets its regulation soldier’s march; it is sure of reaching its goal, and so it saunters toward it at all its ease. Of a strict proof of the necessity of sleeping and waking and of their periodicity, nothing more is lacking than everything. Could our authors give it, the whole learned world would sacrifice a hecatomb this very day, and a new epoch of the sciences would begin. Let us hear further. — The spiritual life of waking, namely, continues on into sleep, and forms the one part of the dream, the recollection of the day’s occupations. But unconscious natural life is the source of the activities of the soul, since spirit is the negation of nature; so in sleep too there must be produced in us an [p. I.91] infinite multitude of presentations. Thereby, then, there arises a *becoming-conscious of natural life*, which forms the other part of the dream. Thus spiritual and natural life combine and intersect in the dream. — Here the author has again been pleased to take the dialectical proceeding-forth for a genetic one; for the becoming-conscious of natural life, so he thinks, proceeds from the latter as from its cause, and thus dreams in fact arise. But the *consciousness* of natural life is surely not natural life itself, but its opposite; if, then, in the dream spiritual life and the becoming-conscious of natural life combine, this yields no combination of spiritual life and natural life, but of spiritual life and spiritual life; and that was not the concept of the dream. — The incipient linking of natural and spiritual consciousness which we saw in the dream, so *Michelet* continues, has now to develop further. In order that both sides may enter into true struggle and opposition, the dreaming soul must continue itself into the state of waking consciousness as well, and challenge it on its own ground; only so can the victory of spirit over corporeity, which is our aim, be won. This victory is possible only through the complete identification of natural and spiritual life. — How so? In order that a bull may fight with a dog, must the dog first become a bull, and the bull a dog? — Now since, it is said further, spiritual life is personal conscious life, the next step toward this identification is that natural life too should become a conscious, personal one, then set itself as person over against person, and finally that the soul should become master of itself by both personalities flowing together into one. — This is by no means a joke, but real, most peculiar earnest. We are quoting the author’s words throughout, and no doubt can arise about their sense. Out of nature, and more precisely, first of all out of corporeity, a soul has resulted as its opposite; it has gradually negated itself onward to waking, conscious, spiritual personality, which, however, has yet to become wholly free of corporeity. In order that this [p. I.92] may happen, there results from corporeity, while it sleeps, a second soul, which likewise negates itself onward to personality; then the two slip into each other, and thereby the first soul is freed from its corporeity and becomes, in due form, thinking spirit! It would be interesting to know whether corporeity hatches a new little soul every night, perhaps in every afternoon nap as well; but on this science is silent. — *Michelet* continues: if, then, the waking state is really to be conscious totality, i.e. thinking spirit, it must not exclude natural life, dream-consciousness, from itself. It must allow it influence upon itself; it appears as an external power which establishes itself within waking consciousness and comes to consciousness at first only in single flashes, but afterwards ever more pervasively. Thus, *first*, there is present in the single individual a double life and a double consciousness, the *divining soul*. It in turn develops itself through several stages. First, my whole future and past life tears, at single points, the veil with which it is hidden from us; thereby we see a piece of our real, actual life, which has its source in natural life, before it has emerged from this source; this is *vision*, which itself has several kinds, e.g. presentiment proper, complete vision, second sight, etc. As I leap over the conditions of time and space for the occurrences of my own subject or of others related to me, and conjure up what is not yet or no longer objective into the presence of my presentation or intuition: so I can further extend this connection of my subject to its relation to the whole of nature, and sense the existing world without the mediation of space and time. This is the *sensing of metal* and *water*, *homesickness*, and the like. Thus, finally, the conscious relation to universal natural life makes itself into the sole life, embracing our whole personality, and thereby itself becomes a personality; this is *clairvoyance*, though not yet the magnetic kind. In this way natural life has become so much the dominant one over spiritual life that the latter seems to be wholly extinguished. With the submerging of this [p. I.93] personality, the consciousness of natural life emerges, as the only personality that has remained, within the waking state itself. This means nothing other than that the activity of the brain disappears entirely, and consciousness sinks down into the region of the stomach and the pit of the stomach, or, as *Steffens* most ingeniously puts it: the sun of the brain sets, and the stars of the abdomen rise. — The sense of what has been said is that the consciousness of natural life is the sum of those sensations which are produced immediately by the bodily states. Now the body is in connection with the whole of external nature, its past and its future; therefore we are able to sense what is near and what is far, what is past and what is to come, or, according to circumstances, also to intuit it. How simple this explanation is, to be sure! But why is the author shy of speaking here already of two souls in one body? Consistently, after all, that must be what is meant, and a philosopher ought not to be afraid of the word. But he immediately becomes bolder, as he advances from the divining soul to the second stage. — Through the fact, he continues, that natural life has hereby raised itself to a conscious personality, it has *secondly* become equal to the spiritual life, and confronts it, as it were, as its double. Thus we have the highest opposition of natural and spiritual life as the *relation of two personalities related to each other*. The individual is thus divided into two personalities, which also become external to each other and stand opposed to each other as independent ones. — But how is natural life, having become conscious personality, still natural life, since personality and consciousness form the sharpest opposition to nature? How is the opposition of two personalities an opposition of natural and spiritual life? And how is it the highest opposition, after sleep and waking were declared to be that shortly before? — The author continues: this standpoint is on the one hand a liberation from the dependence in which waking consciousness was entirely depressed; yet on the other hand, with the disappearance of dependence on nature, there is present dependence on a foreign person, in that it is not merely abandoned to universal natural life, [p. I.94] but this life has concentrated itself in the arbitrary will of a foreign person. We have here the rapport of two persons, of whom the one is the spiritual consciousness of the other, while the latter has its natural being in the former. — Here, then, we have the two souls in the best form. But the one has now emigrated from the body in which it formerly resulted together with the other, and has nestled itself into another one. How it does this we did not, to be sure, see; but we rightly asked repeatedly above *why, then,* a determinate subjective spirit, as the negation of a determinate body, should be bound precisely to this body; so it can indeed also be somewhere else, as we now clearly see. — This relation, we read further, is now at first so one-sided that the one person, who has not yet come to consciousness at all, as a person merely in itself, has its whole being, physical and spiritual, in the life of another, complete person: the relation of the embryo to the mother. — This relation fits what, according to the above, it was supposed to be, like — to speak with the proverb — a fist in the eye. Where, then, is the second conscious personality here? Where is the separateness of the spiritual and the natural conscious life of one and the same individual? Further, if this state is a necessary moment of development for an existing spiritual life, so that it may come to freedom from corporeity, to thinking, how then do men, barren women, etc., arrive at their thinking? — Secondly, says *Michelet*, the inequality of the persons has disappeared insofar as each feels its being and the roots of its life in the other, but still with the difference that in the one person waking consciousness predominates, in the other dreaming consciousness; that is *animal magnetism*. Its true concept is: when natural life has made itself master of the waking state and of its free spiritual consciousness to such a degree that it has entirely subjected this to itself, then man has by that very fact ceased to be his own master. This depression is the cessation of the fluidity of life. Just as in every illness generally the organs do not mesh with one another in their functions, but each leads a particular life for itself, [p. I.95] so here, in the magical life of the soul, natural and spiritual life fall apart and are foreign to each other. This foreignness brings with it that such a person can fall under the power of another. The magnetized individual feels a part of its self in identity with another person. Both souls are in substantial unity. Bodies exclude each other; but because the soul is immaterial, spatial asunderness is not present for it at all; the one soul is thus, as we may express it, posited in the space of the other. This is the so-called magnetic rapport. Both persons have their natural life in one, only still with the difference that the magnetizing person has at the same time brought the substantial unity of both souls under the sway of its waking consciousness, whereas that of the magnetized person seems as if extinguished. — Here too, then, spiritual life is entirely suppressed. But this state was earlier designated as unmagnetic clairvoyance; in magnetism, on the other hand, we were promised that both lives, the spiritual and the conscious natural life of the same individual, would stand opposed to each other as two independent personalities. — Finally, we hear further, the opposition of the two personalities is overcome to the point that natural life, which the person in the waking state distinguishes from itself as a second personality, is no longer a real but an imagined personality, which thus rises up in the interior of the individual and passes itself off as his better self, as the guardian spirit and genius of this man. For, since natural life in this depression of free spirit has brought waking consciousness under itself, it is also declared to be the higher, better I. An instinct, a blind feeling then drives us to action; it is the appearance of universal rationality in anthropological form, it is the true meaning of what we conceive in presentation as a guidance by divine providence. In the ethical sphere it is, according to *Erdmann*, conscience. — The second consciousness, the second soul, which had earlier emigrated, has now come home again. How can it come about at all that the consciousness of natural life [p. I.96] gets up to so much crazy nonsense, while spiritual life is entirely rational? After all, they are both siblings of the same mother, corporeality, born under the same conditions; the one is only somewhat younger than the other. The voice of conscience, the tender feeling for the good, the true and the beautiful, which in men of finer mind is apt to run ahead of clear judgment — these are, as we learn here, the immediate products of corporeality, of natural life. — The third standpoint of the magical life of the soul, its summit, is that these two personalities interpenetrate each other again and want to knead themselves together into one: *derangement*. Here the individual can no longer keep the two personalities apart; both cross each other, but without yet being able truly to grow into a single one. That both personalities touch each other so immediately, and the one cannot let go of the other, without both nevertheless coalescing into a single one, is precisely what produces in the mad this rage and this staring manner. — As if every madman, i.e. here: everyone sick in soul, showed rage and a staring manner! We have here roughly the same definition of the diseases of the soul that we found in *Rosenkranz*, and which does not fit the imbecility cited later at all, and fits many other kinds only very badly. In passing we recall that *Rosenkranz* and *Erdmann* present the diseases of the soul as particularizations of the self-feeling, yet as such by which spirit “falls down” in its necessary developments. From this we learn that in the dialectical series of development a later moment can by chance also be a lower one than its earlier ones. — With derangement the trichotomy of the magical life of the soul has reached its last member, and the struggle of the soul with corporeality its end. With it, *Michelet* continues, natural life has made itself ever more like the waking state, and has finally become a personality with equal rights. — So then, we add, derangement is a higher spiritual state than the voice of conscience, the feeling of what is right. — In this spiritual kneading-through of the two, it goes on, natural life sublates its last inadequacy to the spiritual, and they turn over into a higher [p. I.97] sphere, the mediated unity of spirit and body. Here natural being is penetrated by the spiritual; it becomes its home, its property; this is the *actuality of the soul*. It has a trichotomy: *habit* as the *becoming* actuality of the soul, *physiognomy* as the actuality that *has become*, and finally *pantomime*. In *Rosenkranz* the last trichotomy is called: mimic, physiognomic and craniological expression; *Erdmann* closes with the doctrine of habit. Here again we have wholly “innocent” trichotomies; with them the doctrine of the soul, as the first main part of the doctrine of subjective spirit, comes to an end.

Whichever of our readers has followed us this far, him we call happy. For him there is no longer any secret in the life of the soul; all riddles are solved; his own feeling must tell him that not a shadow of doubt or uncertainty has remained, for he has absolute knowledge of it. For him too spirit has been freed, through the deed of animal magnetism and of the *Hegelian* categories of reason, from the bonds in which the understanding had hitherto constrained it. He now sees why the soul is nothing but the proximate negation of the body; why it is not a substantial being in the plain sense of the understanding of this word, but a sum of sensations, presentations, etc., forever coming to be and passing away; why each of these states is what it is, but also everything else that it is not; that the soul at first sticks to the brain, but can also sink down into the stomach; that it is one, but can also have a second beside itself; that it inhabits the body, but also emigrates and returns home again; he sees how much of this knowledge psychology owes to animal magnetism. And all this he knows through the power of the dialectical method, which has not only raised those objects to the fullest transparency and comprehensibility, but at the same time, through the course of an iron necessity which it has kept to free of all arbitrariness, has most brilliantly proved itself to be an irrefutable truth. We, for our part, were tempted while reading through the section described above to pinch ourselves in the arm in order to test whether we were [p. I.98] really awake. Then there came over us something like a dread of strange, uncanny things; for we doubted whether the authors really had arms and legs and a face like other people, since they thought and spoke so entirely differently; until we finally came to the conviction that what we had read was, in content and method — an unheard-of drivel.

After all that we have already demonstrated against the dialectical method, we may be permitted to be brief about the trichotomies into which spirit falls apart at its second and third main stages. The “innocent trichotomies” continue: e.g. *describing*, *observing* and *experiencing* as the moments of perceiving consciousness; the *desiring subject*, the *desired object* and the *enjoyment of the object* as the would-be moments of subjectivity, and the like. The dialectical progress from the universal to the particular gradually turns into one from the particular to the universal; for it is frequently given as the motive of the advance that thinking spirit, which is the universal and totality, must come to light. Instead of trichotomies we find in *Rosenkranz*, in the last section, a series of dichotomies; thus, besides theoretical and practical spirit, lusting and desiring, positive and negative desire, propensity and inclination, affect and passion, and others, all of which lack the third dialectical member. *Amphora coepit institui… cur urceus exit?* [A wine jar was begun … why does a pitcher come out? — Horace, *Ars poetica* 21–22] Sometimes the dialectic seems to want to keep to the genesis, as in the trichotomy: the *image*, *forgetting*, *presentation*; at another time it runs flatly counter to it, as when *passion* is listed as the negation of the *drive for knowledge*.

Of *freedom* we have already spoken. Its concept was formerly, and again in most recent times, discussed so variously that a correct historical understanding of what is at issue in it no longer presents any difficulty. But it is so important that we cannot refrain from considering more closely the way in which our authors treat it. This may at the same time be the last example of an application of the dialectical method. We [p. I.99] keep here to *Erdmann’s* presentation, because it is the most detailed.

After subjective spirit has developed itself up to thinking, it has, according to § 124 ff., become the capacity to transform itself, its thinking, reason, independently into an objectivity; it is practical spirit, will. Since, further, it knows objectivity as its own determinateness, it must at the same time know it as a contradiction not to exist objectively as well; the contradiction in theoretical spirit leads necessarily to practical spirit. — That the transition is purely arbitrary in any case has already been shown. But it is just as arbitrary to call this capacity of subjective spirit to transform itself into an objectivity will. The will is not mere thinking; the thinking of a murder is still worlds apart from the willing of it. Further, if a willing is to transform itself into objectivity, there is needed first the body, and then manifold external things and favorable circumstances; only under these presuppositions is it that capacity. Or does the distinction which criminal lawyers make between intention, attempt and deed count for nothing with the author? But even in the accomplished deed the will has not become objectivity; it remains where it was, in the mind of the man, and only its effects, mediated by the body, stand there as external objects. This holds, duly modified, even of the body, as well as of all other mental states. No book, be it a poet’s work or a code of law, is spirit or has spirit, in the proper sense of these words. It is paper and ink; the spirit resides in those who wrote it, or who read and understand it. The like takes place with all social institutions and the like. These are again trivialities; but whoever speaks of an objective spirit, as our authors do, might have cause to take them into consideration. Further, just as a will can be present without the capacity to transform the subjective into objectivity, so conversely the latter can turn up without the former, if one leaves this “transforming into objectivity” so indeterminate. For sensations and other mental states which are not a willing also act outward through facial expressions, involuntary movements [p. I.100] and the like. Finally, and this is to be stressed especially, it is entirely wrong that the will always results from reason. Alas! it only too often has nothing in common with it. Supposing, then, that the transition from theoretical spirit to that capacity of transforming thinking into an objectivity were not what it is, a child of arbitrariness, we still would not thereby have the concept of the will; this concept is taken in from outside, and badly enough, surreptitiously obtained, and with it the whole doctrine of practical spirit. — In § 127 we read: “the concept of the will just stated indicates the path which its development must take. As intelligence had to *become* what it was, so the development of the will consists in its *becoming* the realization of reason, i.e. the rational free will. Before it has become this, it appears as unfree; a contradiction analogous to the one which drove spirit beyond naturalness.” — The will, then, is according to its concept rational will, for it is reason itself in its capacity to transform itself into an objectivity. And of this same reason it is now asserted that it is unreason which transforms itself into objectivity, just as spirit, which according to its concept is thinking, is at first thoughtlessness. With exactly the same scientific necessity a conjurer, stepping before his audience and holding out his open, empty hand, would say: Here is a ring, but, gentlemen, you see, this ring is really a bird. The concept of the unfree will is not “surreptitiously obtained”; that would be put too finely, and therefore incorrectly. We only mean to say that the author needs the unfree will in order to accommodate desire, inclination, passion and other states given by experience, and therefore he assumes it without ceremony. — The concept of the unfree will, we read further, is the concept of the *determined* will. — But the author himself, immediately afterward, opposes the undetermined will to the free will; so this too is an unfree will: why does he now assert that the unfree will is precisely the determined one? Because it pleases him. This is the dialectical necessity which brings him to the determined will as the first moment of development of the will.

[p. I.101] The second moment is then the *undetermined* will. Before it comes to this, however, the determined will must still pass through a series of intermediate developments. If among these practical feeling, respect and disrespect and the like also occur, we see how far the author is from understanding the nature of the will even in the empirical manner of conception. The last intermediate development of the determined will is passion. In it, according to § 155, one determination has pushed itself forward so far that all the others are posited negatively and cannot be realized; the will has entirely vanished into a *must*. With this the contradiction that lay in the determined will, namely to be rational and yet determined, is driven to its highest point and must turn over into its opposite. — The concept of passion here set up is false. The *Hegelian* school prides itself on having rejected the splitting of spirit into independent faculties. Theoretical spirit is for it not a faculty of thinking but the sum of actual thoughts; practical spirit or will not a faculty of willing but the sum of individual willing. But no passion is able to take the entire willing of a man captive in such a way that, besides the willing of the passionately desired object, there would not also be a very numerous other willing. He whom the craving for fame rules still wants, after all, to eat and drink and a great many things besides. It is false that in passion being determined reaches its extreme point; for it has no degrees. Determination of the will is its necessity, as an effect of sufficient causes; so the well-known usage of language will have it. What is not wholly necessary is not necessary at all, and more than a whole necessity is not thinkable. Therefore the most passionate willing, if determined at all, is determined to exactly the same degree as the most dispassionate. Arbitrary, then, is once again the transition from the first main moment into the second, insofar as it is made by means of the concept of passion; it could just as well be made from practical feeling, as the first intermediate development of the determined will.

[p. I.102] And why must passion pass over into its opposite? Because it contains a contradiction, in that the will vanishes entirely into a must. But this contradiction one has wantonly made oneself, by first letting the will result from reason and then nevertheless, quite arbitrarily, speaking of a determined will. But we must ask further whether, then, the concept of a rational will is incompatible with determination. The defenders of determinism, as is well known, maintain that determined willing can very well also be rational. How does the author refute them and demonstrate the contradiction? By a play on words! The will, so he says, is according to its concept rational, but at first it is irrational; a rational will which is irrational is a contradiction. The rational will I call the free one, but the irrational one the determined, unfree one; therefore the rational and the determined will are opposed to each other. But is it a question here of names, then, or of concepts? The author can *call* the rational will a free one too; that it is this, in contrast to the unfreedom of the determined will, we shall believe when he has shown that the rational will is without cause. From an important scientific dispute carried on for centuries it is not permissible merely to take over the catchwords, the names, while leaving the concepts untouched, and, after having played with the former for some time like a child, to suppose that one has decided the dispute over freedom and determinism.

Why, further, must the determined will pass over precisely into the undetermined one? We read in § 155: “In passion the shape is already contained into which the will has to go forth. For in passion there is posited, first, a negative relation to all determinations; secondly, that the will must remain as the possibility of realizing all its determinations. Taking both together, then, the will is to be conceived as the negative relation to the determinations of the will, which as such is the possibility of realizing them all. But thus the will is undetermined will.” — What is one to say to this? [p. I.103] Every sentence is an untruth. In passion there is *not* posited a negative relation to *all* determinations, but to all *with the exception of one*, which has pushed itself forward; so it says in the same § a few lines earlier. Very well: now, with this determination, let the author try to pass over to the undetermined will, or let him not believe he can catch his readers with so clumsy a ruse! Further, it does *not* lie in passion that the will must *remain*, if what the author said in the very same § is true, that in passion the will has vanished and is no longer will. It could at most arise again, but not remain. It is false that the negative relation to the determinations of the will is the *possibility* of realizing them all; for it was just said that it is the *impossibility* of realizing them. All this is found in the passage of the same § cited above. At the same time we have received a new proof that the author has only the word, not the concept, of the determined will. For the determinations of the will that are supposed to be concentrated in passion, and the others which cannot be realized in it, plainly designate not the dependence of willing on causes but its direction toward determinate objects; two concepts which differ from each other in precisely everything that is at issue in determinism. Determination is taken here, as in *Hegel* generally, in the logical sense, as the particularization of the universal, and so the determined will is nothing but a will in its direction toward an object, i.e. a determinate will, not the will in general. This, however, is not what is otherwise understood by the determinism of the will. Just as little, though, is the undetermined will simply the possibility of realizing all determinations of the will; here too the author has merely picked up the word, and the concept has been left lying. Yet in § 156 he declares the undetermined will to be the negative abstract universality hovering above all the determinations of willing, and thus makes it perfectly clear that determination of the will is for him nothing but direction of the will, and that he has as little concept of indeterminism as of determinism. This, then, is the manner in which, by virtue of the dialectical [p. I.104] method the second principal moment of the will, the undetermined will, is obtained.

The result, however, is that there is just as much a determined as an undetermined will. The latter now likewise develops further: it becomes *arbitrary will*, *resolution* and *decision*. But how, then, does an undetermined will come to a resolution and a decision? Very simply: as the abstract universality of all determinations it deliberates about them with itself; then it *concludes* the deliberation and is thus *resolution*; finally it *rids* itself of all other determinations, actually externalizes itself into externality, *unlocks* itself toward it, and this gives the *decision*. Herewith the riddles of indeterminism are solved, and we comprehend how a willing without a cause, a transcendentally free willing, is possible, nay necessary. This we learn verbatim from §. 158 and §. 159. §. 160 further instructs us that the undetermined will, because, in deciding, it can after all choose only from among all its determinations, demonstrates that it is really determined. So, then, without end the determined will turns over into the undetermined, and the latter back again into the former. — Here we could very easily console the undetermined will for the misfortune that has befallen it by pointing out to it that its determinations, from which it must choose, are precisely all possible ones, so that a restriction to them is no more to be feared than if one assigned to someone as his prison a place which he may pick out for himself anywhere in the universe. But let us hurry on. The turning over of the two wills into each other has its ground in the contradiction which each contains, in that, as is said in §. 160, the determined will *wills* nothing, the undetermined wills *Nothing*. This witticism shows conclusively the author’s ignorance with regard to the questions at issue. In determinism and indeterminism the question is whether the individual willing that actually dwells in us is necessary through sufficient causes, or without cause and therefore contingent. Supposing it were necessary, it would, as we have already said earlier, still remain what it is, a willing; supposing it were contingent, it would still remain a willing of [p. I.105] something, as surely as we are speaking of an actual willing. — The truth of that endless process of turning over is, according to §. 161, that the will is to be interpreted as undetermined in its determination, and determined in its being undetermined; thus it is truly self-determining, free will. Herewith the dialectical development of the free will is completed.

And what, now, is this free, rational will, the highest thing which, in our view, human power is capable of striving for? It has already been said: it is the self-determining will, which, however, mark well, means the self-determinating will, and this in turn means: the will that particularizes itself in its universality, in which all its differences are contained as sublated. Thus the free will is the same as thinking; they are spirit itself. Yet subjective spirit does not yet have the highest freedom, since only in absolute spirit are all possible differences, including those of subjective and objective spirit, sublated. And now let us recall our dialectical series of development from A to Z. There we found that every member contains within itself, as sublated, all the preceding ones, and, because the development goes round in a circle, every member contains all the others. Thus the organism contains chemism and mechanism, but with them also the concept, being and essence, infinite and finite spirit; in short: not merely spirit, but every moment of development in the dialectical series possesses freedom, or rather: is freedom. This is *Hegelian* freedom! — And this babble, this childish play on words with determinism, indeterminism and freedom, which is too thoughtless even to understand the questions at issue: it boasts of having solved these questions for all time! Nay, people dare to look down with a pitying smile on men who stand in the first ranks of the great thinkers. The poor fellows! they took so much trouble and did not notice that they were dealing with a “trick question,” and that the will is neither merely determined nor merely undetermined, but in truth both at once, or, what is the same, neither of the two! — Certainly, whoever ventured such a thing in any other science would for ever be branded with the mark of intellectual incapacity and shameless presumption; [p. I.106] only in philosophy, poor mistreated philosophy, is it held to be permissible and honorable.

—

And herewith enough of particulars. We now wish to gather together the features of the works before us which have so far been indicated piecemeal, and to combine them into one picture.

The dialectical development proceeds from the concept A to Not-A, from there to the unity of both, B, and so on. This could very well go on further in a straight line without end; but then knowledge would not be absolute; the circle is absolute, it closes together with itself. But what new concepts, then, can be obtained through that method, whether it goes straight ahead or round in a circle? From A we obtain B, i.e. the unity of A and Not-A. From B we obtain C; and what is this? It is the unity of B and Not-B, i.e. C is the unity *of* the unity of A and Not-A *and* of the non-unity of A and Not-A. We come to D. It is the unity of C and Not-C; i.e. D is the unity *of* the unity of the unity of A and Not-A and of the non-unity of A and Not-A, *and* of the non-unity of the unity of A and Not-A and of the non-unity of A and Not-A. We need go no further; it is clear that on this path no concept is to be found which would consist of anything other than the multiplications and combinations of A, of “unity” and of “not.” The dialectical method is wholly incapable of developing really new concepts by itself, whether it takes sublation as simple negation or, for greater convenience, also as preservation; above, too, A is preserved in C and D. If it nevertheless wishes to get further, nothing remains for it to do but continually to take concepts which it has not itself produced from elsewhere, i.e. from outside, and to pass them off as its own products. For our authors this outside is partly experience, from which, as we have seen, they take the concepts of age, sex, etc., partly, and principally, other writings. That is why they are most detailed in that part where ethnographies, physiologies [p. I.107] and so on supply them with most material, in the doctrine of the soul. In the other parts, namely those on theoretical and practical spirit, they must content themselves with citing a pack of faculties of the soul that have become stereotyped and, at most, plundering Spinoza on the side. This *taking up of concepts from outside while passing them off as self-produced* is the *first* characteristic feature of the works before us.

But in order to be able to pass off the concepts taken from outside as self-produced, one must fit them into the dialectical course of development. This is accomplished by two means. The first is that one does not take the dialectic, its negations, unities and trichotomies, strictly. This permits many variations. One lets Not-A be the negation of A, to be sure, but all sorts of other things besides. Thus spirit is indeed a within-one-another, hence the negation of nature, of asunderness; but on the side it is also thinking. Or Not-A is a negation which is not yet one, an intermediate negation. Thus the soul is the negation of nature, yet not yet the proper one, which only spirit is. Or it is a partial negation, like the changeable natural determinacies alongside the permanent ones. Or it is merely another concept altogether, like sex alongside age. Or it stands to A in the relation of the particular to the universal, as is mostly the case in the first part, or else in the relation of the universal to the particular, as is the case in the second and third parts. Or the later moment is for once also a lower one — spirit “falls down” — whereas it ought really to be a higher one, as with the diseases of the soul, and so on. Further, B need not always be the strict unity of A and Not-A. It is occasionally also merely the mixing of the two, like the dream; or their sum, like subjectivity; or some other combination of them, like the disposition, which is taken as self-consciousness *of* feeling, not as self-consciousness *and* feeling; or it is any third concept you please, like the symbolism of the dream, or the restoration of diseased self-consciousness; or one leaves out the third member altogether and [p. I.108] contents oneself with two, as with theoretical and practical spirit, and so on. If, finally, all this does not help to gain at least the semblance of a dialectical development, then one gives instead a superficial arrangement of a certain material in three members, and declares it to be an “innocent trichotomy.” *Arbitrariness in handling the method adopted as the only correct one* is the *second* characteristic feature.

The other means of fitting the concepts taken from outside into the dialectical course consists in disfiguring these concepts themselves at will. Often, as we have seen, only the names still remain and let one guess what the authors are speaking of, while the concepts are distorted to the point of absurdity. Where experience does not suit the trichotomies, a remedy is found; the five senses, the four temperaments are transformed with ease into three. The *disfigurement of the concepts of experience to the point of unrecognizability* is the *third* feature. One comprehends that through the application of these two means experience *must* fit into the dialectic. But one also comprehends that through this procedure the authors have neither made the actual states of spirit comprehensible nor proved their method.

If one applies a perverse method in a most violent manner; if one deliberately makes all concepts play into one another and blurs them, instead of holding them fast in their differences and determinacies — which is precisely what thinking consists in — and pursuing the relations that arise from these; if, because a fantastic head can in the end really think whatever it pleases, one takes this faculty for lofty power and this pleasure for the law of the actual, but despises experience; if one does this, and does it for long: then the sense for conscientious inquiry, the earnestness of disposition which alone is able to sustain a genuinely scientific thinking, may well gradually vanish altogether; then one can also sink to the level of drivel. A *loose play with empty concepts*, [p. I.109] *which here and there even becomes drivel*, is *the most pervasive characteristic trait of the works before us*.
