Or is it not drivel when one asserts that water and fire outside us are perceived through the water and fire in us; or that tone is filled time? when one calls it an ingenious explanation that sleepwalkers are held fast on roofs by the attraction of the moon? when one acknowledges it as a sign of quiet strength that German peasants wear their blue or green coats lined with red? when one holds it proven that among all the heavenly bodies only on the earth do ensouled beings live, or that the sleeper always assumes the position of the fetus, and so on? — Whoever despises experience does not think it worth the trouble to know it either. This explains how we read here that summer and winter are the sun’s nearness and the sun’s remoteness; that solid bodies do not conduct sound as well as the air; that saliva is a specific water produced from the tongue; that the flesh is the organ of feeling; and similar things which, in anyone who is not an absolute philosopher, one would not hesitate to call gross ignorance.

Such is the character of the works before us. Were we to give them a motto, it would be the same one that *Dante* read above Hell: *Lasciate ogni speranza, voi ch’ entrate* [Abandon all hope, ye who enter]; in German: Here no psychology is to be found.

*Michelet’s* work displays the characteristic traits cited most conspicuously; drivel in particular, and occasionally also ignorance in matters very much worth knowing, come forward with naive ingenuousness and in broad outlines. *Rosenkranz* is more cautious in pursuing glaring views, milder and more level-headed. He would occupy one of the first places if dilettantism were admissible in philosophy. *Erdmann’s* book is a guide for lectures, short, concise and outwardly clear. A homespun industriousness prevails in it, which keeps inherited implements in tidy order for daily use, without entering much into technological investigations into their origin and worth.

[p. I.110] Psychology has taken a great step backward in the *Hegelian* school. Through *Wolff* it had become a dead scaffolding of faculties of the soul, without insight into their nature and the laws of their operation. *Kant* and his school had stopped there almost without moving. But people nevertheless sought indefatigably for better definitions and divisions of these faculties, and thereby acknowledged the task of correctly apprehending what is given in experience. People also searched for laws, not merely in reproductions, but by investigating the now friendly, now hostile interaction of the faculties. The *Hegelian* school, in the arrogance of its method, scorns experience and denies the laws: with that it has overthrown the foundations of its science.

It is a peculiarity of this school that, while it exploits the classifications of that older school, it takes no notice of the highly significant more recent achievements. We mean in particular *Herbart’s* achievements. This thinker, whom we must now also number among the departed, has, on a most simple foundation — the concept of the simplicity of the soul, and the proposition that simple sensations are under certain circumstances to be regarded as forces — built with the help of calculation a system of psychology which shows the origin of the most important mental states and their laws with a precision hitherto undreamt of. The original, independent, universal faculties of the soul, which blocked every path to knowledge, have become individual states of development whose origin and interaction lie clearly before our eyes. For the foundations general metaphysics supplies the proofs, but they can also be adopted as hypotheses, and then they accomplish for psychology what the vibration or emanation hypothesis accomplishes for insight into the phenomena of light. Psychology, though still in its becoming, seems thereby for the first time to have been raised to the rank of a science. However new the execution, the need for it is as old — and, we might say, the presentiment of it. In *Leibniz* the concept of presentations and of their striving plays a principal role. *Wolff* held that all states of the soul were only [p. I.111] modifications of presenting, and even declared in his Logic § 136 that the assumption of a force was a deceptive explanation of experience, one that explains nothing. *Lambert*, in his New Organon, Part I, p. 461, teaches that the weaker presentations are suppressed by the stronger; even the presentations of space he traces back to an inner drawing, hence to serial presentations, and in doing so appeals to experience. For *Herder*, in his Ideas etc., the faculties of the soul count very definitely as derived and individual ones for the individual states. *Kant*, in his Attempt to Introduce the Concept of Negative Magnitudes into Philosophy, asserts that every positive state of the soul can cease only through opposed states. He emphasizes in this connection how much effort it often costs to remove a desire, or, in abstracting, to keep certain presentations at a distance; but where the effort in the ceasing of a presentation is not noticeable, it might nevertheless well be present. Finally, how closely *Fries* agrees with *Herbart* in his thinking about psychology the latter has expressly acknowledged. What these men — witnesses for a view who, it is to be hoped, are not wholly to be rejected even in the eyes of the *Hegelians* — had divined, *Herbart* carried into effect. One may doubt this work, the result of a long life and of a mental power universally acknowledged to be unusually strong, one may reject it, but one cannot ignore it without dishonesty. The *Hegelian* school least of all may do this. For if a single page in *Herbart’s* psychological works is correct, the whole edifice of *Hegelian* psychology falls into ruins. That the *Herbartian* philosophy stands in general in the most decided contradiction to the *Hegelian*, that at present it alone in Germany possesses the inner vital force by virtue of which it stands, in closed power, as an equal opponent over against the latter: this has been stated and conceded even by friends of the *Hegelian* system. It is too late for ignoring. — *Michelet* prefaces his work with a history of psychology of 31 pages, in which even *Eschenmayer*, *Schubert* and *Steffens* are discussed; about *Herbart* not a little word. Only once later, on p. 283, where there is talk of the “people” who ascribe understanding to animals, [p. I.112] do we read: “*Herbart* even fears that, if dogs could speak (yes indeed: *if*!), they would soon outstrip man.” A worthy supplement to the author’s history of modern philosophy! *Herbart* never expressed that opinion, either explicitly or implicitly. *Erdmann* can be excused by the purpose of his book if he pays no particular attention to anyone besides *Hegel*. *Rosenkranz* mentions *Herbart* several times. He speaks modestly and admiringly of his profound mind, his most ingenious treatment, his utmost precision and elegance; but the points which must either be refuted or else refute his own book he leaves lying like a *noli me tangere* [touch-me-not], and quietly slips away. Moreover, the assertion put forward on p. XV, that *Herbart* declared the method of relations to be a universal method of the whole business of philosophy, is an error which is possible only with a very superficial knowledge of the works of the man who was his predecessor in *Kant’s* chair. But the weakness of many of *Hegel’s* pupils in apprehending the historical has proved itself so often and so strongly that blunders of the kind indicated hardly attract notice any more.

More than through the step backward indicated, the *Hegelian* psychology, nay the *Hegelian* philosophy in general, would do harm if it succeeded in putting, in many more heads, its arbitrary play with concepts, its contempt for and ignorance of experience, in place of exact observation and level-headed thinking. Already it bears a heavy part of the blame through which philosophy has sunk into disrepute with the mass of those who occupy themselves with the empirical sciences, and in part with the most distinguished among them. Recently we had to read words in this connection which one cannot accept with indifference. They refer in the first instance to that period of German philosophy which was founded by *Hegel’s* famous predecessor, who is now also to become his successor. After there has been talk of those philosophers who have no notion of genuine empirical knowledge but are all the prouder of empty speculations, it is said there: “These are the real and [p. I.113] true stupefiers of the age. I myself spent part of my years of study at a university where the greatest philosopher and metaphysician of the century carried the student youth away into admiration and imitation: who could then protect himself against infection? I too lived through this period, so rich in words and ideas, so poor in true knowledge and solid studies; it cost me two precious years of my life. I cannot describe the fright and the horror with which I awoke from this delirium to consciousness. How many of the most gifted and most talented did I see perish in this frenzy, how many laments over a completely misspent life have I not had to hear later!” And the conclusion runs: “Self-overestimation, arrogance, vanity and presumption, a lame ambition which bestows on itself in excess the recognition that the world must deny it: these proceed from the lecture halls of these men.” Thus speaks *Liebig*, a man whom Germany counts among its ornaments, whom no one will deny the mind to comprehend genuine philosophy of nature, and who had the opportunity to get to know the one of which he speaks. It was with pain that we read these words, for they contain a severe censure; with double pain, because the censure is for the most part just. And if we have them reprinted here, it is not done in order to revile our own colleagues: but a great harm is not healed by covering it up, but by first of all dragging it into the light. The *Hegelian* philosophy of nature is not a whit better than its predecessor, and the *Hegelian* philosophy of spirit likewise.

Can we hope to convince the friends of the *Hegelian* psychology by our counter-remarks? *Rosenkranz* relates on p. 99 that he had intended to carry out a psychical physiology in *Hegel’s* sense, according to which to every sensation there corresponds the affection of a particular organ, and, e.g., courage is felt in the breast, vexation in the liver, and so on. But he gave up the plan, he says, when he read *Müller’s* Physiology, which proves by facts that sensation, in those organs where the individual possesses a particular irritability, [p. I.114] stands out especially, and hence, in different individuals, in very different organs. To this *Erdmann* replies in § 57: “A psychical pathology such as *Hegel* demands is no more refuted by pathological instances than a monstrous birth refutes the fact that man has two eyes.” But surely it does refute the claim that he must have them! Now if a *Müller*, with *his* reasons, in such *palpable* matters, can achieve nothing against a zealous Hegelian, we withdraw in humility. Yet it seems to us that the number of these *Hegelians* at any price has after all gradually become very small; *Erdmann* himself is no longer to be counted among them now. We saw younger men fall away in droves. The content of the system had perished for them in the shipwreck; but while they rowed toward the shore with one arm, with the other, like *Camões*, they still held the dialectical method aloft to save it. When the distress grew, however, they threw this too into the waves. We saw older men, after holding out long, renounce it with quiet earnestness. Among those who remained faithful, strife broke out. Each one would know of only one man who rightly understood the master, and this one was himself. From within, people began to shake now this foundation, now that. In addition there came attempts to give the loose fabric of the system more firmness by weaving in a few new, stronger threads. *Bobrik* even sought to fuse *Herbart* and *Hegel*; *Chalybäus* found the salvation of philosophy in the same thought, and has recently begun to carry it out in his “Phenomenological Pages”; *Ulrici*, in his book on the principle and method of the *Hegelian* philosophy, which we have just received, and in whose first sections we find with pleasure manifold agreement with the views set forth here, makes a similar effort, so as not to lose being altogether over becoming. Individual men who still continue to hold the attention of the wider public fast to the system do so by pursuing practical tendencies or scientific directions which existed long without this system and are even now for the most part independent of it. Under such [p. I.115] circumstances the time seems to have come when level-headedness returns to the philosophical public. It will acknowledge *Hegel’s* mind, to whose power the greatness of its effects bears witness, but it will not be blind to the faults of his system. To facilitate orientation is the purpose of these pages.

[scan 126]

## Die Psychologie der Hegelschen Schule. Zweites Heft: Die Erwiderungen der Herren K. Rosenkranz und J. E. Erdmann. — The Psychology of the Hegelian School. Part Two: The Replies of Messrs. K. Rosenkranz and J. E. Erdmann.

examined by Dr. F. *Exner*, Full Professor of Philosophy at the Imperial and Royal University of Prague.

Leipzig, Friedrich Fleischer. 1844.

[scan 130] The little work “The Psychology of the Hegelian School, Leipzig, Fr. Fleischer, 1842” was not designed to receive a continuation; if it now receives one after all, I believe that in doing so I am fulfilling a duty toward science, and another toward myself.

I had attempted to examine the psychology of the Hegelian school in the works of its principal expositors *Rosenkranz*, *Michelet* and *Erdmann*, and from the result thus obtained to draw an inference back to the worth of the Hegelian philosophy in general, in accord with the statement: “The working-out of detail, the concrete unfolding into the particular, must best justify, or refute, the truth of the universal. Criticism is then only a consequence of the immanent transcendence.” (Psychology by *Rosenkranz*, 1st ed. p. VII, 2nd ed. p. IX.) According to what has been said about it, partly in literary journals, partly in independent philosophical works, that little work has attracted some attention and won approval from men who are named with respect even by their opponents. That it has even gained favor and produced conviction among the “great public,” that it has even produced “great jubilation” among many, I, being at a distance from the main centers of that traffic, learned only from its refutation by Prof. *Rosenkranz*. [p. II.2] He has, namely, added to the second edition of his Psychology, which has appeared since, an appendix in 24 sections: “Refutation of the Supposed Refutation of the Hegelian Psychology Given by Dr. *Exner*.” Soon afterward there appeared also from Prof. *Erdmann*, in the preface to the second edition of his Outline of Logic and Metaphysics, a reply, though a very short one; Prof. *Michelet* has so far, as far as I know, kept silent, and there is no reason to wait any longer for a reply. In any case a detailed defense of the system of psychology under attack now lies before us; it may be assumed that it has turned out as good as it could turn out, given its subject and the persons concerned. I believe I owe it to the public’s interest in the subject to bring what was begun, so far as it rests with me, to a conclusion as well, by examining whether that defense is fit to revise, or to confirm, the judgment passed earlier. The final conclusion must, to be sure, be brought by time; but time flows for the sciences only where thoughts are in motion.

I have, however, yet another, a personal interest in this continuation. My critique was severe and without indulgence. There is no need to demonstrate the dignity of philosophy to the Hegelian school, to it, for which the becoming of philosophy and the becoming of the Godhead are so often one. Elsewhere too there is no doubt among the discerning about the significance of our science, and the Germans have grown accustomed to regarding it as a national concern. If, accordingly, the Hegelian psychology really is as I was convinced it was, then my *serious word* in a *serious matter* was in its place, and the jest that occasionally flows in is explained by the nature of many objects, which would only make ridiculous anyone who chose to treat them otherwise. I have spoken as a *man* in an important affair. The “Refutation” of Prof. *Rosenkranz*, which almost exclusively comes into consideration here, is composed in such a tone that, were I now to reply in the same one, we should present to the public roughly the spectacle of two boys fighting each other with [p. II.3] word and fist in the street. Were he to attempt to carry this tone anywhere from his written speech into his spoken speech, he would no doubt exclude himself from educated society. But this does not concern me; it is his affair. Instead of combating my *views* with the rigor which I myself wanted and challenged, however, people have thought themselves entitled to bring accusations against my *person*. To this I believe I owe myself an answer. The question what the public could possibly have to do with personalities the accused may no longer put, however earnest it may sound to the accuser. It takes little courage to cast suspicion on the intentions of an “author completely unknown until then,” and yet he is forced to do in person what others can leave to others. If some shame should arise from this for my opponents here and there, they have at least only themselves to blame. I shall confine my defense to the smallest possible space and reserve the larger for purely scientific inquiry. Since, moreover, the spirit in my opponents’ refutations is after all the same one that produced their psychologies, its characterization is also not without immediate connection with our main question: the worth of the psychology of the Hegelian school.

### Persönliches. — Personal Matters.

What struck me most when I first looked over the titles of the 24 sections was the heading: “*The Moral Insinuation*.” Prof. R. begins this section on p. 426 with the words: “It is always sad that in philosophical disputes the reproach seldom fails to appear that the opponent is putting forward a doctrine pernicious to morality, even where he is not dealing with morality at all.” I too consider the bringing of such accusations sad; I consider it at the same time base. Prof. R. reports further: “Herr *Exner* does not know [p. II.4] what he is to say to the *monstrous* view: ‘The subject, as living and self-conscious, is said to have the certainty that no object could prevent its negation, i.e. that every desire of man must find its satisfaction. If this is true for the adherents of the *Hegelian* philosophy, we do not know whether we should extol them as the most blessed of men or lament them as the most wretched; for who can decide whether a man all of whose *desires*, *wise* or *foolish*, were satisfied would be the happiest or the most miserable?’” Upon this he continues: “I am perfectly able to decide that. In the Jesuitical sense in which Herr *Exner* asks here, he would be the *most miserable*. The how of this misery is set out sufficiently at the close of my Psychology. Herr *Exner’s* scientific crudeness and deliberate concealment are repugnant here. The *psychological* phenomenon by itself does not yet fall at all under the category of good and evil, of the wise and the foolish, which he here suddenly mixes in, whereas I constantly fight *against the mixing of psychology with morality*. Only the distinction of the *natural*, the *artificial* and the *unnatural* can enter psychology as an immediate anticipation of ethical determinations.

“Now, psychologically, does it not lie in the concept of desire to strive for its satisfaction?

“Will my opponent answer: No? —

“Can desire be satisfied? —

“Will my opponent answer: No? —

“Is not, from the standpoint of the natural will, the satisfaction of desire what is called happiness — as distinguished from the religious concept of blessedness?

“Will my opponent answer: No?

“But may every desire be satisfied under *all* circumstances?

“With this question, which I have decidedly answered in the negative several times, the *ethical* interest first enters, whose development, however, belongs to a sphere other than psychology. What good has all my talking done me, in particular also the exposition in the preface concerning this [p. II.5] point, if such consequences are drawn after all!”

Prof. R. had asserted, and still asserts, that every man as subject is the absolute power over the objects of the external world, and that he therefore has the certainty that every one of his desires, toward whatever object it may be directed, must overcome that object and find its satisfaction through it, i.e. *that every man obtains everything he desires*. No one will expect me to contest such an assertion with scientific seriousness, and just as I said on another occasion that the Hegelian school must surely have its very own animal world, because its animals are without desire, so my reply here runs: Whether the adherents of the Hegelian school are beings who obtain everything they wish for, I do not know; nor do I know, if they possess this miraculous gift, whether they are to be envied for it. This, I hope, is the perfectly clear sense of the passage quoted above by Prof. R. himself, and its continuation runs: “With regard to the *other* poor children of men, however, this assertion needs no refutation.” And to this, with reference to this point and similar points just discussed, the words again immediately follow: “Holding fast to erroneous speculations *in glaring opposition to experience* can at times awaken respect, because the power of thinking makes itself known thereby; but where the energy of thinking does not come forth unremittingly and unquestionably, where even frivolity leaves its unmistakable traces, there we turn away with indignation.” With this the discussion of this subject closes. Now let one compare Prof. R.’s reply, and one sees at once how he assumes that I had foisted on the Hegelians the assertion that *every desire may find its satisfaction*. I will not believe that Prof. R. merely feigned so gross a misunderstanding of the clear expression of my words in order to be able to make the reproach of a malicious insinuation; for if I believed it, I should have nothing further to answer. I do not believe it, although Prof. R. does not quote the continuation, just given, of the passage he quoted, [p. II.6] which would after all have made any misunderstanding impossible for the reader; I do not believe it, although Prof. R., ten pages earlier (p. 416), discusses the *very same passage*, there *grasps its sense correctly*, reproaches me: “He is indignant that the living should in itself have power over the object that stands over against it as its means,” and combats this objection of mine. But this one thing I do believe: that whoever makes against another the reproach of “scientific crudeness and deliberate concealment,” whoever brings forward this reproach in a tone with which he deeply wounds either the honor of the person attacked or his own, such a one must surely at least have read the passage on which he bases his reproach better than, say, a maidservant reads her novel, *half asleep*. —

Is this *conscientious polemic*?

Of the second moral insinuation I am supposed to be guilty through my assertion that the works examined teach the principle: *might is right*. Indeed, I hereby accuse my opponents of a doctrine pernicious to morality; according to Prof. R.’s own concept of moral insinuation, then, the only question is whether this happens on an occasion where they are “not dealing with morality at all.” It happens where Prof. R. teaches: “Usurpers must prove their entitlement to the power they have arrogated to themselves through struggle,” and likewise slaves, oppressed peoples, colonies, states that are newly constituting themselves; where Prof. *Michelet* teaches: “The coercion which slavery exercises is a right against the first, still more barbarous violence of homicide, and this right is also the right of the heroes to found states,” and the like. Such things do seem after all to belong in some measure to morality. Hence the question at most is still whether the principle indicated lies in those doctrines; if it did, one might surely also say what is true. Prof. R. denies it. He recalls in the text of his 2nd ed. that *Bachmann* already raised that objection and was combated by *Gabler*. I know I am not the first, nor yet the second, to point to that questionable passage in *Hegel’s* philosophy, and I find nothing in this by which the weight of the objection [p. II.7] would be diminished. *Gabler’s* rejoinder, however, which has no knowledge of Prof. R.’s Psychology, can be left aside where the question is whether that principle comes to light *in this Psychology*. In his defense its author first has the word “arrogated” in that sentence about usurpers printed anew in spaced letters. But even if he had it marked out in red ink, it would avail nothing; for the other words would always remain, that *usurpers* prove their *right* through *struggle*, which means nothing other than: where the power is, there is the right. After he has then quite needlessly remarked “that in psychology he has nothing at all to do with the relation of positive right to the right of reason,” he puts the question: “Is my assertion about usurpers perhaps refutable *historically*?” and closes with the pronouncement: “This much remains true after all, that until recently many princes did not doubt at all that, when they had made a conquest, the vanquished, whether they liked it or not, had at once to revere them as their legitimate lords.” Prof. R. thus asserts first: might is not right, as *Gabler* has shown; next he asserts: but might is right after all, for *historically* it has often *been taken for it*; and finally he challenges us to *refute* him *historically*, as if the concept of right were one given empirically. I confess that, despite so much that was extraordinary which had already come to light in the first part of my critique, I was once again so unskillful at this point as to be surprised; for I was not prepared for such a weakness of argumentation, which Prof. R. would hardly let pass in one of his students. As long as he will not have further clarified his concepts on so important a subject, or learned to present them more correctly, so long will that objection have to count, not indeed as a moral insinuation, but certainly as a well-founded reproach. For it should not be necessary to remark expressly that a moral insinuation strikes at the character, not merely at the scientific view; but that a man, despite great faults in his philosophical system, can nevertheless [p. II.8] have a spotless, respectable character, this much one ought at least to have learned from the history of philosophy. — These are the *moral insinuations* laid to my charge.

He whose feeling for supposedly impure imputations is so tender that it throws him into a blind zeal of defense may fairly be expected himself to disdain conducting a polemic with any but perfectly honest weapons. — In my critique I had presupposed, as something whose correctness goes without saying, that a good psychology must be in a position to render some help to pedagogy and to other sciences derived from it, and in the course of the work I had come back to this two or three times, remarking briefly how wholly incapable the psychological doctrines developed are of offering any support to pedagogical treatment. This procedure is now declared, in turns of phrase which one may read for oneself on pp. 393 and 394, to be highly absurd, to be “the worst manner of criticism,” and in order to make it comprehensible, it is pointed out, with a pitying shrug of the shoulders, that the author is a *professor in Prague, hence a Catholic*, and reference is made to “*Catholic asceticism*,” to “the confessional, its foremost temple.” What does Prof. R. aim at by this? Either nothing, or he wants to call to his aid confessional antipathies which he believes he may presuppose in his public, in order to present his opponent in the eyes of this public as an unfree man, who is from the outset incapable of and lost to science. And he does so on an occasion where right is surely so manifestly on his opponent’s side that one may assume that only despair at being able to set up nothing of consequence against it, and the feeling that a vital point was at stake here, misled him so badly; he does so where scientific reasons, and nothing but scientific reasons, had been offered to him, and where it had been expressly declared that one was deliberately choosing for examination a side of the Hegelian system “which lies further from the principal objects of the loud feuding.” In a similar way he alludes (p. 413) [p. II.9] to the political and social circumstances of the man he is combating. With whom does Prof. R. hope to find success by such means? With his fellow believers and fellow citizens? I believe I do them more honor, but also more justice, if I assume that they despise such goings-on. Earlier I already had occasion to regret that the absolute philosophy is not ashamed at times to render midwife’s services even to the grossest superstition; now we may hope to live to see an absolute philosopher call up the mob, in order to march through the country at its head with the No-Popery cry and occasionally, no doubt, with the once popular Hep-Hep shout as well. Is this *honest* polemic?

I had further remarked critically in my critique that the psychology of the Hegelian school believes it has completely comprehended a series of states, hitherto still in many respects uncomprehended, which arise from the influence of bodily constitutions on the spirit, e.g. the differences of temperament, the idiosyncrasies, whereas it nevertheless knows how to say nothing about them but what is known to all. In particular I censured *Michelet* for incautiously abetting superstition in the doctrine of presentiments and visions, of magnetic clairvoyance, etc. Prof. R. assures us (p. 395) that it is a sign of lack of education if someone “thinks he has already got everything down to size;” he even censures *Michelet* because the latter “seems to rival absolute spirit strongly in the attribute of omniscience too.” But he also says: “Now I am of course, in my confidence in the power of cognition, not so *timid and faint-hearted as Herr Exner seems to wish philosophers to be*;” and the close of this section, headed “The Blasé Attitude of the *nil admirari* [being astonished at nothing] and the *Craving for Wonders*,” runs: “This blasé attitude of being already finished with everything and of having packed the universe into the nutshell of a few formulas is reprehensible as a product of levity; but still more insufferable is that *half-pious blasé attitude of perennial astonishment*, which puts the easy panegyric of gaping wonder in place of the hard work of cognition, and is seized by the craving [p. II.10] to exchange the concept for not-knowing about the marvelous.” It needs no great art of interpretation to find what I am accused of here: a hostile disposition toward science and a predilection for credulous astonishment. This happens without any passage of my little work being adduced as proof, nor was any such to be found. On the contrary, I speak out expressly and sharply against everything that gives nourishment to superstition; my whole critique is a *complaint* that the works examined contain *too little knowledge*, a *summons* to more thorough *inquiry*. What a pity, therefore, that that accusation is so clumsy; otherwise it would be a really fine little trick, by which one quietly ignores the principal reproach I had made, the reproach of unscientific procedure, and turns it back on me myself in an underhand way. As it is, it can surely only be called a *malicious* insinuation itself; it is a *dishonest* polemic.

The section “The Incurability of Diseases of the Soul” (p. 418) begins with the following reproach: “Herr *Exner* reports that I am supposed to have said: ‘some diseases of the soul are curable, others not.’ To this report he adds that with me, then, *frivolity* is already joined *to triviality*. — Had I said *nothing further* about the curability of diseases of the soul, my critic might perhaps hold forth about me in this way. Since this is not the case, he prepares his triumph over me only through *frivolous concealment*.”

Prof. R. thereupon sets out what else he taught on that occasion about the curability of diseases of the soul. Now had I really “reported” nothing but what he here makes me say, then “my critic might perhaps hold forth about me in this way;” but the passage of my little work to which he refers is immediately preceded, on p. 22, by the following, which had earlier received its supporting evidence: “Of what physicians and empirical psychologists have collected so far we find all sorts of things here; but from the authors’ own absolute knowledge not the slightest addition, no new experience, not even a fleeting witty remark; indeed, the coarse material, in no way soluble in the vapor of thought, [p. II.11] drags it down to triviality.” Now follows that passage, which, however, *in full* runs thus: “About the cure of diseases of the soul *Rosenkranz* (p. 156) has nothing to say but that some are incurable, others curable; the latter *because spirit in itself always remains the real possibility of reason. How the former, the incurability of some, is compatible with this does not concern him, and* so frivolity is already joined to triviality.” What further, then, has Prof. R. taught that I did not report? He himself gives out the following as such: “I find the possibility of cure in the fact that through the illness spirit is not negated *as such*, as *total*.” The rest that he adds is an amplification of this thought, as everyone can convince himself on p. 418 of the “Refutation.” Now the same thought is indeed found also in the Psychology, 1st ed. p. 156; but there it is reduced by the author himself to the equivalent one, that *spirit in itself retains the real possibility of reason*, and precisely these words are found in my report. Thus *my account* is charged with frivolous concealment with the help of the quite simple trick that *one oneself conceals a part of it*. By thus quoting the beginning and end of my passage but leaving out the middle, and moreover ignoring the connection with what precedes, one first gains a semblance of right to a harsh reproach, and at the same time presents my remarks about triviality and frivolity as disconnected and therefore groundless; one also has the further advantage of not having to give the justification demanded in the omitted passage. *How*, I had asked, is the incurability of some diseases of the soul to be reconciled with the curability of others, if the ground for the latter lies in the fact that spirit in itself retains the real possibility of reason, and this ground admittedly (1st ed. p. 156) holds for the former as well? This is passed over in silence. The reproach I had to make against the Hegelian psychology, that despite the liveliest assurances of knowledge it nevertheless, on closer inspection, so often knows nothing, [p. II.12] is painful, to be sure; but advantages that one seeks to win by fighting in the manner described here can hardly be real ones for the system itself.

While in what has just been discussed I am reproached with a frivolous *concealment*, on another occasion, according to pp. 378 ff., I am supposed to have made a false *insertion*. There it says: “E.g. I say that the sleeper sinks into the position which the organism has as a fetus. Herr *Exner* disputes this, cites himself as an example of another sleeping posture, and even at the end of the book calls my assertion drivel. But he *inserts* a word which is missing in my text, namely the word: *always*; now since it is a well-known fact that people *can* sleep in the most varied, even the most uncomfortable positions, I am supposed to have contradicted experience.” I did in fact insert that word in my critique (p. 109), which is missing in Prof. R. (p. 68); the malicious falsification thus seems to be proven. Nevertheless here too we again come upon nothing but a little fencer’s trick, of which an *honest* polemic might well be ashamed. The passage of my critique cited, p. 109, reads in full as follows: “*A loose play with empty concepts, which here and there even becomes drivel, is the most pervasive characteristic trait of the works before us*. Or is it not drivel when one asserts that water and fire outside us are perceived through the fire and water in us; or that tone is filled time? when one calls it an ingenious explanation that sleepwalkers are held fast on roofs by the attraction of the moon? when one acknowledges it as a sign of quiet strength that German peasants wear their blue or green coats lined with red? when one considers it proven that among all the heavenly bodies ensouled beings live only on the earth, or that the sleeper always assumes the position of the fetus, etc.? — He who despises experience does not think it worth the trouble to know it either. This explains how we read here that summer and winter are nearness to and distance from the sun; that solid bodies do not conduct sound as well as air; that saliva is a tongue-produced, [p. II.13] specific water; the flesh is the organ of feeling; and similar things, which in anyone who is not an absolute philosopher one would not hesitate to call gross ignorance.” One sees that what had earlier been discussed at greater length is here briefly recapitulated in the concluding section. Prof. R.’s remark on the fetal position of the sleeper, too, was dealt with earlier, on p. 8 of my critique, though only in three lines, and there, where the very words of the work had to be taken as the basis, I did *not* use the “always.” But I assumed there, without further discussion, that from the context it *went without saying*, and on this my reproach that the assertion contradicts experience was founded. Later, where it was no longer a matter of the author’s words at all, but of a reference back to thoughts already judged, the “always” was taken up, precisely because it expresses the author’s thought; but without quotation marks. Yet Prof. R. now asserts that the “always” is *not* his thought; *not always*, so he says he taught, but *sometimes* does the sleeper adopt that position, which he therefore does not *have to* adopt, but only *can*. Let us therefore look at his passage (1st ed., pp. 67 and 68) in its context. It reads: “The state in which the cerebral sensibility sinks into itself and the plastic activity promotes the nourishment of the body is *sleep*; that in which the sensibility enters into commerce with the external world, in which the nerves take affections into themselves, while reproduction works only slowly, is that of *waking*. Originally, in the mother’s womb, man sleeps, and his birth is his primitive awakening. The position of the sleeper therefore also repeats the posture of the fetus in the uterus. The head sinks onto the breast, the arms cross over the body, etc.” The penultimate sentence, whose sense is in question, forms with the preceding one a closely knit whole, and nothing indicates a transition into another quantity or modality. Either, then, we must take it too as a universal one, as the preceding ones evidently are, or, if it is demanded that a “sometimes,” a “can,” be inserted there, then we must be allowed [p. II.14] to read here too: “Originally, in the mother’s womb, man *can sometimes* sleep, and his birth *can sometimes* be his primitive awakening; the state in which the sensibility enters into commerce with the external world *can sometimes* be that of waking; that in which the cerebral sensibility sinks into itself and the plastic activity promotes the nourishment of the body *can sometimes* be sleep, etc. But the author could hardly bear to see the meshes of his dialectical net so cruelly torn, and he will therefore have to resign himself to putting up with the other interpretation as well. The same follows, moreover, from the *conceptual* content of the passage. For the *concept* of sleeping is developed dialectically, and *from it*, hence with *dialectical necessity*, its connection with fetal life is demonstrated; thus the two conclusions follow: the fetus sleeps, the sleeper has the fetal position; and so the assertions are surely universal and apodictic ones. To be sure, the matter is in itself utterly trivial, but not when the reproach of *interpolation* is founded upon it. Moreover, in *other* works one would on a similar occasion gladly suppose merely an inaccuracy of expression which the reader corrects for himself; but let one look back at the anthology laid out in the above passage quoted from p. 109 of my critique, and one will be convinced that the levity of assertion which makes mischief in the works discussed justifies no indulgence whatever. The “etc.” in the above enumeration of drivel is not an empty formula; the “abstraction” can, if required, develop into all sorts of further “concrete” amusements. I see and well understand that Prof. R. too does not now wish to put forward that assertion universally; but let him, now that he has thought better of it, make my censure a crime not of mine, but of the character of his work and the carelessness of his exposition, and least of all let him attempt by a subterfuge to shift a fault off himself and onto his opponent.

With this apparent scrupulousness with which he watches over the mention of his own propositions, Prof. R. permits himself [p. II.15] unabashed, however (p. 425), to insert a “perhaps” into a passage quoted from my critique, and to knead together, out of two sentences separated by seven lines and mutilated by him, a single one of admittedly silly appearance, which he then makes fun of; and this new product is quoted with quotation marks. In the same way, in a passage quoted on p. 428, he makes a “da er” [since he] out of a “der” [who], thereby destroys the grammatical construction of the sentence, and then rails at the “vulgar language.” (See these passages in my critique, p. 33 and p. 41.) In a similar manner it is said (p. 426) that I *extolled* of *Schelling* how the mere word of the positive already turned a joyful hope toward him, whereas I (p. 29) merely *narrate a fact*; and on p. 409 it is asserted that I made against the Hegelian philosophy the silly reproach of “treating concepts which *also already* occur in the Wolffian psychology,” whereas what I reproach it with is that it treats them *no better*, indeed in part even worse, than *Wolff* did. This is, if not a dishonest, then certainly a highly *frivolous* polemic.

And now, having discussed the most important passages in which not my arguments but my person was the object of the attack, let the reader survey what has been cited, in order to see what picture Prof. R. has drawn for the public of his opponent. To him this opponent is a man who, spoiled for science from childhood on by a faith that holds thought captive, given over with rigid mind to a dull astonishment, hates knowledge, and combats it with the weapons of spiteful insinuation and malicious falsification of its sources. — Perhaps Prof. R. himself is surprised at the figure which the features drawn by him yield when they are put together; perhaps he himself will find, when he reads my replies, that he has brought grave accusations with irresponsible levity and has thoughtlessly made himself guilty, for the most part, of that of which he accuses me, and that by this side of his refutation he has fastened on our literature, which at present suffers only too much from the personal squabbling of philosophers, one more ugly [p. II.16] stain: this is to be wished in his own interest, but the stain remains. A few months before he wrote his refutation, he complained publicly, against *Leroux*, about the scientific opponents who “subject the moral conduct of the individuals placed under accusation to a pharisaical critique,” and he enjoined: “the present apologetics (of the Hegelian philosophy) is in the difficult position of knowing these intentions (of the opponents) well, and yet for its part abstaining from all intrusion of the personal. Science has, more than ever, to prove its chastity.” Against one thing, then, he is now secured — I mean against that epitaph which the Athenians set up for one of their philosophers: “He lived as he taught.”

To dispose of the most unpleasant matters all together at once, I will also consider here Prof. *Erdmann’s* short reply; it worthily joins the above. After he (p. X of the 2nd edition of his “Logic and Metaphysics”) has complained about *Michelet*, who now seems to have become the *whipping-boy* on whom people strike out time after time, he continues: “However little I otherwise wish to compare the two, such quoting is nevertheless in its result hardly better than the procedure which *Exner* has recently permitted himself against my ‘Outline of Psychology.’ This is so ‘homespun’ to him that he, by contrast, spices the confectioner’s pastry of his critique with *fabrications*. Indeed, he utters these with such confidence that even the level-headed *Drobisch*, not expecting such audacity, repeats as a ‘very apt remark’ in his ‘Empirical Psychology’ the *untruth* that I had represented somnambulism and derangement not as abnormal but as necessary states, whereas my ‘Outline,’ §. 35 and 44, expressly says the opposite.” —

Certainly, a critique that rests on *fabrications* is too contemptible a botch to deserve more than a short, blunt dismissal. But this dismissal — does it need no proof? One suffices, if it is valid; it runs: *Exner* has asserted, and the level-headed *Drobisch*, though misled by that audacity, has repeated after him, that *Erdmann* [p. II.17] represents somnambulism and derangement as *necessary* states; this is an untruth, since §. 35 and 44 expressly say the opposite. — This passage, I confess, left me somewhat taken aback at first, for it is after all just too positive; but since I was not conscious of any untruth, I feared a misunderstanding on *Drobisch’s* part. I therefore took up his book, and found there (p. 336) the following passage from p. 60 of my critique faithfully quoted: “All three works before us also list, in the series of the dialectical moments of development of subjective spirit, somnambulism, disease of the soul, passion. *Either* the dialectical development is at the same time the factual one, and then every human being on his way from the cradle to the grave must necessarily at least once be a *clairvoyant*, once *insane*, once exclusively *ruled by a passion*; *or*, if one wishes to give experience its due and deny this, then the dialectical development is *not* the factual one.” Here, then, is the *corpus delicti*. I had raised the question how the dialectical method might be related to the genetic one, i.e. to that method which demonstrates the origin of changeable states from their causes, through whose occurrence they become *necessary*; or in other words, whether the series of dialectically developed concepts portray the real connection of actual things and states according to their causal relation. In that case all dialectically developed mental states would have to occur factually and *necessarily*, and indeed in the same order in which they were developed. That incriminated passage of my critique, referring to this difference of method, consists of two sentences. The first says that Prof. E. also lists, in the series of the dialectical moments of development of subjective spirit, somnambulism, disease of the soul, passion; and this he himself has not contradicted. Somnambulism or magnetic rapport forms in his work the third member of a dialectical trilogy whose first member is, in the author’s words, embryonic life, and whose second is those phenomena of rapport in which the individual is immediately touched by [p. II.18] what concerns its world. Disease of the soul is the second member of a trilogy whose first member is that self-feeling in which all the determinacies of the psycho-somatic totality of the individual are *its own*; whose third consists in the coincidence of the two lives of the individual, which negates their being distinct. Passion, finally, as enduring passion, is the third member of a trilogy whose first and second members are formed by emotion and transient passion. The first sentence of my passage is therefore not merely unchallenged; it is also true. Its second sentence, however, *asserts nothing whatever about what* Prof. E. *taught*; it says: *Either* Prof. E. teaches that the dialectical development is at the same time the genetic one, i.e. that the dialectically developed moments of spirit are all also factually necessary, *or* he asserts the opposite; in the former case he contradicts experience, in the latter his method suffers from an essential defect, because it leaves us uninstructed about the causal connection of the phenomena. This is expressed with perfect clarity, and has been understood just as correctly by *Drobisch* and *Rosenkranz*. Shall I now pursue such a wretched business still further, show still more clearly on whose side the *fabrication*, the *gross untruth*, lies here? In the face of that reply every distrust, and with it every precaution, is permitted. My passage continues as follows: “We let everyone choose as he pleases, *but hold to the express decision of the school*, and persist in the conclusion just drawn. *Further confirmations* of our opinion we shall find later in the works before us.” The express decision of the school meant here is cited by me somewhat earlier (p. 59) as the result of the preceding investigation, and reads: “Thus we *cannot doubt* the essential *difference* which the school makes between the dialectical and the genetic method, and the former shows *not* the temporal *and causal* connection, but only the *conceptual* one.” In the preceding investigation, among other things, three passages from Prof. E.’s work are cited which help to establish this result. [p. II.19] Not only, then, did I *not assert* that Prof. E. declares somnambulism and diseases of the soul to be factually necessary states; I even *expressly* said the *opposite*, and *not merely once*, *but repeatedly*; for in the “further confirmations” I come back to it several times. So, e.g., p. 74: “From this too we learn that the dialectical method is *not* the genetic one.” — In such a way does he found his reproach of fabrications, and this is admittedly a great deal easier than refuting my objections to his developments of determinism, indeterminism and freedom.

According to this, it seems that Prof. E. holds his own honor to be so cheap a thing that he believes he need take it only little into account in the economy of his doings; but he has no right to deal in the same manner with the honor of others. We see men, for the sake of a word that casts doubt on the honorableness of their character, take up arms in a fight of life and death, and we call this custom barbaric; and yet how estimable, at least, appears the delicacy of sentiment underlying it, compared with the mental dullness and moral recklessness with which absolute philosophers, who take themselves for the pinnacles of the world spirit, fancy they may abuse their own honor and that of others. Or does such a procedure apply only to the “author completely unknown until then,” or indeed to the “writer from south-eastern Germany”? Is he an *ἔκσπονδος* [one outside the truce], toward whom one has no duty, an outlawed man without rights who, driven by chance onto the literary shore, may be slaughtered without ceremony by its inhabitants to the idol of the land? Then truly he would have cause to be ashamed of the attempt to take part in a literature which is managed on such principles by men who have some name in it. —

The writer of these lines did not consider it compatible with the respect owed to a literature that he should “do his studying before the public”; but he has nevertheless done it, as well as he could. If, among the existing philosophical systems, he turned to one more than to another, he was determined to do so [p. II.20] neither by the deafening noise of surroundings enthusing over it, nor by the spirit of a coterie, still less by prospects of external gain, not even by the overwhelming power of the proximity of an eminent personality. The principal systems lay before him with equal objectivity, and only the correctness or falsity of the thoughts which he believed he found in them gave him his direction, not without that inner struggle which everyone knows who has thought for himself. This direction and its scientific views he abandons to every critique — if it so pleases, to every abuse as well; whatever is true in them will fight its way to a place: but the innermost sanctuary of his intentions and his willing no man lets be defiled with impunity. — And herewith enough of personalities, since after all I have been forced to go into them.

### Weitere Charakterisirung der Widerlegung. — Further Characterization of the Refutation.

It is not easy to follow Prof. R.’s refutation critically; for with the greatest possible ease it discusses everything in motley confusion, leaving the reader the trouble of seeing whether he can discover a thread holding the torn pieces together. Some remarks may therefore come first which will characterize more closely the scientific polemic with which we now have to deal.

Already some months before the appearance of the refutation, Cotta’s *Vierteljahrsschrift* and, after it, the *A. A. Zeitung* had known how my objections would be refuted: namely by the assertion that I stand at the standpoint of the abstract understanding, which the speculative philosopher considers far too low for one to be able to judge from it what goes on upon the dialectical heights. It was easy for them to prophesy, for this is a commonplace of the school; but precisely because it is worn out and threadbare, it was at least not to be expected that it would spread itself about quite so widely. Rather one could hope that Prof. R. would disdain it like those much-hounded phrases [p. II.21] of which he says that a writer can hardly use them with honor any longer. But now, right at the beginning of his reply (p. 370), he lets himself be heard on my work as follows: “This brochure, written in the most accessible popular style and in a jovial humor, is a product of the polemic of the *abstract understanding*, which resists everything speculative.” And again (p. 371): “Herr *Exner* is stuck through and through in that form of cognition which since *Kant* we call the *form of the understanding*.” Further (p. 387): “Herr *Exner* complains about the unrest of the dialectical process, which drives on from contradiction to contradiction and does not let it come to a standstill even in absolute spirit. To the *abstract understanding* this complaint is quite natural.” And again (p. 390): “The concept of *absolute spirit* is therefore that in which speculation too finds *peace*, a peace which is higher than all *Exnerian* reason, which is namely only a *scholastic understanding* with the presumption of reason.” And again (p. 410): “Herr *Exner* has an admirable skill in understanding speculation in the way in which it does not wish to be understood. I have already shown above that from his standpoint of the *toughest, most abstract understanding* he cannot do otherwise at all.” And so it goes on in other passages. Prof. R., then, has *reason*, and I have only *understanding*. How does he mean this, I wonder? Does he take himself and the other Hegelians for beings organized more nobly by nature than other thinkers, for the royal stags and thoroughbred horses, as it were, among philosophers? Such a wholly excessive modesty would not be new in the history of our science; it is quite possible that it might be brought back by our age, which chews over again so much that was long since consumed. But then the higher beings would have to want to keep their higher knowledge entirely to themselves, and not plague us with claims to universal validity; we might ask indulgence for the commonness of our nature, beyond which we simply could not go; for if, in our limitedness, it should perhaps occur to us to take ourselves for still higher beings than they are, just as the raven in the fable liked his own voice better than [p. II.22] the nightingale’s, there would be no way of thoroughly changing this. Above all, however, we shall ask at least not to be harnessed to the plough. But it occurs to me that Prof. R., like the best natural historians, recognizes several races, to be sure, but only one species of man, and as a Hegelian he places its essence in thinking; so we shall presumably run along among the other featherless bipeds. For thinking of the understanding is also a thinking, though a lower one, while the thinking of reason is the higher. The higher? Who asserts it? The Hegelians; the non-Hegelians think otherwise, and who is right has not yet been decided in a universally valid way. This, then, is how the matter stands: the Hegelians assert the truth and necessity of so-called dialectical thinking, their opponents deny it; on both sides reasons are adduced with zeal, but without general success. But the Hegelians add: we hereby decree at the same time that our view is rational and that one merely of the understanding, that we are those most highly gifted in spirit above all others, but our opponents one and all are feeble-minded. In this way the rest of us have become poor philosophers of the understanding.

Still, it would be possible that they are right; not merely about the dialectical method, but also in this, that only an unusual weakness of insight can misjudge it. This is no doubt what Prof. R. means when he reproaches me most forcefully (p. 387) with not seeing “how a posited concept is self-contradictory, and the result of the contradiction is its dissolution into another concept, whose unity, however, by no means permits a resting, but rather, in developing itself, leads to the concept of another contradiction.” Here, however, he is mistaken. I see very well that posited concepts contain contradictions, and I believe I have demonstrated more of them in his psychological concepts than he himself saw there. In general, it is not the thought that contradictions must be developed that predominantly characterizes the dialectical method, since, as is acknowledged, it is found outside that method as well, but rather the one emphasized by Prof. R. on p. 371: the resolution of the [p. II.23] differences, the *unity* of the *opposed*. Is the correctness of this thought really so evident that only feeble-mindedness can doubt it? What grounds, then, have been put forward for it? *Schelling’s* philosophy of nature appealed to an intellectual intuition; the Hegelian school has given it up, but only in appearance. It teaches that the dialectical method must prove itself in its application. So it develops from some concept or other its opposite, and then it is asserted gravely and solemnly that the two opposed concepts are one. The opponents, to be sure, say that they do not see this unity; whereupon they are given to understand that *they lack reason*. So we have *intellectual intuition in another garb*, and so we receive by the mass the assertions of that unity, which, alas! always remain mere assertions. *Hegel*, it is true, has given in his Encyclopaedia a proof of his own for the *falsity of the principium exclusi tertii* [principle of the excluded third]; but he has been shown to have a levity, “*levitatem*,” in his conception of this law of thought so striking that hardly anyone will overlook it who has taken the time to become acquainted with the despised old logic. I am not aware that any pupil has attempted to defend the master against this. Will Prof. R. do so? He will have attentive readers.

Thus, to my knowledge, there remains only that ground which runs through the whole system, but stands in its simplest form at the head of the Phenomenology: the Now is the night and also the noon, the Here a tree and also a house; thus night and noon are one in the Now, house and tree one in the Here. — We will disregard the violence that is done to thought no less than to language when one says: the Now is the noon, instead of: now it is noon, and: the Here is a house, instead of: here is a house; what lies at the bottom is the thought, as true as it is old, that the senses show a changeableness of things by virtue of which these are thus and also otherwise, i.e. opposed to themselves. And on this, that is, on the testimony of the *senses*, *Hegel* asserts that the opposed is one. But the men of the empirical sciences [p. II.24] speak of *illusions of the senses*, and no one denies their existence. How, then, does one come to the conjecture that such an illusion is present anywhere? Surely only through the observation that a particular statement of the senses contradicts what one already knows, whether through other perceptions or from elsewhere, and thus thinks. This necessitates new observations, which are continued and varied until old and new agree well on all sides. The empiricists are therefore of the opinion that their perceptions must submit to a critique by their thinking, and in this respect they place the latter higher than the former. Philosophers too teach such a primacy of thinking; by virtue of their thinking they demand the removal of every contradiction from thinking, insofar as it means to be cognition, no matter whether it has penetrated into it through the senses or by another way; for *contradiction*, they say, *destroys itself*. Since *Socrates*, agreement and conflict of thoughts, within and outside philosophy, have counted as a criterion of truth and of error, if not as the only one, and where the senses seemed to exhibit something contradictory as actual, an error of perception or of thinking was presupposed. Not so *Hegel*. The senses show us, he says, contradictions in the actual, and from now on thinking is to take no offense at this, however much its nature may resist it; it must allow that contradiction is the truth, that what is opposed is one. This is the rational insight, standing above that of the common understanding. On what, then, does it rest? On the testimony of the *senses*, which, moreover, has not even been closely examined. It is thus not a higher insight than that of the understanding, but a lower one, as surely as sensuous cognition stands beneath that of the understanding; the Hegelian system is (and again I am not the first to demonstrate this) in its innermost kernel crude *empiricism*. Does one want proofs from the most recent times? *Gabler* (“The Hegelian Philosophy,” p. 143) assures us that the unity of substance, despite the successively alternating opposed determinations, contains nothing contradictory at all, and continues: “Indeed, it [p. II.25] should rather be the most comprehensible thing for everyone, since every self-conscious, willing and thinking I already contains the solution of that contradiction, in that each, capable of every difference, as of the opposed, and passing over into it, nevertheless does not cease to be the One identical with itself, but is, and remains, *with itself* in all *its* differences and determinations, and keeps itself.” The chief proof for the unity of the opposed in the I is thus taken — from *experience*! In the same way Prof. R. (2nd ed., p. 107) finds in the *fact* of the simultaneous existence of opposed presentations in consciousness a particular case in which the speculative nature of the concept, to be the unity of unity and of opposition, proves itself; indeed he even says (p. 105): “As for, first, the supposed impossibility of the non-existence (read: existence) of contradiction, it has now surely been sufficiently refuted by *Hegel’s* Logic; indeed, the very assertion of the impossibility already contained a naive refutation, since one did, after all, have to think the concept of contradiction which one declared impossible, and thus had to grant it existence, at least in thinking, until one had negated it again.” The existence of a square circle, of a wooden iron, thus presents no difficulty; these things exist at least in our thinking, because it is a *fact* that we think them. *Leibniz*, to be sure, remarked that such things cannot be thought, but only said. In a similar way Prof. *Erdmann* (“Logic and Metaphysics,” 2nd ed., p. 75) assures us that contradiction “deserves to be predicated of everything. Instead of this, the fear of contradiction is so great that it is designated as the unthinkable, while a multitude of appearances are real examples of existing contradiction, without their being regarded, for all that, as impossibilities.” The argument, then, runs: the unity of the opposed is, for it is factually given. — If one covers this empiricism with the high-sounding name of reason, if one speaks of a total absence of presuppositions, of absolute knowledge, if one roams about almost exclusively in the regions of the highest universalities, which seemingly have nothing whatever in common with experience and yet [p. II.26] live and exist only through it, then one can deceive dim eyes; but the presumption and hollowness of the thought have nonetheless become ever more visible.

For Prof. R. all this may say nothing; nor may it disconcert him in the least into what relation, through *Hegel’s* principle, philosophy enters with all the other sciences, which are all built upon the principle of the exclusion of contradiction, and in which, if it is false, no stone remains upon another. He will perhaps declare them to be errors by the mass, or thoughtlessnesses, as *Hegel* did with mathematics, or else grant them a truth, to be sure, which however is properly none, and with which they will therefore hardly be satisfied; but the one thing at least he ought not to deny: that, if that unity of the opposed is really so very easily comprehensible, he and his friends have proved themselves bad teachers. For they have written a hundred books, and yet with the spreading of this principle they have got no further than to a school among schools; a numerous one, admittedly, but one which has the peculiar fate not to increase with time but, as it seems, to decrease strikingly, and, like a general in distress, to suffer defections on all sides. Thus Prof. R. must look on as men who were already quite nicely rational, and in whom no little pride was taken, fall back again into the narrow-mindedness of the abstract understanding. In this the Hegelian system fares like Faust with the Earth Spirit, which he had the power to attract, but not to hold. How will Prof. R. explain these phenomena? From the weakness of his philosophical system, or from the speculative incapacity of the fallen, of those formerly acknowledged as mighty dialecticians? He will forgive me if, to say nothing of older examples, I merely draw his attention to one recent example known to me, to L. *Feuerbach*. He, surely, was once as rational as anyone in Germany, and even in “The Essence of Christianity” he still shone in the pure water of Hegeldom, albeit as one of the Left, and was therefore not long ago still treated by my opponent with much tenderness and attentiveness. [p. II.27] And now this man has the misfortune, in his latest work, “Principles of the Philosophy of the Future,” of wanting through his “new philosophy” to *negate* the Hegelian system, and indeed to negate it *without contradiction*. As regards in particular the unity of the opposed, he asserts (§ 47) that the Hegelian philosophy unites the oppositions only by making them groundless and without support through abstraction, whereupon they all at once collapse; and in § 48 he says: “The means of *uniting opposed* or *contradictory determinations without contradiction* in one and the same being is only — *time*” — with this he has gone over completely to the side of the men of the understanding. In the same way Prof. R. has to live to see his “friend *Dorguth*” designate the presumptions of Hegelian reason as “folly.” Now if such things are done in the green wood, how may one wonder at the dry? But one could *learn* from it — *modesty*. There was, admittedly, a time when the first intoxication of enthusiasm, combined with the favor of the circumstances of the time, made it comprehensible that many Hegelians let themselves be carried away almost to the deification of their master, to the public contempt and maltreatment of those who thought otherwise, to the renewal of that “divine rudeness” which disfigures certain parts of our literature; the Neoplatonists, too, raved about their *Plotinus*, Arabs and Scholastics about their *Aristotle*, Wolffians about their *Wolff*, philosophers of nature about their *Schelling*. Today, however, after so many experiences, the lessons of ancient and modern history, after all the disappointments and failed hopes, the discord within, the loss of so many friends, the continuing contradiction from without — today, when people already venture to call the Hegelian system a “*système introuvable*” [a system not to be found] and to proclaim it as a *fact* “*that a Hegelian school no longer exists at all now*,” the Hegelians ought to know that what they need against their adversaries is *grounds*, not *abuse*. For that, neither much reason nor much understanding would be required. Their talk of the tenacious understanding-mindedness of their opponents is nothing but *abuse*.

[p. II.28] Directly connected with this is the reproach of *misunderstanding*, and in the heading of a section of its own I am even credited with a virtuosity in this art. To escape this reproach entirely is impossible at present, whether one tries to write in *Hegel’s* sense or against him. The three psychologies of the Hegelian school which I have examined diverge from one another in many ways; I do not believe that their authors will explain this otherwise than by a misunderstanding of *Hegel* on all sides. One also knows the various fractions of the school, crumbling ever further, their scissions on all the main points of the doctrine, and the bitterness with which they not infrequently impute to one another the misunderstanding of the master. If this befalls Hegelians, then non-Hegelians must naturally fare still worse? “I will not dispute,” Prof. R. wrote not long ago, “that in Germany hundreds of adherents, like hundreds of opponents, understand *Hegel* as if he taught that” etc.; “but,” he continues, “that is not so. One must take *Hegel* as he is, and not judge him by the one-sidedness of his interpreters.” How naive! What, then, is *Hegel* like? And is Prof. R. not himself one of his interpreters?

For my justification, however, I believe I do not even need an appeal to this general human weakness in understanding the Hegelian system. In the section mentioned above, three pieces of evidence for my virtuosity in misunderstanding are adduced. Prof. R. recalls first (p. 411) his doctrine that consciousness progresses from observing to experiencing, from the individual to the universal. “But now, having arrived at this, it realizes that in itself the ground of the individual is rather the universal, which merely repeats itself in the individual as in its specimens.” And further he says: “What does Herr *Exner* make of this? He asks whether one would really say:

“‘Redness, this universal property of cabbage roses, is the ground why a rose standing before him, the non-Hegelian, has precisely forty petals? Is it usual for them (the non-Hegelians), when a new law is discovered in a body, a new force for chemical effects, that *the body suddenly* [p. II.29] *vanishes from the worktable* and comes to light in their spirit as a new accession?’” —

There now follow fine expectorations of “platitudes, clumsy notions, burlesque wit,” and the irrational misunderstanding is demonstrated.

First I remark that the passage cited from my critique does indeed look clumsy enough here, though not through my fault; Prof. R. has again been pleased to quote somewhat inaccurately. That passage makes two objections, taken from the relation of the color of a cabbage rose to the number of its petals, and from the non-vanishing of the examined body from the worktable. They refer to *two* assertions of the book examined, which are summarized as follows on p. 26 of my critique: “To the consciousness of the understanding, it says on pp. 198 ff., the universal of things shows itself as the ground of the individual in them; this universal shows itself *further* as its thoughts, and thereby it is sublated for objectivity and has become identical with its subjectivity.” — Prof. R., however, mentions only the first of these assertions and passes over the second in silence, whereupon the second part of my objection is, of course, left standing like a scarecrow on a harvested field. But let us look more closely. If Prof. R. believes that I seriously meant that he asserts that vanishing of a body in consequence of a reflection of the understanding upon it, he is mistaken; I do not consider him insane. But what is at issue is the establishing of the *concept* of the *consciousness of the understanding*, and of such concepts I have demanded, in agreement with my opponent, that they *must correspond to experience*. Now he expressly teaches that second point which I have just had him say; he writes (p. 202, 1st ed.) that in the consciousness of the understanding “the opposition of external objectivity and of the subjectivity opposed to it has indeed vanished,” and “consciousness therefore sublates objectivity, as something external to it, within itself.” The transformation of objectivity into subjectivity is, moreover, quite properly the guiding thought in the author’s psychological developments on their course from the soul [p. II.30] through consciousness to thinking spirit, and this development is the special carrying-out of a chief principle of the system, that one, grown so popular in recent philosophy, of the unity of thinking and being. Now it seemed to me, however, *to contradict experience very greatly* that *all those men* to whom we credit *understanding* should assume a *becoming-sublated of objectivity* through their thinking, a *vanishing of the difference between objectivity and subjectivity*, although I granted that the Hegelians do so; and so I said: “But where the talk is of the *universally human* consciousness of the understanding, let nothing be slipped in on us that is not usually found also in a sensible Englishman or Frenchman, or in a German non-Hegelian.” And upon this followed the question whether, for example, it may well have lain in the consciousness of the understanding of a *Davy* to assume that a body which he had just analyzed chemically had vanished from his table and come to light in his spirit. I know well that Prof. R. reckons as belonging to an external object not merely the universal, but also those individual features of it which are the object of external sensation; but precisely because he does this, and because they really do belong to it, I see in his assertion of the vanishing of *objectivity* through *thinking* an absurdity and a contradiction among his own doctrines. I merely remind him of what he said (2nd ed., p. 238): “Reason is immanent in objectivity. *It by no means exhausts its essence*, for to that belongs its qualitative determinateness.” But what reason does not succeed in, the understanding will be still less able to do. Now I do not know whether in this I am caught up in a misunderstanding of his view; I would therefore be very grateful to him if he had not merely asserted that this is so, but also proved it.

It is exactly the same with my second objection, which is directed against the view that the *universal* is the *ground* of the *individual*. What I would have to say against it demands a different context from the one which, in a work such as [p. II.31] this and its predecessor, can be expected and given; for that reason it is only partly indicated in the latter, and must at present, too, be postponed to a better occasion. The following merely for my justification against the reproach of misunderstanding. — Already what is understood in that view by the universal is highly indeterminate. One thinks sometimes of *laws* according to which the changes of external things take place, sometimes of *forces* through which they arise, sometimes also of concepts of the *species* and *genus* of the things that arise. With regard to all these universalities, however, I consider it factually untrue that they are regarded by scientifically educated men, unless these happen to belong to certain philosophical schools, as the ground of the individual; that is, I consider the *concept* of the *consciousness of the understanding* set up by Prof. R. to be *incorrect* in this respect too. Those men speak, for example, of a law according to which certain substances always combine chemically in definite stoichiometric proportions; but that, when a particular compound now lies before them, they regard that law as its ground is wholly unknown to me. To my knowledge they presuppose as the ground certain properties, as yet unknown to them, of the combined atoms, while the law is to them *only their own thought*, in which they clothe their confident conviction that under the same conditions the same result will occur, and has occurred hitherto. Rather might they assert a force as the ground of the combination, say a force of attraction, be it the electrical one, or gravitation within immeasurably small distances, or another. But here too it would not be a *universal* force, since they leave forces as universalities to the philosophers of nature, would also hesitate to presuppose without further ado the force of attraction in the case of expansible bodies, but would explain the compound before them directly from the *most individual* forces of attraction, namely those of the substances combined here, convinced that the forces of attraction of other substances would have yielded an entirely different compound. Least of all, however, does it occur to them to consider the concepts of species and genus, the “universalities of the abstract understanding,” [p. II.32] as the Hegelian school calls them, to be those grounds. These, in such an application, are rather wholly and entirely fruits of metaphysical speculation, which, as is well known, sown by *Plato*, came recently to their highest ripeness in Schelling’s philosophy of nature, but bloom again in *Hegel*, though with a somewhat altered color.
