At least, once again, I am not in the least aware that *those men* attempt to derive any individual characteristic of an object, e.g. the number of petals of a cabbage rose, from a characteristic of its higher concept of variety or species, e.g. its color. I do not fear to be mistaken in this, where it is a question not of theories but of very well-known facts; for however reprehensible the views of those men of the understanding may seem to the rational thinking of Prof. R., and however difficult it always is to draw a sharp boundary between thinkers of a consciousness merely of the understanding — that is to say here, a consciousness formed by no metaphysical exertions — and the philosophically educated, experience nevertheless teaches irrefutably that the views of the Hegelian philosophy in question are anything but native, let alone familiar, to the *general consciousness of the understanding* of the educated world. What I myself think of the scientific significance of those universalities Prof. R. can find, if he pleases, in my little work on nominalism and rationalism.<sup>∗)</sup> I thus believe I have justified the second of the objections he found so offensive; [p. II.33] but the misunderstandings with which I am reproached I have not been able to discover here either.

∗) Dr. Chr. H. *Weisse* has reviewed this little work in detail and in a benevolent manner (*Jahrbücher für wissenschaftliche Kritik* 1843, nos. 48–50). I owe him all the more thanks for this, the less the insignificance of the essay led one to expect such attention. I would gladly make his opposing views my own, should they prove sound; for what matters is the truth, and “Weissian” would sound no worse than Kantian, Hegelian, or Herbartian. For now I must content myself with two remarks. 1) It is not the *concept* of the being of simple beings, but *simple beings* themselves, independent of all relations, that are for *Herbart* the ultimate presuppositions of the world of appearances. If his system were realism because he has a *concept* of his absolute prius, there would be no dispute between nominalism and realism, for everyone must think what he thinks in concepts.

The second proof of my virtuosity in misunderstanding is given by Prof. R. (pp. 411 ff.) in the following words: “Because I distinguish the modification of habit only into the active and the passive, he thus rebukes me mightily: ‘where, pray, do I place those habits which occur not infrequently and cause careful *governesses* anxiety enough, e.g. always *turning up one’s nose* or *baring one’s teeth*? Is it skill or hardening?’

“Out of love for mankind, so that the poor governesses in Bohemia may know where they stand with *Hegel*, I hereby declare most solemnly that I consider the above-named mannerisms, which cause them so much *anxiety*, to be *skills*, and that their pupils, if they do not duly check these to such an extent, may train themselves into regular *grimaciers*.” What I criticized was not that Prof. R. divides habits into active and passive, but that he divides them into skills and hardenings. The expressions active and passive habit he introduced only in the 2nd ed., p. 176, in a new line, whereas in the first edition there is talk only of the habits in which “the process of the identification of the content with the self proceeds either from outside inward, or from inside outward.” Now even if one is willing to let these expressions count as synonymous with those, although strictly speaking they are not, the choice of the name skill for active habit still remains, even now, an unskillful one, and my criticism above means no more than this, and the examples I adduced are meant to bring no more than this to light. Is this a misunderstanding?

2) That general concepts could be the absolute prius both of being and of thinking has always been incomprehensible to me. Of thinking? Then there would be *thoughts before thinking*. In *Kant* the categories are a prius of cognition, not of thinking. Of being? So *Hegel* holds, and he has developed this view into a system which might easily make every other one erected on the same ground superfluous as an insignificant variety.

[p. II.34] On this occasion a remark forces itself upon me which I shall have cause to repeat more often still. I spoke against the account which Prof. R. and his friends give of habit on almost two full pages, pp. 23 and 24 of my critique. I pointed out, among other things, that the explanation of the origin of habits from repetition explains nothing unless it is shown at the same time *how* repetition then produces a habit; further, what a blunder it is to believe that with that word one has comprehended not merely bodily habits but also mental ones, up to the highest, virtuous self-mastery; how unscientifically one proceeds if, in explaining them, one does not even distinguish the most striking differences among them, since it is surely something different to be accustomed to heat and cold, or to insult and disgrace, to walk stooped from habit, and to be a mocker from habit. These remarks seemed to me of such a kind that the strength of a psychology could and should well prove itself on them. Prof. R. picks out from all my objections a single one, and indeed the most insignificant, in order to proclaim me with a trumpet blast a virtuoso in misunderstanding; the rest remain untouched. True, in the 2nd ed., p. 116, he takes some trouble. He adds to the old text a new half page, in which he says once again what he has already said once, and concludes with comic gravity: “Because habit is something so everyday, it does not yet follow from this that it *would be easy to comprehend*.” This, however, is all I get out of him. Perhaps he has the misfortune of being deaf in one ear, and I happened to speak the more important things toward the weaker side of his head. That would indeed sufficiently explain why, here and on other occasions, he is so talkative toward the one side, but so completely mute toward the other.

The third misunderstanding is supposed to lie in my conception of the doctrine of *self-consciousness*. Prof. R., namely, has rational self-consciousness proceed dialectically from the mutual recognition of the self of men, this recognition from the relation of slavery, [p. II.35] and further back from the struggle of men for life and death, the struggle, however, from self-consciousness, and self-consciousness from the consciousness of the understanding. My misunderstanding is supposed, according to p. 412, to show itself in this, that out of the *dependence of self-consciousness*, which he calls *selflessness*, I make a *lack of self-consciousness* in the slaves, and that I reproach him with substituting the Kantian concept of *human dignity* for the concept of self-consciousness.

In this development Prof. R., as is well known, follows *Hegel’s* Phenomenology, whose content is the becoming of knowledge, and whose course of thought is a continuous ascent from the individual through ever higher universalities to the highest. It begins with what is given by the senses, the *Here* and *Now*, thus with the whole breadth of the world of appearances. Stepping beyond sense-certainty, which has to do with these individualities, it arrives at the *consciousness of the understanding*, at the *genera* of sensible things. Over against these, however, stands the subject that presents them, and the higher unity of both is reached at the stage of *self-consciousness*. This comprehends all those genera and individual beings, the whole of objectivity, and at the same time subjectivity in its unity with the former, under and within itself. But over against the one self-consciousness stands another self-consciousness in the plurality of self-conscious beings: these lead to the higher universality of *rational self-consciousness*, which is the unity of all self-conscious beings, and through them of the subjectivity and the objectivity of all sensible things and their genera. With this the concept of *spirit* is reached. But at this stage of universality, too, we find at first a plurality of spirits, above which there stands at last the all-embracing highest unity and universality, *absolute spirit*, or *absolute knowing*. It is in this sense that *Gabler* says in the work cited (p. 140), after having found the task of philosophy to consist in its having to explain the world from One supreme principle, one supreme universality: “If one asks where, then, the *universal*, even if it does not as such [p. II.36] have an external and immediate existence, is to be found at all in the universe, the answer is: it has its existence only in thinking, and not only this, but *thinking*, and indeed thinking only from the side that it itself is (which, after all, no one will wish to deny it), *is also itself being*, or the *existence of the universal as such*, or of the totality of all particular determinations, even if these are at first not yet posited in their particularity and distinctness.” But thinking is precisely spirit. — Whether *Hegel’s* Phenomenology is a section of psychology or of metaphysics, and how it relates to his Logic, is disputed within the school itself. As psychology it evidently shows the becoming not of universally human knowledge but of Hegelian knowledge, for no unprejudiced person will see in the concepts of consciousness, of rational self-consciousness, of spirit, of absolute spirit set up there anything other than just the alleged knowledge of this school. Now those disciples who treated psychology specially have without hesitation lifted out individual portions of that work and incorporated them into their psychologies. One such portion is the development of rational self-consciousness under discussion here, which we shall consider more closely.

At the standpoint of consciousness, according to *Hegel*, the universalities of sensible things, their *genera*, have emerged; at the same time the opposition of subjectivity and objectivity persists. The next higher universality or *genus* is self-consciousness. It is the certainty of I = I, of the unity of subjectivity and objectivity. “Since (Phenomenology, Collected Works, vol. 2, p. 137) one has set out from the first immediate unity, and through the moments of formation and of process has returned to the unity of these two moments, and thereby again to the first simple substance, this *reflected unity* is a different one from the first. Over against that *immediate* unity, or unity expressed as a *being*, this second one is the universal unity, which has all these moments within it as sublated. It is the *simple genus*, which in the movement of life itself does not *exist for itself as this simple*; [p. II.37] but in this *result* life points to something other than what it is, namely to *consciousness*, for which it is as this unity, or as genus. This other life, however, for which the genus as such is, and which is *genus for itself*, *self-consciousness*, is to itself at first only as this simple essence, and has itself as pure I for its object; in its experience, which is now to be considered, this abstract object will enrich itself for it and receive the unfolding which we have seen in life … The simple I is this genus, or the simple universal, for which the differences are no differences, only by being the *negative essence* of the shaped, self-subsistent moments.” — The plurality of self-conscious beings leads further, as was already said, to the still higher universality, rational self-consciousness. Now what is this? Prof. R. answers (p. 241 of the 2nd ed.): “As self-consciousness, spirit must attain the unity of its self both with what is selfless and with every other self; it must also raise the truth which it is for itself to certainty in an objective manner. The unity of consciousness in its universal objectivity and of self-consciousness in the universality of its subjectivity is rationality; for just as the I is nothing other than thought, without the slightest sensible admixture, so too the determinations of objectivity that are universal in and for themselves, quality, quantity, measure, essence, etc., are nothing other than thoughts.” Rationality, however, is “the knowing of oneself in the categories and of everything in them,” and reason in and for itself is, according to p. 238, “the totality of the pure categories in the abstract element of thinking.” In other words: the development of rational self-consciousness is the development of the categories, in which the certainty is given, on the one hand, that they have objective truth, indeed are the truth of the objective world, whereby they contain within themselves, as sublated, the opposition of objectivity and subjectivity and thus also the whole objective world; on the other hand, that they are valid for all self-conscious beings, hence universally valid, whereby the difference of the several subjectivities is sublated. [p. II.38] But in order that, so it is now further taught, when the certainty of I = I, and with it the sublation of the difference between objectivity and subjectivity, is already present, *the categories too*, and with them the conviction of their *validity for all men*, may come about, it is necessary — besides desire and its satisfaction, which we leave aside here — that men enter into a *struggle for life and death* against one another; that they in consequence enter into the relation of *lords and bondsmen*, i.e. slaves, toward one another; further, that the bondsmen cultivate themselves through their labor, and attain *equality* with their lords through *revolt* or *emancipation*. This then yields the mutual *recognition* of them as equal selves, and with it *rational self-consciousness, the categories*.

Against this development I raised a series of objections. I said, in the first place, that dragging in the struggle for life and death, lordship and bondage, and the recognition following from *these* circumstances, is a wholly improper intrusion of heterogeneous objects, whose scientific investigation belongs not to psychology but to the philosophy of history, though many another science would also have a say in it. It seems to me that a glance at the above account of the course of thought according to *Hegel* and *Rosenkranz*, a reminder that what is at issue is the *development of the categories in human minds*, suffices. It is of course well known that in the human mind everything is intimately interconnected; further, that its developments depend on its body, on its external surroundings, on the history of the people to which it belongs. In this way, no doubt, the Hegelian categories too, which at present exercise a power in many heads in Germany, stand in connection with the struggles of a crude past of our people for freedom and slavery. But this holds also of the migration of peoples, of the military and urban life of the Middle Ages, of the Crusades, etc.; nevertheless I do not find that Prof. R. has discussed them. Why not? I asked once before, but it [p. II.39] seems I spoke it into his sick ear. *Hegel* himself treats still other forms of self-consciousness, Stoicism, Skepticism, the unhappy consciousness, and places them in the most intimate connection with those of the struggle, etc. He says (p. 176): “But self-consciousness is all reality, not only *for itself* but *in itself*, only by *becoming* this reality, or rather by proving itself to be such. It proves itself so *along the path* in which, first, in the dialectical movement of meaning, perceiving and the understanding, otherness as *in itself*, and then, in the movement through the independence of consciousness in lordship and bondage, through the thought of freedom, skeptical liberation and the struggle of the absolute liberation of the consciousness divided within itself, otherness, insofar as it is only for *it*, vanishes for *it itself*.” Why does Prof. R. pass over the one set of states, and not the others too? — Yet he was not, after all, entirely mute toward this objection of mine. He says (p. 428): “Indeed, afterwards Herr *Exner* also remarks that the subject properly belongs to the history of philosophy. But I told him that myself beforehand.” This is untrue. On p. 235 he says only that “the details” of it belong there; I, however, maintained: “the whole.” Still, I read the above with pleasure, for it expresses an open agreement. My pleasure grew as I read on: “For here too a confusion still prevails, which I have striven to check as far as I could. A detailed working-through of the philosophy of history, which I finally arrived at for myself in 1841, has made the boundaries between it and psychology still clearer to me.” Naturally I now expected, as the first ripe fruit of that working-through, no less than a complete reworking of the section on self-consciousness, in which the modest conclusion of that passage, “Science does not go in seven-league boots with the individual,” further confirmed me. But when I looked it up, everything had remained as before.

I further objected that it is false that the struggle for life and death, lordship and bondage, revolt or emancipation are *necessary conditions* of mutual recognition, [p. II.40] and thus of rational self-consciousness. However often those struggles may have occurred in reality, it still cannot be proved that they *had to* occur, still less that they took place *everywhere*. Prof. R. has the wild American tribes in view; but whence does he know that *every* people has had such a *prehistory*? Have the members of every family, of every tribe, also fought such struggles among one another? But every student of history will probably be able to adduce more instances than Prof. R. will be able to refute. In the 2nd ed., p. 228, the following new addition is found: “Offense has been taken at this development; but to refute it one would have to prove that more cases were conceivable than a struggle for life and death, or its avoidance through the subjection of one subject to the other.” More cases, in order that recognition may arise? I have proved it. I pointed to human deeds and works, to acts of love, magnanimity, gratitude, to productions of artistic genius, to necessity and to men’s own advantage, which, as is well known, are very powerful peacemakers. I drew attention to the contradiction into which Prof. R. falls when he declares that struggle necessary and yet at the same time asserts that the cultivation of the slave through labor procures him the recognition of the master, and that in more cultivated times the state guarantees mutual recognition, whereby he has himself proved to himself the possibility of a third and a fourth case. I added the question how it is possible, pray, that the state should guarantee to its citizens a certain moment of the mind, mutual recognition, and thereby rational self-consciousness; whether it can also guarantee them understanding, memory, etc. But of all this Prof. R. seems to have heard nothing.

I further said against it that the concept of lordship, slavery and mutual recognition of self-consciousness is empirically conceived incorrectly, and finally, that the Kantian concept of *human dignity* has been *substituted* for the concept of self-consciousness. Prof. R. has heard this latter reproach [p. II.41] entirely, the former at least in part, and with them I am supposed to have betrayed here my virtuosity in misunderstanding. I remarked, namely, against his account of the servile condition, that according to the testimony of experience the slave, on falling into slavery, by no means becomes a newborn child, which can distinguish itself neither from other men nor from the remaining external things. Prof. R. replies that I confuse the *selflessness* of the bondsman which he asserts with *lack of self-consciousness*. But what, then, is self-consciousness? Surely the knowing of oneself, of the I = I, hence the distinguishing of the I from every not-I. What, then, is the self? Prof. R. will allow me to keep to his own words, which stand on p. 220: “Consciousness, as knowing of an other, is the relation in which the knowing subject stands to the objects. Without the *self* this relation would be impossible. The *self* is therefore present in all objective knowing as its bearer, which *Kant* expressed, with respect to presentation, by saying that the I *accompanies* all our presentations. It does not, however, merely accompany them, as a servant, say, accompanies his master, but *it is* itself the incessantly self-renewing act of spirit whereby spirit, *distinguishing itself within itself from itself*, first makes possible the distinguishing of itself from an other which it is not, even though for itself, in the order of development, it apprehends itself as consciousness sooner than as self-consciousness.” The self, then, according to him too, is the I. On p. 221 it is equated with subjectivity, which is the same thing. From this it surely seems to me to follow that *selflessness here is the same as lack of self-consciousness*. On p. 230 it is said: “The bondsman thus comes to himself through the cultivation of labor. Having thus arrived at the *apprehension of his self*, he must recognize his relation to the master as a contradiction of his *self-consciousness*.” But if through labor he comes to himself, to the apprehension of his self, then he *had not apprehended* it before, i.e. he was *without self-consciousness*. Yet Prof. R. assures us that what the bondsman lacks is only the *independence* of self-consciousness, not self-consciousness itself. [p. II.42] What, then, is this independence of self-consciousness? According to p. 229 it is “the infinite relation of its self to itself,” or “self-consciousness independent of all externality, of life itself.” Is it thereby more than self-consciousness? No; for the essence of self-consciousness was placed precisely in its being the certainty of the sublatedness of all objectivity and of its unity with subjectivity, and already at the stage of desire, which was surely not foreign to the bondsman before his enslavement, spirit had found itself to be the absolute power over all objectivity. Between the consciousness of the understanding and rational self-consciousness Prof. R. himself knows, as an intermediate stage, only self-consciousness in itself. Since, then, the bondsman has not yet attained rational self-consciousness, already possessed self-consciousness before his enslavement, and yet has lost something of his former possessions in bondage, this can only be self-consciousness. That would, to be sure, be a step backward, a “falling down” of spirit, which, however, as is well known, is quite admissible in the system. My objection therefore stands. I did not, however, assert that Prof. R. really conceives of the slave as a being without self-consciousness; rather, I declared this impossible, and *for that very reason* assumed the substitution of another concept. But I was entitled to point out that at this place his concepts gape apart a little, and that he would do well to *insert* a tenable *connecting link* between self-consciousness in itself and rational self-consciousness, for which the dialectical method will surely be willing. — I then censured as conflicting with experience the assertion that masters regard their slaves who, shunning the struggle, submit, as animals. Prof. R. replies (p. 412): “The masters did not regard the slaves as *animals*, but partly as only *little* better, partly even as still *lower*.” But the question was not *how highly* they regarded them, but *as what*. For a particular work one can often use a machine better than a man; nevertheless one regards the machine as a machine. Prof. R. does remark, [p. II.43] after all (p. 226), that the struggle for life and death is necessary so that men may not “take an ape for a man.” Whoever, then, shunned the struggle and submitted does not yet count as a man for his master; he has not yet distinguished himself from the ape; but apes are surely animals.

In the same way I contradicted the view that the struggle for life and death, wherever it arose, came about *in order to test by it* whether the one fought against had *self-consciousness*; that the slave, until he has done slave labor for a longer time, is always *without rational self-consciousness*; that the master *finds lacking* in the bondsman rational self-consciousness, Hegelian self-consciousness, the categories with their sublated objectivity, and *for that reason* keeps him as a slave, while he himself *possesses* it, and the like. The great multitude has never troubled itself about such purely metaphysical matters; least of all, at present, would the American savages do so, whom Prof. R. adduces as examples of that stage of mental development, but who, as is well known, would rather fight over a disputed hunting ground or something similar than out of curiosity whether their opponents have developed the categories.

All the talk of the struggle for the mutual recognition of self-consciousness and of the rational self-consciousness following from it is devoid of sense if the concept of self-consciousness that has been set up is strictly adhered to. This concept of the certainty of I = I, of the knowing of oneself in the categories and of everything in them, has a purely theoretical object, the recognition of which entirely lacks the practical interest that underlies the struggle for life and death. It was not for the recognition that in their thinking they had risen to certain speculative universalities that men in more cultivated times fought; rather, what they *at times* wanted to compel was the recognition that others have *duties* toward them, because they themselves are not, say, thinking beings, which is not decisive here, but *willing* beings; they sought to bring about reciprocal relations of willing and acting, founded on the reciprocal conviction that to all of them, as willing beings, [p. II.44] a *progress in moral perfection* is imposed through this their nature. With this the Kantian concept of innate human dignity has presented itself to us. Of the consciousness of *this dignity* I said that it could be lacking or be lost in the condition of extreme bondage, but it *need* not be; that *this* dignity of the bondsman could be misjudged by the master, but it *need* not be, since self-interest is capable of maintaining the relation against better conviction; that the consciousness *of it* could be acquired through labor and cultivation in the condition of bondage, but also *before* it and *without* it. And precisely because only through this concept did sense seem to me to come into that account of the matter, I reproached it with the *substitution* of this concept. Prof. R. opposes a bare contradiction. I had rather expected him to say that that concept was not inserted into that of rational self-consciousness, but lay within it; for this, he would say, is the recognition of the reason common to all men, in which human dignity precisely consists. To that I would have replied: the word reason is a wide garment, rich in folds, in which much can be hidden and occasionally pulled out; but only what has been reached by the scientific path has worth in science. The Hegelian Logic does indeed have the category of the good. But the good there is reason objectifying itself, and reason is the system of the categories of being, of essence and of the concept, by which the concept of morality can be determined neither immediately nor mediately. For the genuine concept of moral dignity the whole Hegelian system has no place, even though the names ethical life and morality are by no means lacking. Its object is that which *is*. The moral, too, is here and there; but whoever apprehends it only as such does not apprehend it as *moral*, for the *immoral is* as well. The moral is characterized by the fact that it *ought* to be; but the ought Hegelians have publicly rejected as something with which their science has nothing to do. Where the word is still used, this happens, say, in the sense of the necessity of the transition of one moment of the concept into another, whereby every trace of its moral [p. II.45] significance has vanished. Of the psychology I believe I have shown that by the scientific route it does not even arrive at the concept of practical spirit, of willing, and likewise that its freedom is no moral freedom. For it is a freedom of the concept, as of that which sublates the subordinate moments within itself and develops itself, a freedom which belongs even to every concept. No wonder, then, that the Hegelian School has so far shown itself little capable of cultivating the field of practical philosophy, and that this field, amid the school’s otherwise so rich literature, lies almost fallow and is forgotten. — So much by way of elucidating my objections that have been accused of misunderstanding. They have made little impression on my opponent; yet it would be ungrateful not to acknowledge that they have borne at least One fruit. Prof. R. wrote in his 1st ed., p. 214: “With a *ravishing* mastery of dialectic *Hegel* has presented in the elaborated Phenomenology the struggle of self-consciousness for its recognition.” In the 2nd ed. (p. 232) the “ravishment” has vanished, and only a “With *his* mastery” etc. has remained; so I have not labored entirely in vain.

But perhaps Prof. R. will find that my present justification, too, against his reproaches of misunderstanding rests only on misunderstandings, and he has fresh cause to complain of the toughness of my understanding. Now in order to defend in some measure not myself but the poor persecuted common understanding, I will briefly show that it is wrong to saddle *it* with all the misunderstandings of which I may have been guilty. I mean, namely, to point out that misunderstandings can be found even in men of lofty reason, who are therefore beyond the weaknesses of the common understanding, and that not, say, with regard to a book of many sheets composed according to the dialectical method and in Hegelian terminology, but with regard to my feeble “brochure written in the most comprehensible popular style.” For this purpose it might suffice to point back to the reproaches which Prof. R. makes against my intentions, and to their groundlessness set out above. I shall be permitted to give them [p. II.46] the mildest interpretation by tracing them back to gross misunderstandings. Others, however, can also be pointed out which have scientific views as their object. Thus he imputes to me (p. 378) that I hate everything antinomic, and on that account wonders at my praise of *Herbart*. Does that hatred, pray, follow from this praise? or does he infer it from my remark that, if certain contradictions were really inescapable, they would indeed have to be developed further? Or from the other, that a philosophy which does not *get out* of contradictions cannot possibly be the true one? — On p. 382 I seem to him to want “physiology and pathology treated along with psychology, entirely within it. For the latter, however, they are only preliminary sciences, necessary conditions.” To whom does he say this? from what does he infer that? I simply cannot bear the “blunders” *when* one does speak of physiological or pathological matters; and when on p. 9 of my critique I raise physiological questions, I thereby merely indicate what anyone *would have* to look to who really wished to explain that which *Michelet* undertakes to explain in a certain place. That this must be done in psychology has never seemed probable to me. — According to p. 384 I am supposed to have expected philosophers to make out *by ear*, with their eyes bound, how the objects around them *look*. That would surely be too silly even for the toughest understanding. But Prof. R. had called the sense of hearing the *deepest* sense, which opens up to us the *inwardness* of things, and thereupon I had asked whether he would venture, with his eyes bound, to guess even those properties of *sounding* things which a boy trained in the Pestalozzian manner might be able to state? For after all one hears only the sound, and from it one at most *infers*, in consequence of earlier experiences, other properties of things, inner ones too. — According to p. 415 I am supposed to have substituted the concept of conviction for that of feeling, when I say: “How many feelings *and* convictions, which are after all also moments of spirit, does a child have which, once they are destroyed, the man is never again able to produce in himself?” [p. II.47] By this, however, I mean only that what is asserted holds *not merely* of feelings *but also* of other states of the soul, e.g. of conviction, and this was, it seems to me, not exactly hard to understand. — On the same page we read: “Herr *Exner* thinks that distraction and absorption are *kinds* of self-feeling,” whereas I say on p. 20: “The unimpeded fluidity of the acts of intelligence, the free alternation between absorption and distraction, *are not states of self-feeling*; this is *not present in every one of them*; it *can merely be added* to it.” — On p. 425 one may read: “Herr *Exner*, who cannot reconcile himself to the thought of a contingent necessity, gives it to be understood in veiled terms that the Hegelian doctrine (of the reproduction of presentations) could hold only of the drivelling or the thought-paralysis of the insane.” The “contingent necessity,” in which the concept suddenly takes leave of its senses and turns dumb and stupid, I really cannot understand; but precisely because *my* necessity of the laws of reproduction is not a contingent one, i.e. none at all, but a true necessity, I openly give it to be understood that the Hegelian doctrine could *not even* hold of the drivelling and the thought-paralysis of the insane. These citations could be greatly multiplied; but it seems to me they suffice, and, to speak seriously, I do not want them explained by my opponent’s reason, but by a certain superficiality with which, as it seems, he often not only writes but also reads.

Since Prof. R. holds my tough understanding-mindedness incapable of judging speculative matters, and feels and recognizes my virtuosity in misunderstanding with due vividness, I can very well explain to myself two further phenomena in his “Refutation”: the one, that while he does contest many of my objections, he obviously does not do so in earnest, since he rather in fact *slips over* them; and the other, that he *wholly ignores* so many of my counter-remarks. No doubt he was of the view that what comes from such an opponent deserves nothing better. To make clearer what I mean, I will adduce some examples.

[p. II.48] The very first section, which follows the introduction of the “Refutation” and bears the title “Dualism of Exner’s Critique,” will serve this purpose not badly. There it is lamented that the understanding lives in a constant *dualism* of the *intelligible* and the *sensuous*. As evidence, disconnected passages from my critique are cited, accompanied by remarks, and the conclusion is made by the following words: “How then should speculation ever be able to satisfy Herr *Exner*?”

“If it speaks of sensuousness, he begs it not to forget spirit, after all.”

“If it speaks of spirit, he smiles at the dreamers who fancy they can manage without the body.”

“If, finally, it speaks of the unity between body and spirit, he remarks that body and spirit are after all two, and complains of the violence and confusion of speculative thinking.”

And herewith a series of very different objections of my critique is refuted in the simplest way. Whether as thoroughly as simply, we shall see.

I had remarked on the *arbitrariness* with which one takes the same thing as One, and then again as a duality, just as one happens to need it. Thus nature and spirit, I said, are taken as One, as soul, and then again as a duality which *determines* itself reciprocally. But if they really are two, I said, then they are never One. Passing over the actual reproach of arbitrariness, Prof. R. singles out only the last sentence, accusing my understanding of dualism. The dualism I take upon myself; indeed, I voluntarily heighten it into a pluralism; for what I assert of this duality, I assert also of every other duality. Formal logic does indeed make unities out of pluralities, but only by leaving out the distinguishing marks; such unities, however, Prof. R. does not mean, but dialectical ones. I regret that he has not been willing to correct my doubt with regard to *the one at hand*, since after all *dialectic is supposed to prove itself in its application*.

I further asked on p. 74 of my critique: “What, further, are we to think of under that being-separated, having-become-free of [p. II.49] spirit from the body, supposing that it continues to form an opposition to its former being-bound *to*, being-poured-out *through* the body? After all, no thought walks about on earth without a body.” Prof. R. again cites only the last sentence, as a proof of my understanding-mindedness, “which shows itself incensed when speculation, for the moment of the antithetical diremption of the concept, drives the *opposition* of nature and spirit as far as the *abstraction* of the one side from the other.” Herewith I am put in my place. But it seems to me that precisely the justification for such an abstraction *in the case at hand* was in question.

I also asked how it comes about, then, that precisely subjective spirit, this negation of nature, is in fact so intimately connected with what it negates, the body, that when my body climbs into the mail coach and makes a journey, my spirit must accompany it, whereas with other negations this is, as is well known, not the case at all. Prof. R. singles out only the passage that speaks of the mail coach, and says nothing whatever about it. The question seems to him already sufficiently solved by the preceding reproach of abstract understanding-mindedness. My understanding, however, is still so weak as to assume that a more careful investigation of it would not be unimportant, if what were at issue were the application of the dialectical method to concrete objects.

In my critique I undertook to show that the concept of practical spirit obtained dialectically, in the psychologies examined, from the concept of theoretical spirit has been surreptitiously obtained. It was, further, one of the chief reproaches I made against the psychology of the Hegelian School that its concepts of the individual mental states correspond poorly to what experience declares about them, and I demonstrated this, among other things, also in the concept of willing that it puts forward. This, namely, is declared to be thinking which is at the same time the capacity to transform itself into an objectivity. I remarked against this that willing is something other than thinking, because the thinking of a murder is something other than the willing of it; that thinking in itself, like willing in itself, is by no means yet the [p. II.50] capacity to transform itself into an objectivity, as it is meant here, since for this the body, and frequently external tools and favorable circumstances, are required, which only together with those yield this capacity; that further, even if the deed actually occurs, the will has nevertheless not in the proper sense transformed itself into objectivity, because only its effects stand there as external objects; that sensations and other mental states, too, produce effects in the external world through facial expressions and involuntary movements, and would thus, if the expression were left so indeterminate, also transform themselves into an objectivity, although no one would call this a deed; that, finally, least of all may it be asserted, as is done here, that the will always results from reason, since unfortunately it only all too often has nothing in common with it. What does Prof. R. reply to this? He admits that the willing and the thinking of a deed are different; wherein, he does not say. He asserts that the will does actually transform itself into objectivity; how that is possible, reason sees quite well, only not the understanding. With these two remarks and a few mocking turns of phrase to adorn them, everything is nicely disposed of.

Prof. R. continues: “Given the incessant need to hold up sensuousness against the understanding, the outer against the inner, and conversely, every determination in which the factor of sensuousness or of abstract understanding-mindedness is lacking is obscure to Herr *Exner*. P. 36: Before such wisdom criticism falls silent.” No one would guess from these last words, cited from my essay, how meaningfully they are placed here. Prof. R. had declared attention to be that state in which intelligence gives itself the direction toward its feeling; I, however, had used this explanation as another example by which to demonstrate the incapacity of the School in grasping empirically given concepts. To invalidate this argumentation of mine, he continues:

“When I say that attending consists in the direction which intelligence gives itself toward itself in its feeling, Herr *Exner* asks loc. cit.: ‘what goes on in spirit when [p. II.51] it, as we say, gives itself a direction toward something?’ I answered to this: precisely this, that it gives itself a direction, is the process that I call attending. That is too little for Herr *Exner*. ‘Intelligence,’ he says wittily, ‘is no astronomer who points his telescope.’”

This I had really said. The “direction” is a metaphor, taken from spatial relations; to explain mental states by metaphors means to leave them unexplained. This, surely, should be nothing new to Prof. R., and so my objection ought not to have struck him as strange. This determination is obscure to me not because “the factor of sensuousness is lacking,” but because it *alone* is present. Prof. R. himself cites a work of *Herbart’s* on attention. If he knows more of it than the title, he ought to have hesitated to try to dispatch with a metaphor a subject on which our science possesses such investigations. And what, now, has my remark brought about? Nothing but the following reply: “Oh yes, oh yes, Herr *Exner*! There is something of this operation (of the astronomer) in the act of attending.” This is naive indeed, but not the worst; the latter he passes over in silence. My critique (p. 36), namely, goes on: “Let us hear further how attention comes about. I must, it says on p. 246, continually *give myself a push* within myself in order to maintain myself in this direction of my intelligence.” And upon this follows my remark: “Before such wisdom criticism falls silent.” I confess that in that explanation of attention by pushing I again miss the factor of any understanding-mindedness at all. Prof. R. seems himself to have been ashamed of it in this place, for he acts as if he knew nothing of it; my remark about criticism falling silent, on the other hand, he does single out, but places it in isolation there where he is only just beginning to discuss this whole subject, and where it is presented to the reader, who does not know what it refers to, as the droll exclamation of astonishment of a tough understanding.

Instead of elucidating the “direction” and the “push,” Prof. R. slips over to the next point of my critique. [p. II.52] He asks: “You take offense at the expression feeling?” The following passage of my critique really does read thus:

“The distinguishing, mediated by attention, of the content originally existing in feeling is further called *intuiting*. What sort of mental act distinguishing is remains unsaid here, as before. Naturally one recalls that what one usually intuits is not so much one’s feeling as paintings, houses, landscapes, in short external things: one therefore asks how the above is meant? The answer we read on p. 248: ‘In directing myself toward myself, I grasp my feeling and throw it out into time and space.’ If, astonished, we ask further how this throw is possible, we hear: ‘through the infinite elasticity of our self.’”

To characterize the utter worthlessness of such a psychological treatment as it deserves, I added: “The writer of these lines confesses that he blushed with shame when he read this. For he too, after all, counts himself among the philosophical public, before which one may venture to babble like a child, while close beside it the natural sciences deliver their well-ordered discourses with convincing earnestness. And so the child, too, is often enough put in the corner by the men, and, as circumstances require, even gets the rod now and then.” Prof. R. ignores all this. Not the throw, not the elasticity, not the push concerns him; rather, as if the talk were still exclusively of the attention discussed earlier, he assures us that he spoke only of the *origin* of *attention*, which does indeed lie in feeling. In corroboration of his view he appeals to a passage of *Herbart’s*, according to which, in a problem, what had been *perceptible to feeling* only as a difficulty can now, through analysis, be sharply *thought* as a contradiction. Prof. R. has a peculiar misfortune with that man; he need only speak of him, and he has already committed some clumsiness. *Herbart* holds that a feeling *may* well precede attention, but *need* not, and least of all does he consider feeling to be the necessary *object* of attention.

[p. II.53] And because I do not most obediently put up with those arbitrary unities and pluralities, those contradictions between theory and experience, that actual transformation of spirit into objectivity, the inner direction, the inner push, the throw out into space and time, the infinite elasticity of the self and the like, I am a dualist, an abstract man of the understanding, and herewith refuted as well. When one reads this, one falls into the unpleasant dilemma either of doubting the sharpness of the opponent’s understanding, or of presupposing in him the intention, by plucking about at individual pieces of my objections and passing sweeping judgment on them while passing over their actual substance, simply to make the public believe that he “has not shrunk from looking his opponent in the face,” and has refuted him entirely and thoroughly.

If herewith enough examples have been given of *evading* the objections under the form of *engaging* with them, and yet all of them only from the first section of the “Refutation” following the introduction, so that 22 other sections have remained unconsidered, I may add that examples of the complete *ignoring* of not unimportant objections are at least as easy to find. I refer to what I remarked on pp. 10 and 11 of my critique about the concepts of seeing and of tone, on p. 19 about self-feeling, on pp. 20 and 21 about the concept of mental illness and its kinds, on p. 27 about self-consciousness, on p. 38 about recollection, on pp. 44 and 46 about thinking and willing, on pp. 50 and 51 about desire and inclination, on p. 52 about affect and passion, etc. Almost all these remarks come down to this: that the dialectical concepts determine the objects named contrary to experience, and are therefore incorrect. Now Prof. R. has rightly said that he would have to write a book if he wanted to answer all my objections; but he has in fact written one, his 2nd edition of the Psychology. There he had to secure his exposition against those objections, in whatever form this might be done. Half of my polemic rests on the asserted contrariety to experience of the psychological concepts of that [p. II.54] work; if one wanted to invalidate it — and that one wanted to is shown by the *existence* of the “Refutation” — then one had to engage with what matters; those points are “essential” ones. Prof. R. does, to be sure, also say: “A book has a certain right to continue in its original form,” and on that account he avoids a “more deeply penetrating transformation” of it; to me, however, this truth is new and by no means evident. It rests, no doubt, on the well-known concepts of right according to which every thing, however constituted, has a right to be if it is, and to which recently even a Socrates had to succumb. I by no means demand, however, a complete transformation of the Hegelian psychology, but only that it take those attacks into account for its own salvation. Or, to be frank, I do not demand even that. Neither the psychology nor the philosophy of nature of *Hegel* could continue to exist if they took experience into consideration earnestly and honestly; but self-destruction cannot be expected of anyone. So nothing remains for them but, like certain birds before the enemy, to shut their eyes really tight. Prof. R. will perhaps again call this a banal phrase; but he has done nothing that could invalidate it. Rather, wherever in our polemic it is a matter of holding his concepts up to the standard of experience, he walks around softly, as if on tiptoe, and seeks to get away as soon as possible unnoticed; but where he can speak of the power of the concept, of absolute negativity, of contingent necessity, of finite and infinite infinity and similar great things, there he at once sits high on horseback and trots along like a king. Indeed, he then rides the wind; who could catch up with him when it pleases him to gallop?

A not uninteresting trait of character of the “Refutation,” which will hardly escape any reader, is, further, the alternation between mild concessions and defiant rebuffs, between the crestfallen modesty of a timid inquirer and the most presumptuous confidence of knowledge, which hurls the adversary into the dust with a kick. Its polemic resembles a snake which now lies flat on the ground, now again, at its full [p. II.55] length, darts high up into the air, almost as if it were wounded. Defiance and confidence form the prevailing tone, and ample care has been taken that they need not first be proved here; the other is interspersed. How modest it sounds, does it not, when Prof. R. (p. 379) says with reference to himself: “Nothing is more pernicious for science than that bad haughtiness which lacks the courage to admit its errors, because what matters to it is not the truth but the appearance of being in the right;” or when he (p. 399) opines that “in the Hegelian School the period of the first enthusiasm, anticipating everything in the joy of the insight attained, has now been followed by a period of hesitant reflection;” or (p. 428): “Science does not go in seven-league boots with the individual.” Even thanks are confessed to be owed to me; for on p. 396 it says, with reference to my remarks on the differences between *Michelet*, *Erdmann* and Prof. R.: “Whoever is not wholly possessed by the demon of school-making will only be grateful to him for this unsparing but highly instructive and stimulating compilation.” A few lines earlier, however, he declared that I had thereby merely “exploited with much formal acumen” his own preface; thus he himself, naturally, owes me no thanks; he merely lays them upon the consciences of his friends.

In the face of my reproach of neglecting experience, he declares (p. 379) that he must really account for himself on *three* points, which he does not wish to pass over in silence. In consequence of this accounting, summer and winter, as nearness to and distance from the sun, are removed from the second edition, because this is, he says, after all a somewhat too daring trope, and the same befalls the lunar center which formerly held the sleepwalkers fast on the roofs. The third point, the explanation of presentiments from electrical influences, is not given up, but declared confirmed by *Moser’s* discovery of invisible light. I have nothing against that; my objection, however, was that nothing is gained for the *conceptual penetration* which the School lays claim to, if one [p. II.56] explains an empirical riddle by an empirical riddle. Or has Prof. R. already *comprehended* electricity? Then I only wonder that he did not make that discovery *before Moser*. But it seems to me that it will not be disagreeable to him if this question is not pressed further. He concludes his speech on the three points with the exclamation: “So these would be my heresies against experience!” for as regards other points, of which, to be sure, the whole first half of my little work is full, “Herr *Exner* likes, with everything he does not comprehend, to assure us at once that it conflicts with all sound experience.” One sees that it took battering rams as thick as the lunar center and the nearness to the sun to break through the wall of this self-sufficiency completely at even one small spot.

I might almost also claim for myself the honor of another concession. I had, namely, criticized the dichotomy of theoretical and practical spirit, and demanded a trichotomy in the name of the method. This is now present in the 2nd ed. The third part of the work has received a third section, “free spirit,” though only a short one, and one, as it seems, composed in such haste that it was forgotten to include it in the table of contents. Yet the merit of having occasioned it does not properly belong to me. Prof. R. has rather, since he wrote the first edition, “seen that *Hegel* found it necessary in the third edition of the Encyclopedia” to add that part; for this reason, and because he had in any case already “given it earlier in substance,” he has now made this “improvement.” Thus at least the justice of my demand is acknowledged. A more important concession is made to that part of my critique which shows that the dialectical development only gets further by drawing in and using, in a concealed manner, what is given in experience, whereby, however, it becomes just as unfaithful to itself as it mutilates and falsifies experience. It is said on p. 383: “that speculation always has outside itself a great remainder of experience not yet worked up by it, and that this often appears in it very *inorganically*, drawn in more tentatively at first [p. II.57] than comprehended with decisiveness, is grounded in the nature of the matter.” He then consoles himself with this nature of the matter, but nevertheless admits “that the errors of speculation in working up the empirical remain errors.” Naturally it depends on how great they are, and on this something more will have to be added later.

It is another concession when Prof. R. replies to a detailed censure (p. 380): “On revising my Psychology I found that the whole chapter on the dream-life of the soul suffers from an indefiniteness of expression which often does not let the *actual phenomenon* and the *opinions* about it stand clearly apart. As far as was possible without recasting the whole, I have now sought to remedy this circumstance.” Only I could have wished that Prof. R. would extend the same acknowledgment to his doctrines on the causes of diseases of the soul, on physiognomy and phrenology as well, and in doing so separate more exactly not merely phenomenon from opinions, but also opinions from opinions. It must, to be sure, again be acknowledged that his “refutation” (pp. 419 and 420), taking this wish to heart, says a good deal about those subjects very correctly and sensibly; but it also brings some things that are questionable. For with regard to those subjects I had remarked that Prof. R. likes to step in between the contending parties with the most sovereign mien of the judge, and immediately afterward to withdraw with a submissive bow to both sides, granting both of them to be right, and asserting even the most contradictory opinions close beside one another. To this he retorts on p. 419:

“On the field of *political* deed the poet thunders at us:

Speak like a man, for *or* against, / Only the watchword: slave *or* free.

In the domain of thinking, on the other hand, *mediation* reigns; and the *extreme*, if it is to be true not merely *polemically* but *in and for* itself, is an *error*. The dispassionateness of science must not fear being branded heretical [p. II.58] for lacking the decisiveness of *sharpness*. Its sharpness is a *two-edged sword*. It must, from the

Neither — Nor, through the / Either — Or, to the / Both — And,

advance step by step, and beware only of the slackness of the leathern *also*, *also*, whose comfortable loquacity, to be sure, costs no racking of brains.”

This passage contains a quantum of scientific resignation and modesty that seems to me excessive. For it seems to me that precisely on the field of political deed, where it is a matter of respect for the rights of others, no one is entitled to want to carry through his view unconditionally against the view of those who think otherwise but have equal rights; that he must rather put up with mediation in what is done; whereas, on the contrary, on the field of science decisiveness and sharpness have their undisputed right, and alone are of significance. This is naturally not to say that, if extremes are present in science, the truth must always lie with one of them; it may also lie in the middle. But wherever it may be, it can only be as a sharply determined, exactly delimited thought. But does Prof. R. show sharp thinking, a happy scientific mediation of extremes, when he says: *Heinroth* is right, for the spirit *can* fall ill, and *Friedreich* is also right, for the spirit *cannot* fall ill? Or when he makes similar pronouncements on physiognomy and craniology, such as are cited from his work on p. 25 of my critique? How does this Both — And differ from the “leathern” also, also? In the 1st ed., p. 172, a passage belonging here reads: “But however right *Lichtenberg* is, *Lavater* was nevertheless *not wrong*;” in the 2nd ed. (p. 188) it has received the following form: “But however right *Lichtenberg* is, *Lavater* was nevertheless not *absolutely* wrong.” Not to be wrong, however, means roughly as much as to be right; not to be absolutely wrong, on the other hand, as much as to be wrong, yet not entirely. Although, accordingly, [p. II.59] this principal passage has been turned in the 2nd ed., by the insertion, almost into its direct opposite, all the rest, added for its grounding and elucidation, has nevertheless remained literally unchanged. In a quite similar manner the 2nd ed. (pp. 192 and 193) transforms the sentences: “But the hard bone *must* tell the truth;” “*the* significance of the protuberances on the cerebrum or cerebellum cannot be denied,” quite simply thus: “But, *as it seems*, the hard bone *must* tell the truth;” “*a* significance of the protuberances on the cerebrum or cerebellum cannot be denied.” All the rest remains as it was. If this does not point to a completely shambling, “leathern” form of thought without sharpness, to pure “comfortable loquacity,” then such a thing could hardly exist any longer. It seems that the necessity of having to defend himself against certain objections has *here* led Prof. R. to renounce scientific sharpness in the name of science; but if one notices on how many other occasions criticism had cause to lament the lack of it, one might almost assume that he believed in earnest that he could do without it for the *whole of science*. This concession, however, counts for more than I would ever demand.

Prof. R. complains (p. 382) that I “mercilessly pursue every metaphorical expression” in him. He thus concedes the existence of metaphors, and I do not deny my intolerance toward them when, like, say, the “direction” discussed above under attention, or the “push,” and the “elasticity of spirit,” they take the place of sharp concepts in science. But he also loves *hyperboles*, and it is only fine that he likewise admits them, albeit in a very lofty tone. Relying on the concept of reason as the *universal* truth, he exclaims: “The tremendous thing in this standpoint lies in this, that the subject says to the world: thou art mine!” I was reminded here of the assertion of the absolute power of the subject over objectivity, by virtue of which every desire *must* find its satisfaction, as well as of *Michelet’s* doctrine that everything spatial and temporal in every [p. II.60] moment is ideally *present* in the soul. I thought in this connection of the countless longing for knowledge and other possessions that languishes *unappeased* in men, and of how often the daily needs of life have been felt and lamented by noble spirits as a sad *limit* and a heavy *burden*, and I found that assertion — ridiculous. To this Prof. R. replies (pp. 22 ff.), though somewhat in veiled terms: I beg your pardon; I only meant to say that man has reason, and is thereby able, to be sure, to cognize a good deal of the world, and in future ever more. Against the assertion that reason is the power of the world, and that therefore nothing in it can conflict with rational self-consciousness, I had remarked that there is, after all, really something irrational in the world. He replies in less veiled terms: I beg your pardon; I only meant to say that we cognize even the irrationality of things through our reason. Against the doctrine of the harmony of all reason, in the sense of an *identity* of objectivity and subjectivity, I had asked whether he knew, say, what lands there are at the South Pole, what progressive motion our sun has, and the like. He replies: I beg your pardon; I only meant to say that our reason knows the universal of the categories, but not the particular. Against such enlightenments there is nothing to be said. But why the mighty hyperboles, the tremendous bombast, if their kernel is such common truths?

At times Prof. R. even becomes quite subdued, and almost despondent. Absolute knowing hides behind authorities, and seeks alms at strangers’ doors. With regard to the abandoned doctrine of the force of the moon’s center he says (p. 380): “I had read it in books of the school of Schubert and Görres, which loves the geocentric, heliocentric and selenocentric standpoint, etc., and among the many wild things that the astrology of this school contains, that *nonsense* had really still seemed ingenious to me.” I had shown some surprise when he inferred “the modesty of the Germans, which rests on the quiet ground of strength,” from the [p. II.61] national dress of the German peasants, blue outside, red inside, since I know of many other colors on German peasants as well; he now says on p. 382: “Is it my fault that the German peasant supplies such correct examples for *Goethe’s Theory of Colors*.” In order to support his explanations of presentiments from magnetic influence, he says (p. 381): “I find that sober empirical psychologists, like *Fischer* in Switzerland, who are anything but Hegelians, hold this kind of rapport to be *not at all impossible*.” Because I criticized his definition of feeling, he assures us (p. 413) that he “compared it with the definitions in other psychologies;” he even cites a long definition from *Scheidler’s* Psychology, “the first that came to hand,” and concludes with the assurance that the Hegelian one is better. In the same way he relates (p. 391), in order to justify himself against my criticism of his descriptions of the states of the disposition, that he took up the books of *Platner*, *Carus* the elder, *Stiedenroth*, *Beneke* and others, and compared their portrayals with those of *Hegel*, but had to award the prize to the latter. I am far from taking these utterances amiss in Prof. R.; I rather value them as a proof that his circle of thought has not yet suffered that ossification which makes all attention to the views of others impossible. But a knowing that makes so many *confessions* and *concessions*, that occasionally takes *nonsense* for *ingenious*, that props itself up on the testimony of others to its *possibility*, and even still feels the need to strengthen itself by the sight of others’ *weakness*, is no absolute knowing, and has no right to pass sentence, in a tone of arrogance and contempt, on the opponents who have brought it to such utterances.

This, then, is the character of the “refutation” which Prof. R., with some pomp — for he announces it already on the title page of his book with an expression of disdain — believed he had to set against my critique. Its presumption, with which it lays claim to a reason of its own and seeks to clear itself a broad path by the abusive talk of tenacious understanding-mindedness, its comfortable reproach of misunderstanding without any serious [p. II.62] attempt to prove it or to make it impossible in future; its apparent refutation of some objections while really evading them; its total ignoring of so many others; its extorted modesty and admission, and then again its haughty defiance: will all this be able to save the Hegelian psychology? We must wait and see. — For now it is my duty to remark that Prof. R. has not evaded or passed over all my objections; I therefore have to go into the way in which he has sought to invalidate the principal objections of my critique.

First, however, I must still explain myself on an incidental point. In my critique I had laid some weight on *Herbart’s* psychological achievements, in contrast to those of the Hegelian school. It was meant as an attempt to open a discussion in this direction which promised results not unimportant for science. For where the strongest objects advance into combat against one another, a decisive battle may soonest be expected. Prof. R. goes into my intention (pp. 376 ff.) to the extent that he formally summons me to an examination regarding my own knowledge of *Herbart*; regarding his own opinions, however, he refers me to pp. 457–475 of his History of the Kantian Philosophy. The account of the Herbartian philosophy contained in this work was, soon after its appearance, publicly shown — from the author’s closest surroundings, and by a man to whom he will hardly deny exact insight into the subject — to display the grossest ignorance of the system described. How can he even dare to refer me to what is said there? This belongs among the manifold depressing experiences which the literature of the present compels us to undergo. Neither the conspicuous superficiality with which Prof. R. worked up his psychology, and on account of which I reproached him with dilettantism, nor the wounding tone of condescension that reigns in his refutation, not even the hardly conscientious attempt to play the debate over onto the contemptible field of personalities, has convinced me so much as this reference has, [p. II.63] that he wishes less to earn the applause of those competent to judge through the gain of honest inquiry than rather, by asserting and pronouncing before the eyes of the ignorant, to salvage a paltry semblance. For it is hardly possible that he acted *bona fide* in the case above. In that History of the Kantian Philosophy he sets up, to mention only a few things, the following as *Herbart’s* doctrines: being, reality, quality are the same; the negation of the disturbance of a real being is a motion; the method of relations has its name because philosophy works up the empirical metaphysically; the aesthetic judgment must, in order to have practical value, become an unconditional one; benevolence is trust, credit; the Idea of inner freedom is the condition of the four other concepts, of right, of equity, of benevolence and of perfection; *Kant* determined the will formally, whereas in *Herbart* precisely the material element of the practical predominates. Even in the 2nd ed. it is asserted that the method of relations is in *Herbart* the general philosophical method, and that every presentation in the soul is disturbed by every other. Here it is a matter of facts, and those adduced are all false. What *Herbart* taught on these points is by no means disputed or doubtful; it lies before us so clearly that to want to prove it would be just as superfluous as to prove that *Euclid* knew the Pythagorean theorem, or that *Kant* asserted a transcendental freedom. One need only read it up. If in the Hegelian school things have come so far, according to the latest results, that no two pupils any longer agree on which are the principal doctrines of the master and which is their proper sense, the same by no means holds with regard to *Herbart*, who thought clearly and knew how to give the most definite expression to what he thought. The points above all concern at the same time principal points of *Herbart’s* metaphysics, practical philosophy and psychology; for anyone who is still so deeply in the dark about them as Prof. R., every scientific debate about that system unfortunately still comes too early. With regard to *Herbart*, Prof. R. has for the present only to fulfil the duty of an [p. II.64] honest pupil: he has to read, to learn, and when he has done this properly, then perhaps it might be possible to talk with him further about this subject.

### Die Haupteinwendungen gegen die Hegelsche Psychologie. — The Principal Objections to the Hegelian Psychology.

If there were no plants on earth, there would also be no botany; in the same way we would have no psychology without the empirically given manifold of mental states. To comprehend *these* becomes a need as education advances, and the science thus arisen is precisely what has been called psychology. Perhaps a psychology, a botany, could be set up which occupied itself not with actual mental states, with actual plants, but with merely possible ones, and constructed these *a priori*; notwithstanding this, science would not be released from the duty of drawing what is actually given into the circle of its inquiries as well, and of opening up the understanding of it. This, rather, we demand of it under all circumstances; the former only when it has first proved to us the possibility and necessity of such an undertaking. But if it is to grasp the empirically given states, one might think it goes without saying that it has to do this *exactly* as well, that it has therefore to *describe* them, that it has to give *distinct concepts* of those states. At least this is otherwise usually held to be a condition of scientific character; the warrant for an exception would therefore have to be proved. So I had accordingly also set the Hegelian psychology, from the outset, the demand *that the concepts of mental states must correspond exactly to what is empirically given, and must be made distinct by descriptions or definitions*.

The correctness of the first demand is acknowledged; only incidentally, to be sure, and in passing, which could hardly be appropriate to its significance in this science. It is otherwise [p. II.65] with the second, which insists on exact descriptions and definitions. Prof. R. says in this respect, after reproaching me with the Kantian *form of the understanding*: “All distinctions are *fixed* in it as indissoluble determinacies, independent for themselves. But such a fixing contradicts the finitude of determination. What is untrue in it is therefore made good again by afterward *forgetting* the *separation* first set up, without noticing it. Or the understanding also deceives itself about its necessary contradictions by giving *other names* to the same determinations when they recur, and then actually taking them for different ones. The understanding therefore demands for psychology a *description of the states of the soul*. It wants a neat *separation* of them; the *passing over* of the one into the other, the *motion* and *change*, or even their entanglement in *necessary* contradictions, it does not hold to be objective facts, but ascribes such facts to a *sick* thinking contrary to experience.” Exact descriptions of the states of the soul, then, can according to Prof. R. *not* be given, because the determinations of spirit are always engaged in passing over, in motion and change, and therefore cannot be fixed. But if I *fix* a certain determination A *in my thoughts*, i.e. *think it exactly*, have I thereby declared that it is *itself a fixed one*, which persists without transition? That the understanding without Hegelian reason does not acknowledge the changeability of the moments of spirit is false; what psychologist would have denied it? But granted that an A passes over into B, can A and B therefore not be thought distinctly, even if one grants that what truly is there is the unity of A and B? A curved line contains a continuous change of direction; nevertheless the mathematician knows for each of its points which direction ends in it and which new one begins; the equation of the curve gives a perfectly distinct concept of all the changes of its directions. When known substances neutralize each other, the chemist knows exactly what properties they possess before and what after the neutralization, [p. II.66] although the neutralization itself is a transition. It is possible that even the A itself is only a transition of some a into b, and the a in turn a transition of an α into β; then one will think the transition of A into B distinctly only if one has fixed in thought each of the determinations a, b, α and β, i.e. if one knows *what* each is, and *into what* it passes over. Without this knowledge no transition is distinguished from another; they are all the same, and with that, or else with a *regressus in infinitum* [infinite regress] of determinations presupposing one another, knowledge and all science of these states comes to an end. Thus the changeability of mental states does not release us from the duty of determining them exactly, and the rule holds: either sharp determinations of concepts as descriptions and definitions, or no psychology. — But it is asserted that every concept is simple; what quality, quantity, ground, etc. are cannot be said. If this were correct, we would indeed demand in vain an analysis of the concepts of the states of spirit. But then the whole of psychology would also consist in nothing more than the series of names of the moments passing over into one another. The table of contents of the printed work would be the complete work itself, and Prof. R. would not have needed to add to it several hundred pages as useless ballast. That simplicity is indeed asserted, but also continually refuted, in that one actually gives analyses of concepts and says, for example: attention is the direction which spirit gives itself toward itself in its feeling, and the like. Indeed, one is so much at odds with oneself that one even boasts in several places of the exactness of one’s *descriptions* and determinations of concepts, after having after all declared them impossible (e.g. pp. 391, 414). They are therefore empty words by which my second demand is rejected in the passage cited above; but the rejection itself remains highly characteristic of the Hegelian school. It was said earlier that it, or at least Prof. R., renounced sharpness of judgment, holding to the sharp deed; with equal courage it now also gives up clarity of concepts. How much scientific character will [p. II.67] be left over there? Add to this that whoever renounces distinctness of his concepts cannot possibly apprehend what is empirically given faithfully through them, that therefore my first demand, already conceded, must likewise remain unfulfilled through the rejection of the second, and one obtains the picture of a scientific psychology of a certainly most remarkable kind, and yet one to which the works under consideration correspond wonderfully exactly.

For if at other times a man’s deed is sometimes better than his word, here it must be stated as a fact that Prof. R.’s psychology contains scarcely a trace of a faithful apprehension of mental states in distinct concepts. The proof has been furnished in the first part of my critique, and above too I have pointed to individual passages pertaining to this. It takes some courage, in the face of these demonstrations, to speak of at most *three heresies* against experience of which Prof. R. is supposed to have made himself guilty, and since he expressly rejects some others, I must, I suppose, add something more.

What I objected to his accounts of memory and of feeling he disputes on pp. 319 and 413 of the 2nd ed. I refer to what is said on p. 42 of my critique about the former, and on pp. 12, 36 and 48 about the latter; the matter judges itself. — In feeling, so we are now assured, *spirit excites itself*. What is spirit? The sublated unity of all *spiritual* moments, one of which is precisely now to be defined for us; thus with this determination of the concept we turn round in a circle. — Whether it is empirical that spirit is the *absolute power* over the external world, that it *sublates* objectivity and brings it to unity with its subjectivity, has already been discussed earlier. But from this view it is inferred that spirit, as desiring, *negates the desired object*, and that this negation is accomplished in *enjoyment*, so that through it the desiring subject “gives itself the real certainty of its power over the same (the object).” Against this I had objected: “If a food, or the longed-for sight of a painting, is enjoyed, food and picture remain for *spirit*, after the enjoyment as before it, something *external*; they become neither nothing nor a constituent of the soul. Thus says [p. II.68] experience loudly enough, and only a very confused thinking can contradict it.” To this Prof. R. replies:

“No, all experience contradicts that of Herr *Exner*, even if he let it cry out ever so loudly.

“What, when I enjoy a food, do I not negate it?

“What, when I look at a painting, do my eyes not negate it in order to posit it for my consciousness through the mediation of the optic nerve? Does not even the linguistic idiom say: to *devour* something with one’s eyes?

“When I eat a food that excites my desire, it remains, according to Herr *Exner*, something *external* to his spirit. Then, I conclude, he did not desire it at all, did not consume it at all with human sense. Otherwise he would surely have to know how it tastes to him. Or does his tongue not belong to his spirit? At other times his spirit must surely follow his body even ‘into the mail coach’; is the sweet concord suddenly to be lacking here?

“And my eyes, to be sure, do not negate the painting materially, so as to make it vanish from the wall, but, as the nature of the sense of sight demands, ideally. And with such apprehension it really becomes a constituent piece in me — and just at this moment I see quite distinctly several pictures from the Prague university building rising before the eyes of my spirit, which I have *taken away* from there with me as constituent pieces of it. *Taking*, even if merely with the eyes, is surely a *negation*?”

Does this passage now contain a *confused* thinking, or does it prove the *empirical character* of the concepts of desire and enjoyment that were put forward? *Negation* here, according to its connection with the main thought of this psychology, signifies a *sublation* of the object as *object*. A consumed food may have ceased to be a food; but has it also ceased to be an object for spirit? Has an atom of it so united itself with spirit that it would now be a constituent of it? Organic chemistry denies it. But the taste of the food is surely there now as a moment in spirit? Certainly; but it is precisely a moment of *spirit*, and not the smallest part of the *food*, [p. II.69] nor any of its properties, even though it would not be present without certain properties of the food.
