If the food is really negated through enjoyment, this negation obviously means nothing other than the transformation of the object into another object; and this is a *second* concept of *negation*, very different from the one above. — But the painting? It too is negated, negated through being looked at! I can, first of all, assure Prof. R. that the paintings of which he speaks still hang quite unchanged in their places, and that I have so far noticed absolutely nothing about them to show that they have been looked at by him. The negation in this case, then, is not the transformation of the object into another. The basilisk glances which once, it seems, annihilated the objects they looked upon have, after all, gone out of fashion. But the sight, so it is asserted, negates the painting *ideally*. Yes indeed; where thoughts give out, “there a word turns up at the right time;” for this negation is now nothing more than a presentation’s being mediated through the action of an external object; and why this should be called ideal negation, Prof. R. may know. In any case we have herewith a *third* concept of negation. Further, one can taste a food and see a painting even without any preceding or accompanying desire, and thus without what is here called enjoyment, just as one tastes or sees indifferent things; yet in this case too that is present which was just before called the negation of the food and of the painting. Not merely *desire*, therefore, but also so-called sensation negates its object in the above sense; with what right, then, is this negation made a characteristic mark of desire and its satisfaction? It seems to me that confused thinking and the inadequacy of the concepts to the most ordinary experience lie here so nakedly and barely before our eyes that one must almost hesitate to point to them. And yet, what defiance in the passage just cited — with such poverty, what beggar’s pride!

Does it really agree with experience, what Prof. R. repeatedly (p. 274) asserts, “that free subjectivity disposes of [p. II.70] its content with absolute sovereignty,” i.e. that the reproduction of obscured presentations lies in the *unlimited arbitrary will* of man; or does every schoolboy know the opposite when he notices that he has forgotten something he learned, even after he had already known it quite well? Does it show clear thinking when, beside that pronouncement about the absolute power of spirit over its presentations, as over objectivity, there stands the other (p. 348): “Whether the subject has a propensity, and for what it can have one, thus *does not depend on it*, but in this it succumbs to its *original fate*?” Likewise we read (p. 198): “Since nature cannot posit itself as subject against spirit, spirit must succeed in taking possession of its corporeality. The *victory* over it is *guaranteed* to spirit even before the battle.” And (p. 194): “The ground of its development is *spirit itself* in its *simple* subjectivity, *free* in and for itself *of the organism*.” And again (p. 180): “For in fact spirit is this *infinite power* of *being able to abstract* from all externality, even its *very own*.” But then also (p. 193): “Man is infinite according to generic possibility; as an actual subject he is *limited*, and indeed it is *nature* that assigns him *determinate limits*. Each one receives particular aptitudes as innate; his freedom can develop them more or less, but can neither annihilate them nor put others in their place.” Can one give such talk, which lets the absolute power and freedom of spirit dwell together with all sorts of limitations of it as peacefully as a skilful animal tamer lets the lion and the sheep dwell in the same cage — can one give it any other name than that of drivel?

Does experience teach that (p. 105) “spirit is free of *time*, that the *velocity* of thoughts is just as much an *absolute* one as their spatiality,” or does it rather show that the coming and going of our thoughts not seldom takes a very noticeable time? Does it speak for clarity of thinking when one says (p. 65): “The objective proof that it (the intelligence of the suckling) is no *tabula rasa*, [p. II.71] but movement full of content, is given through *speaking*?” As if the suckling spoke, and the period of two years were not long enough to inscribe even an empty tablet with a few strokes! We also read (p. 82): “Spirit as sensing soul has no particular *seat*, but is, throughout the whole organism, *everywhere* center and everywhere periphery;” and further (p. 87): “A pain in the body, a pulling, stabbing, etc., is often sensed very *indeterminately*, and seems, as to location, to be somewhere quite other than where it actually is. Through the sympathy of the sensory nerves, pain is even sensed, very *deceptively*, in limbs which have already been removed from the organism.” It would be interesting to know how these two thoughts can be *clearly* thought together. On p. 20 we learn: “Finally man has an unlimited *universality* with regard to *nourishment*... Man is *in himself* the subjective *totality* of planetary life. His organization is in itself already *homogeneous* with *all* the materials and forms of that life, and hence he displays in the means of his nourishment a *complete unlimitedness* in *making* everything *vegetable* and *animal* homogeneous with himself, in being able to digest it.” First, then, man possesses an *unlimited universality* with regard to nourishment, and that for a cogent speculative reason, namely because he is the totality of planetary life; but then he can after all digest only what is vegetable and animal. About the infusoria Prof. R. seems to have had experiences of his own, for he writes (p. 24): “The *transition* from the *inorganic* to the *organic* is here (in his view of the origin of the racial differences of man) necessarily presupposed; a transition which we have vividly before our eyes in the *infusoria*.” *Ehrenberg*, as far as I know, has so far found precisely the opposite.

These are only isolated but telling examples, and, in connection with the rich material collected in the 1st part of my critique, they will sufficiently prove that Prof. R. makes extensive use of the strange freedom which he claims for himself, [p. II.72] namely the freedom of giving neither distinct concepts nor sharp judgments, and that he is far removed from the petty labor of concerning himself much, down from the height of his speculation, with what is empirically given. But here the not unimportant question forces itself upon us whether, apart from individual opinions, it was *possible* for him, in accordance with his scientific standpoint, to proceed otherwise? *Can Hegelian psychology set up concepts of mental states which contain an exact apprehension of what is empirically given?* With this we have *arrived* at the investigation of the relation of the empirical to thinking that obtains in *Hegel’s* system, at the *possibility of pure thinking*. This point has been sufficiently cleared up by the logical investigations of *Trendelenburg* and the polemical writings later exchanged between him and *Gabler*; here I have only to set forth some things concerning my special subject, in order to make visible the props which Prof. R. recently took pains to place under the building of his science, which threatens to collapse.

I had asserted that the dialectical method is incapable of producing new concepts by its own power, starting from any concept whatever; hence it also cannot arrive at those through which the empirically given mental states are correctly apprehended. If it nevertheless seems to do this, that happens only because it, which passes itself off as a *presuppositionless* method *that takes nothing from outside*, continually takes what is given from outside and stealthily incorporates it into itself. For the concept A leads dialectically to not-A, and then to the unity of A and not-A, and every further concept gained by conscientious continuation of this method is only a combination of the A, the not, and the unity. But the Hegelian system begins with what it calls the emptiest of all concepts, that of being, which, because it is without any further determination, also presupposes nothing at all, and is supposed to develop out of itself, through that method, not merely the higher categories but all the concepts through which we think the wealth of natural things and of the world of spirit. [p. II.73] This I declared impossible for the above reason, and a series of proofs has been adduced to show that in fact, at least in the psychologies I discussed, it has not happened. To this Prof. R. replies (p. 383): “If, therefore, Herr *Exner* is surprised that speculation in its formation is not independent of empirical knowledge, such surprise can have its ground only in his never having made clear to himself the relation of speculation and empirical knowledge, and in his having rather formed the erroneous view that the former excludes all experience from itself.” I had indeed formed the view that Hegelian speculation, because in it the *self-development* of the concept reigns and every *taking from outside* is expressly renounced, excludes experience; now a dependence of it on empirical knowledge is asserted. This does indeed astonish me greatly, but it will now no doubt be thoroughly proved to me. We read (p. 384): whoever thinks that pure thinking is impossible “deceives himself through the *confusion of the necessity* that the philosopher must *learn*, that he must go to the *school of experience*, with the necessity which *belongs to* the Idea *in itself*.” Further (p. 386): “But the concept of the empirical *mediation* of the knowledge of nature and history is not the same as the seeking out and deriving of the necessity immanent in them.” So this, then, is what is meant: in order that the speculative philosopher may *come to be*, he needs experience as a means of his *formation*; but as a speculative philosopher he has to surrender himself purely to the necessity of the Idea, which rests on itself and has no need of empirical knowledge. But was not this, then, also my view of *Hegel’s* doctrine? For that the philosopher, when it is a question of the means of his formation, will need no less than any potter or joiner, I have not doubted, and that as a speculating Hegelian he must develop pure concepts dialectically, I have asserted. Prof. R. seems to have forgotten along the way that he wanted to prove me guilty of an *erroneous* view. So with this we have got no further. *Gabler* too (“The Hegelian Philosophy,” 1843, p. 13) declares intuition and reflection [p. II.74] to be only pedagogical preliminary stages to speculation, which “*in the first instance* no longer needs the external world.”

Speculation as such, then, does *not* need experience. This is settled, not merely according to *Rosenkranz* and *Gabler*, but also according to *Erdmann’s* “Logic and Metaphysics,” and long since according to *Hegel*. This is also quite good for it; for if it needed experience, it would be hard to help it, since its entire edifice has no opening through which a fact of experience could enter with honor. What it has, it must have produced itself; it has no hands to receive what is offered, nor any power of assimilation to appropriate what is foreign by digestion. Therefore it is also quite useless to it if the philosopher in whom it is active has filled himself beforehand ever so much with empirical knowledge of every kind. This could at most make visible to him certain points toward which he has to head; but it is not the goal that brings the wanderer further, but his walking. Speculation has at the beginning the concept of being and the dialectical method, and further whatever it manages to gain through these two. Yet it has everything, after all, for everything comes about through them. The question therefore returns: *how* is it then *possible* that speculation should produce or discover the concepts of nature and of the world of spirit through the mere unfolding of the Idea?

In *Hegel’s* Phenomenology (p. 605) we read the passage: “For this reason it must be said that nothing is *known* that is not in *experience*, or, as the same thing is also expressed, that is not present as *felt* truth, as the eternal *inwardly revealed*, as the holy that is *believed* — or whatever other expressions are used. For experience is just this, that the content — and it is spirit — is *in itself*, substance, and therefore *object* of *consciousness*. But this substance which spirit is, is its becoming toward what it is *in itself*, and only as this becoming that reflects itself into itself is it in itself in truth *spirit*. It is in itself the movement which cognition is — the transformation of that *in-itself* into the for-itself, of *substance* into [p. II.75] the *subject*, of the object of *consciousness* into object of *self-consciousness*, i.e. into an object just as much sublated, or into the *concept*. It is the circle returning into itself, which presupposes its beginning and reaches it only at the end.” The sense is: spirit in itself is not yet thinking spirit; it is consciousness. But consciousness has the whole world of experience as its object, and so, as it develops into thinking, experience is the presupposition of thinking. One knows only what one has experienced in one’s consciousness, inwardly. This may be correct from the phenomenological standpoint; but then it is only the pedagogical demand, discussed earlier, for experience as the presupposition of incipient thinking, of *learning to think*, of the “subjective beginning” of speculation, about which what is necessary has already been said. However, yet another thought lies in the passage cited: the objects of consciousness, together with consciousness, are themselves spirit, thinking, only at lower stages; and so thinking has the whole of experience within itself, and can therefore also develop it again purely out of itself. Were the premise proved, the conclusion would of course be proved too; as it is, however, nothing more is said by this than: because the whole Hegelian philosophy is correct, Hegelian pure thinking is also possible; or more precisely: if all concepts of experience really are contained in the logical Idea, and can therefore be derived from it, then — they can be derived from it. An indubitable truth; only it brings us no further. With this we have at most the familiar appeal to the dialectical method as a *fact* accomplished in the Hegelian works — to which, however, alas! recognition refuses to be paid. — Prof. R. probably had the above passage in view when he wrote (p. 383): “And yet, if by empirical knowledge is understood the knowing of the actual, speculation is even the deepest *self-experience*, and *Hegel* has said this.” The actual, namely, is, as is well known, the rational, the concepts of things. The same sense seems to belong to the words on the next page, that “nature has had the kindness to incorporate itself into the thinker.” [p. II.76] These two scattered splinters of thought are all that Prof. R. contributes to the solution of that important question.

Since what is at issue here is the matter itself, not this or that one of its champions, let us see what reasons have recently been brought into the field by another, who had a special and serious occasion to express himself on this subject. *Gabler* (“The Hegelian Philosophy,” p. 207) likewise opposes to external experience, which speculation in the first instance does not need, an inner one in the sense noted above, and he even finds (p. 15) in this respect a kinship of speculation with mysticism. He also says (p. 11): “The possibility of it (of pure thinking) lies in this, that the original content, as that of eternal reason itself or of the Logos, together with its form, is already contained in our spirit according to its possibility, grounds our own innermost truth, and that hence our logical, pure and free thinking coincides with it in content and form, and, as it has *its* revelation, so also only therein comes to its own innermost truth, in which it also only then becomes truly free.” And further (p. 15): “But the firmly established principle, apprehended and expressed from this side, is that we already have within us the original truth that is to be cognized, and need only duly do our part in order to find it also within us in the form and determinateness that belong to it.” So we receive again and again the assurance found above in *Hegel*, that the derivation of all the concepts in question from pure reason is possible because — which is just what would have to be proved — the former lie in the latter. Prof. *Gabler* himself thinks it necessary to consider the subject from yet another side as well. He says (p. 19): “What, moreover, considered from another side, should stand obstructively in the way of the possibility of such a total cognition by reason? For we are often enough directed to our inner being by poets and non-poets, indeed by our holy religion itself; there we are to seek and find what satisfies eternally, there strive to attain the imperishable and infinite, truth itself, and all that promises eternal life and salvation, and listen to the innermost testimony [p. II.77] of spirit.” But what poets and religion bid us seek in our inner being are not the categories, not the concepts of chemism and magnetism, of the human races, or of the categorical syllogism. He continues: “For we have, after all, as a distinction over the lesser creatures, received the divine gift of reason — which itself, or its image, merely dwells within the world and natural things without their also knowing of it — in such a way that thinking and knowing are also involved in it, and we are even supposed thereby to have been created in the image of God. What, then, is more natural and even more rational than that the thinking bestowed on us should direct itself not merely to the reason outside us but also to *that* within us, and seek to get completely to the bottom of it — of which, we think, we ought surely to be able to become masters within us even more easily than outside us.” Certainly! But whether through *pure* thinking in the Hegelian sense remains entirely undecided by this. Prof. *Gabler* seems indeed to feel this, for he seeks to give the missing decision, and with it also the solution of the above tautology, in the following proposition (p. 195): “In order to find out whether any asserted proposition is sound, one need only take it in its unconditional universality. The question is: what does thinking as thinking mean? Now if it is asserted that thinking absolutely requires intuition for its content and is empty and impossible without it, then the nature of thinking must in and for itself entail this. But the proposition further states: thinking, although for itself something ideal, nevertheless requires the material, and receives its content only from matter. Now if this really and absolutely lies in the nature of thinking, then it is so with *God* as well, who without matter would have no content for his thinking.” To this, however, *Trendelenburg* (“The Logical Question in *Hegel’s* System,” p. 43) has aptly replied: “Whoever does not care to go the long way of investigating human thinking takes the short leap into divine thinking, and now knows his way about in it better than in his own I. Is he then prophet or philosopher, theosopher or logician? Perhaps, in these parts, neither of the two, for to the first belongs [p. II.78] enthusiasm, to the second, rigor. But one gives oneself the appearance of metaphysical depth if one has easier dealings with divine thinking than with the nearest, most individual appearance, for the understanding of which one must first study the whole of physics.” But Prof. *Gabler* still has one more reason for his assertion in readiness. He says (p. 158): “With the mere assertion of the ‘impossibility’ of such a cognition one will suffice all the less here, the more the reasons for which this impossibility is asserted are likewise nothing more than mere assertions and assurances, to which the opposite ones can be opposed with perfectly equal right.” But an old saying runs: *Qui affirmat, probet* [let him who affirms prove]. Over against the mere assurance of the possibility of pure thinking, the mere denial, which demands the proof of what is asserted, is not of equal right; it annihilates the former, and persists until the proof has been furnished. So at least it has hitherto been held in the sciences, and so it had to be, unless one wished to put the fool at an open advantage over the wise man. Appeal to a higher standpoint cannot help, for it is precisely the warrant of the standpoint that is to be demonstrated. Moreover, the opponents of pure thinking have not confined themselves to a simple contradiction; they have *factually* refuted the *fact* of pure thinking asserted in the last instance by the Hegelian philosophy, and other proofs can be drawn both from the psychological nature of thinking and from the logical relation of the concrete concept to the abstract one. The latter I believe I have given; a refutation Prof. *Rosenkranz*, as we saw above, has not attempted.

But does Prof. R. himself, then, have a *distinct* presentation of what he calls pure thinking? The question can, to begin with, be put more definitely thus: Does Prof. R. trust himself to develop the concepts of rhombohedral calcite, of *tussilago farfara*, of the horse, of a man’s inclination toward French wines, out of the necessity of the Idea? “Thinking,” he says (p. 250), “has as little a sensuous content as a sensuous or half-sensuous form; its content is just as universal [p. II.79] as its form is simple.” And (p. 252): “Intuiting is the original grasping of the content in its compact immediacy; thinking has the same content, but purified of all the adhering contingency of intuiting, in its universality determined in and for itself.” *Erdmann* declares thinking to be a universalizing, but pure thinking a universalizing with abstraction from everything objective. It is with the universal, then, that speculation has to do; not, to be sure, with the abstract, but with the concrete universal. But a universality, though already a very concrete one, is certainly also the concept of the horse, or of the pleasure in a particular card game. Natural history knows species, genera, orders, classes, kingdoms and the like, nothing but more or less concrete universalities. How far, now, is speculation able to descend in this series? Without doubt down to the point where the concept ends and the unthinkable, sensation, begins. But where is this the case? With the horse, or with the soliped, or with the mammal, or with the animal, or with the organism? Whoever believes he has clearly grasped the nature of pure thinking must be able to give a precise answer to this question. If speculation keeps to the highest universalities, then it has not cognized natural things and mental states in their manifoldness; if it lets itself down to what is subordinate, then it may see what becomes of its purity. But Prof. R. probably knows, like *Gabler*, more of inner reason than of outer. Now all mental states are characterized, in distinction from mere natural states, by the fact that they are a *presenting*, or that a presenting essentially occurs in them, the word presenting taken in its widest meaning. But what presenting is can no more be said than what, e.g., the color green is; one must know the latter, like the former, from experience, i.e. they are the content of immediate *sensation*. Now since no state of the soul can be made comprehensible without going back to presenting, *the concept of every moment of spirit*, indeed the concept of spirit itself, as thinking, *includes a sensation* as its [p. II.80] *content within itself*. Nevertheless Prof. R. has set up a whole speculative psychology, and has thereby shown factually that he does not know where pure thinking begins and where it ends. It is hereby at the same time demonstrated that a seemingly highest universality has a sensation for its content, and not, say, as a sublated, negated one, and the same could be proved without difficulty of the concepts of chemism, of the organism, etc.

Still other difficulties with this pure thinking are already well enough known. That the concept of the organism can be “discovered” dialectically Prof. R. does not deny. But to judge by the hopes he nourishes for the further development of the special forms of practical spirit, he certainly does not despair either that in future one will also find out dialectically the various forms of organisms, and discover their concepts. But every concept, as we know, contains being within itself as sublated; according to *Hegel* it is the least that can be said of it, that it is. Speculation thus opens up to us a world of *what is*. But the plants which I keep in my herbarium have a *different being* from the concept of the organism, or of the plant in general. They have one of which we know only through sensation, through what is irrational and inaccessible to speculation. We remain free, however, to put speculation into connection with experience in such a way that we now cognize that what is known speculatively is properly the truth of what is given through experience, that what is for speculation is also what truly is of the things of experience. But *how* is the connection of these two worlds to come about? Through sensation? It knows nothing of thinking. Through thinking? It knows nothing of the individual sensations; how could it establish the right connection? The two remain outside one another; the being of speculation remains a being of thought, which has nothing in common with the being of the actual in the true sense of this word. *Gabler* teaches quite rightly that being, too, is after all only a thought; but on the one hand he, like his whole school, constantly mixes up and confuses the essentially different thoughts of being [p. II.81] and of what is, although what is can indeed be thought, but is nevertheless anything but a thought; on the other hand, the thought of that being which every concept involves is also a different one from the thought of the being which I must assert of a man standing before me. *Trendelenburg* had objected to him that a pure thinking is necessarily creative, and that such a thinking is not found in man. He counters with the distinction between human and divine thinking as an after-thinking and a fore-thinking, a reproducing and a producing one. But how did he himself reason? Thinking is thinking; divine thinking is a *pure* thinking, therefore human thinking is too. This can be turned around: thinking is thinking; divine thinking is *producing*, therefore human thinking is too. Indeed, *if* only the immanent necessary development of the Idea is thinking, *by what* could true human and divine thinking be distinguished? More still: it can easily be shown that *Gabler*, if he means to be consistent, must himself give up God’s creative thinking again. He says (p. 167): “It is spirit itself which, in the form of being or of objectivity, as what is *thought*, presents to itself its creative thoughts as its productions, and in the infinite fullness and manifoldness of nature gives itself the spectacle of the unfolding of its own infinite greatness and glory. Only He, only His creative thinking has been able to bring forth a world; it is only His thinking that continually creates and sustains it.” But he also says (pp. 195 ff.): “The question is: what does thinking as thinking mean? Now if it is asserted that thinking absolutely requires intuition for its content, and is empty and impossible without it, then the nature of thinking must in and for itself entail this. But the proposition further states: thinking, although for itself something ideal, nevertheless requires the material, and receives its content only from matter. Now if this really and absolutely lies in the nature of thinking, then it is so with God too, who without matter would have no content for his thinking. But now God does not merely let matter [p. II.82] come into being, but if the slightest material concretion were contained in divine thinking, it would by that very fact no longer be an absolute thinking; it would then contain within itself fixities and obscurities, such as for us, in merely formal thinking and in presenting, are the corporealities not yet dissolved into thought.” Sensation, inaccessible to all thinking, is so to divine thinking too; it would form *fixities* and *obscurities* in it. If this is so, how is it after all possible that God’s thinking should *unfold* itself in the infinite fullness and manifoldness of nature? How can God create the being of sensation, the material, *develop* it *by thinking*, if it is not *in* thinking? How sustain it, if he is unable to think it? *Here confusion reigns on all sides.* It expresses itself also in the remark that the objections to pure thinking after all touch only the *whence* of concepts, in which there seems to lie almost an abandonment of pure thinking, as also, further, in the wavering with which being is now, as we have seen so far, completely volatilized into thinking and blurs away dissolved in it, now again set over against it in a separating opposition. Thus we read (p. 107) that the self-movement of thought is not the self-generation of *being*, but the self-regeneration of the *thought of being*; that the stages of the concept are not stages of being, but stages of thought; and (p. 166): without being, i.e. the objective, or the other in general, which is distinguished from thinking and is itself non-thinking, there would also be nothing for us, and consequently no knowledge of anything at all. *Erdmann* (“Logic and Metaphysics,” 2nd ed., p. 17) declares the being with which he begins logic to be the infinitive of the copula: is. In doing so he has, as sometimes befalls commentators, spoiled two fundamental thoughts of the master. For he has sublated the freedom from presuppositions, since a copula is not thinkable without something copulated; but he also has a concept of being which is wholly meaningless for the absolute immediacy of the world of experience that is supposed to rest upon it.

Vigorous natures, like L. *Feuerbach*, have not in the long run let themselves be held by the [p. II.83] airy cobwebs of a fluttering pure thinking torn loose from actuality. In his newest work, already mentioned, he says (p. 43): “A *being not distinguished from thinking*, a being that is only a *predicate* or a determination of reason, is only a *thought*, *abstract* being, but in truth *no* being. The identity of thinking and being therefore expresses only the *identity of thinking with itself*, that is to say: absolute thinking does *not get away from itself*, does *not get out of itself* to being. Being remains only a beyond. Absolute philosophy has indeed made the *beyond of theology* into a here-and-now for us, but in return it has made the *here-and-now of the actual world* into a beyond for us;” and loc. cit. (p. 55): “*Hegel* is a thinker who outbids himself in thinking — he wants to grasp the *thing itself*, but *in the thought* of the thing, to be *outside* thinking, but *in thinking* itself — hence the difficulty of grasping the ‘concrete’ concept.” If he has now, as it seems, thrown himself into another extreme still little clear to him, into a coarse empiricism, few thinkers will feel inclined to accompany him; but in any case the force becomes evident thereby with which the inadequacy of the empty Hegelian abstractions annihilates itself. — For us it follows that a philosophy which excludes all sensation from itself as something unthinkable is, even with the best will of those who work it out, *incapable of faithfully apprehending the empirically given mental states in distinct concepts*.

The first of the demands made of it, faithful apprehension and distinct description of the states of the soul given in inner experience, Prof. R.’s psychology has accordingly not fulfilled, but rather rejected as an unwarranted one; but it has not been able to justify its procedure, and has instead proved itself unclear and wavering within itself. My second demand concerned the *demonstration* of the *causes* and *laws* from which and according to which the states of the soul and their changes arise. The natural sciences, which have to do with outer experience, fall, as is well known, into two main groups; [p. II.84] the one, natural history, is occupied with the apprehension, description and ordered arrangement of what is given; the other seeks to investigate the causes and laws of changes. But everything external is changeable. Since anatomy has become comparative, botany also takes account of the inner structure of plants, and mineralogy must serve geology, the two groups have, as is fitting, entered into close connection. Every thinking man asks after causes where he sees changes, and where there are causes, there are also laws of acting, of change. One can know laws by induction without the causes, but not, conversely, have an exact knowledge of the causes without that of the laws; for the law is only the manner of acting of the cause. Thus *Kepler* found the laws of planetary motion, and only *Newton* found gravitation as their cause, from which those laws, together with the laws of falling bodies etc., already known as well, were at once derived. Even such laws could be derived as had previously not been dreamt of at all, and, guided by them, one discovered changes in the heavens that had until then remained unnoticed. Is the knowledge of our inner world, then, destined to remain more imperfect than that of the outer world, so that there we content ourselves with the mere apprehension and description of the facts, but leave their causes and laws undecided? That, I said once before, would correspond poorly to the importance of the subject. Or are the causes and laws in this field of inquiry not cognizable for us? That would surely first have to be tested by a serious attempt. Or are there no causes and laws here at all? So comprehensive an exception to the law of causality could easily make all our knowledge of the outer world unsteady too; in any case it would have to be strictly proved. What answer, then, does Prof. R. now recently give to these questions, which I had urgently commended to his attention? I do not know. Whether my readers will find out his opinions from his statements, which I now cite, with more luck than I, remains to be seen.

After the understanding has again, as is only fair, been soundly scolded, [p. II.85] Prof. R., at the point where his refutation first takes up these questions, (p. 372) continues thus: “Instead of such (dialectical) resolutions (of the contradictions), from which even new contradictions can develop — a Penelopean labor according to Herr *Exner* — the understanding demands *explanations*, i.e. the stating of one determinacy as the ground of the existence of another in such a way that the two determinacies remain in themselves foreign to each other. If they did not remain so, if their being-asunderness were sublated, that would of course already be a speculative misfortune. But especially important to the understanding are its *laws*, i.e. abstract *universalities*, unmoved in themselves, to which it appeals wherever it is unable to comprehend anything. If only it can prop itself up on a law decreed by itself, it believes science saved. A *critique* of its laws is therefore to it an assault on science itself. If ever it runs out of laws, it helps itself by at least *conjecturing* some. It then speaks with modest arrogance of the probability of a *still hidden* law.” So: no causes, but dialectic! No laws, only dialectic! For the rest, Prof. R. still thinks far too well of men of the understanding when he supposes that they merely *conjecture* laws where they have not yet found them; they are so narrow as to presuppose them with all possible confidence. But then again he thinks all too badly of them when he believes they would take a *critique* of their laws quite amiss. Let him just give a critique of the theory of light as *Fresnel* has developed it; it will certainly be tolerated, and he will moreover have the opportunity to show how much he understands of the subject on which he here pronounces.

On p. 390 begins a section which bears the heading: Explanation and the How. Here, then, we shall surely learn precisely what Prof. R. thinks about the subject in question. He begins: “A favorite turn of Herr *Exner’s* is the complaint that the *how* of a determination, the explanation of a *state*, is missing. In my opinion the how *coincides* with the concept of the thing. To look for something else again behind this is to carry water to the sea. [p. II.86] *Hegel* is even of the opinion that occupying oneself with the how is a means of evading comprehension, but says nonetheless in another place that by the how one properly understands the *manner*.” I may fairly be surprised that Prof. R. does not yet know here what is really under discussion; after all, I had said clearly enough (pp. 3, 55) that by the question *how* a certain state arises is meant: through what causes and according to what laws. After a little digression, in which Prof. R. assures us that his descriptions are far better than those of other psychologists, he collects himself, however, and says: “By explanation is often also understood the statement of the contingent *causal connection* in which something stands or can stand. But since everything is able to be a cause in very different directions, through such a statement the concept itself is not so much grasped according to its center as elucidated according to the range into which it can lay itself out.” So the category of causality *does* after all also have an *applicability* to the *moments of spirit*; for if everything can be a cause, then surely they can too. But, so we hear, it is quite insignificant; it is far too *trifling* for a science to investigate more exactly the range into which the concepts it treats can lay themselves out. Prof. R. then reproaches me for the question how particular *psychical* differences, e.g. idiosyncrasy, can arise from *physical* ones, and continues: “To this I have, to be sure, only the general answer that in and for themselves nature and spirit, in their distinction from one another, *are* one. They only become what they are. They are not *the same*, but *in their distinction* they are, without the intervention of a third, *identical*. The manifold differences which result from this form, in *Hegel*, the subject of the anthropological division of psychology. *How* nature and spirit *can* be one in spirit, insofar as the how is supposed to mean a causality lying outside nature, outside spirit, is just as unanswerable a question as every similar one: *how* the earth can fly through four miles in one second, *how* the color green can be green, *how* space can be unbounded, [p. II.87] and so forth.” With regard to those states of the soul, then, which are usually derived from bodily causes, and which Prof. R. himself calls mediated by the body, every causal connection is once more flatly *rejected*; they fall victim to dialectic. And who, pray, had demanded a causality lying *outside* nature, *outside* spirit? If, for the rest, Prof. R. will only recall what he doubtless knows of *Fresnel’s* theory of light, he will know, if not yet entirely, then at least in part, *how* the color green can be green and not yellow, and conversely the color yellow yellow and not green. Perhaps he will also yet come to know a metaphysics which tells him how space can be unbounded, and then, if a little physics were added, it would not even be impossible that even the question how the earth can fly through four miles in one second would no longer seem to him an *absolutely* unanswerable one. How shy absolute knowing suddenly becomes here! It might almost seem as though “that blasé attitude of perennial wonder which puts the easy panegyric of gaping astonishment in place of the hard labor of cognition” had passed from the abstract men of the understanding over to the speculatively rational ones. — But Prof. R. at once makes another sally against the dualism of the abstract understanding, and then continues: “What idiosyncrasy is I have said clearly enough. It is the outermost offshoot of the natural individuality of spirit, the most exclusive peculiarity of singularity. It is, *as goes without saying*, *mediated in the individual*. I expressly state such mediations, more than enough; I recall *inheritance*, *illnesses*, and so forth. But I also point out that *in concreto* the original genesis of an idiosyncrasy *often* cannot be demonstrated at all. The individual in whom it inheres has not observed in himself the origin of such a willfulness of his sensation, and still more is it the case that others perceive it only in its *finished* existence. Here I now say that our psychical rapport, *potentia et actu* [in potency and in act], has a wider range than we usually assume. If someone has once taken arsenic [p. II.88] and had almost died of it, it is very comprehensible that later the mere name arsenic can bring on cramps in him. But why someone must weep in moonlight, or feels oppressed at the sight of a red-haired person, or is made to faint by the smell of a bedbug, and the like, cannot at once be explained, as people like to call it, *although the existence of the reasons is not to be doubted. The existence in itself is necessary, but in many cases appears contingent owing to ignorance of the mediation*.” “Now I should like to know,” he continues shortly afterward, “what is supposed to be so unsatisfying in this account.” He then closes the chapter with the words: “*Up to this point it was in order* that the science of psychology stated the *concept* of the thing; the investigation of its *concrete existence*, the determination of its value for the bodily and mental welfare of man, and still more the statement of particular means for altering it, belong precisely to hygiene and orthobiotics on the one hand, to ascetics and pedagogy on the other.” So we suddenly have *reasons* again, reasons lying in the body for the existence of an idiosyncrasy. Are these reasons, then, *causes*, or is idiosyncrasy only the *dialectical negation* of the bodily abnormality? Let the readers judge for themselves; I have not been able to find out the answer with certainty. — Prof. R. asks what is *insufficient* in this exposition. *What* idiosyncrasy is, he thinks he has said clearly enough. It is for him, namely, the immediate unity, or the immediate contradiction, or the indifference between the individual and objectivity, as sympathy, antipathy and apathy. Whoever finds this clear enough, and does not find that it fits just as well the pleasant, the unpleasant and the indifferent sensation and, God knows, how many other states of the soul, him I will not trouble further. But it is asserted that idiosyncrasy springs from the bodily determinacies of the individual, and indeed from the *individual* ones, as the aptitudes spring from the particular and the temperaments from the universal ones. But now it surely lies in the necessary self-development [p. II.89] of the concept that *every* individual has its *individual* determinacies, without which it would indeed be no individual; but how does it come about, then, that, as is conceded (p. 55), not *every* individual *has idiosyncrasies*? About this nothing is said. In *which* bodily determinacy, pray, does the oppression that someone feels at the sight of a red-haired person have its ground? That, Prof. R. thinks, he does not know; it cannot be explained straightaway. If one were further willing to assume that spirit is the negation and unity of spirit and nature, how then can it be comprehended that that oppression could be the *dialectical negation* of the abnormal *swelling* or *congestion of this nerve ganglion*, or wherever else its bodily ground may lie? That he does not know either. He can explain idiosyncrasy in general from the fact that “our psychic rapport with the external world is livelier and reaches further than we know with our ordinary experience;” the individual case does not concern him, it belongs to hygiene. We men of the understanding know not much more than nothing about the origin of idiosyncrasy, and exactly as much, as one sees, does Prof. R. know. If he now finds nothing scientifically *insufficient* in this, then his contentedness is truly very great.

Where, further, was it in order until then that psychology gave only the *concept* of the thing, but did not concern itself with the *causes and* laws of its concrete existence? With *Spinoza* and his descendants it is so, we see it; but the other psychologists, from *Aristotle* to *Beneke*, have, if history does not deceive, always at least attempted and acknowledged a different order. They frequently had in view the practical needs of the pedagogue and the statesman, and hoped to be able to be of help in man’s noblest business, the formation of man. For the educator wants to bring about or remove mental states in his pupil, and for this purpose he must do something, i.e. employ means which are related to the end as *causes* to *effects*. For this, however, he needs the knowledge of *what causes* mental states arise from, and this knowledge he seeks from the [p. II.90] *psychologist*. Now if the latter, though he has the concept of the thing, i.e. of the states of the soul, knows nothing of the causes and laws of their coming and going, then he is simply useless for those purposes. One need by no means esteem science merely by its utility, and can nevertheless, without becoming vulgar, as Prof. R. in a frivolous humor makes out in the section “The Uselessness of the Hegelian Psychology,” inquire most earnestly what assistance psychology may be able to render in solving the great task of our race. Would that no philosopher had anything worse said of him than that through his science he was of use *only* to mankind. But Prof. R. assures us (p. 394): “I have, however, indicated the transition of psychology into pedagogy, morals and politics at all important points, yet never let the pedagogical, moral, political itself enter, because thereby that overloading of psychology with material foreign to it arises, for which precisely these other sciences exist, which would otherwise not be needed at all.” Why this defense, when no one has accused him? What was demanded of him was not that he should *take* pedagogy *up* into psychology, but that his psychology should be *capable* of furnishing a *scientific foundation* for pedagogical inquiry and treatment. But it seems to be almost as hard to hold Prof. R. fast to a scientific question as to hold a drop of quicksilver fast between two fingers. He continues: “The concept of the thing itself is already the most useful thing of all, for practice too.” So, then, psychology does after all seem able to be of use too; but without insight into causal relations, *how* can it be? He answers at once: “Whoever, e.g., has grasped the concept of memory quite thoroughly will by that very fact have gained the insight that all the artificial means for its cultivation which a bad pedagogy enumerates are just as superfluous manipulations as the elixirs advertised in the newspapers for promoting the growth of hair on bald heads.” So this, then, is the sum of it: psychology brings pedagogy this use, that it shows it that it can be of *no* use to it. This at least is literally true with regard to Hegelian psychology. [p. II.91] If memory consists in the absolute power and freedom with which intelligence connects names with concepts, what sense would a regulation have in the case of such an absolute power, standing above every rule of limitation? Light cannot be caught with fishing nets. For the Hegelian philosophy there is no pedagogy, nor any politics either, despite a good many political lectures; these sciences are *impossible* for it. The school has therefore also not yet produced a single properly pedagogical work, and will never do so. On the firm ground of practice the poetic clouds of speculation melt away. And more still: the Hegelian philosophy admits not merely no *science* of education, but not *education itself*, either of the individual or of a people. For all the countless freedoms with which, according to it, the human spirit teems wholly exclude any influence according to the law of causality. Prof. R. may consider it far too trifling a matter to attend to his own experience, which would surely tell him that memory too can be supported by various artifices; his psychology, in its sublimity, may not concern itself at all with the scientific grounds of this experience: but the complete *uselessness* of *psychology* for pedagogy, and the *impossibility* of *education*, are such serious things that, where they are at stake, a man of head and heart might well make himself heard otherwise than in a few phrases darting to and fro and in the tone of a vulgar buffoonery.

Since Prof. R., in what has been discussed so far, seems *now* to *assert*, *now* again to *deny* the causal connection of the states of the soul, we must see whether other passages of his work give more definite instruction. The second edition has received an addition (p. 96) which, because new, has surely also been especially considered and thought through. It reads: “Every content can be sensed more strongly or more weakly, now in itself, now in relation to another. For the actual existence of a sensation the degree of the one immediately preceding it is not indifferent. One can call this relation the *mechanical* [p. II.92] and the *static* one. Clever people, Machiavellians, seducers calculate the moment at which a sensation must have reached its maximum or minimum in the complex with other sensations. But poets do this too.” There, then, we would have an interaction of presentations asserted with all definiteness, one through which a certain presentation or sensation *must* reach a certain strength at a certain time, i.e. one which is subject to fixed laws. The expressions mechanical and static relation of sensations are borrowed from *Herbart*, but applied here in a way that is not really explicable in a man who without doubt knows their original meaning from the natural sciences precisely. Another addition says (p. 282): “The Hegelian psychology can admit all those *forms* which the Herbartian has devised for describing the play of presentations.” But these forms are, as is well known, *laws* of the *interaction* of *presentations*, and thus causal connection of presentations and laws of their operation would once again be acknowledged. On p. 283 we read: “What strength a presentation has for the subject is conditioned by the latter’s interest, which can be a very different one at different times, for which reason the mechanics and statics of presentations can in a concrete case only be conjectured empirically and approximately, but not calculated scientifically and, as seducers and masters of persuasion often flatter themselves, with infallibility.” This is quite correct, and because interest is itself a moment of spirit, the dependence of one mental state on another, hence a causal connection, is thereby expressed. Similar things occur in other passages. But then we read again: “Association has *no* laws” (p. 279); “the I is that which is *free* of all its presentations, which stands above them” (p. 282); “Intelligence, as *free* subjectivity, disposes of its content with *unconditioned sovereignty*” (p. 274); “The dream is the existence of spirit as *chaos*” (p. 119). And to crown it all: “In the dream intuitions, presentations relate to one another only *mechanically* and *chemically*; their [p. II.93] combination is a *contingent* one” (p. 218). Here, then, causelessness and lawlessness reign again, and in the end mechanism and chance, law and lawlessness even go amicably hand in hand. When one reads this, one feels as if one were listening into a beehive. The most diverse voices hum there through one another, but of melody or harmony one hears nothing. It is evident that Prof. R., in deciding the question whether mental states are subject to the causal relation, and thereby to laws as well, shrank from making use of “the sharpness of his judgment”; he no doubt feared that science might run into danger if he set so terrible an instrument in motion. And yet this question is so important that the *content* and *form* and *worth* of psychology depend on its decision, more perhaps than on any other that might be raised.

Consistency in inconsistency, however, one cannot deny to Prof. R.’s psychology; for his practice in the question mentioned is just as wavering as his theory. Now, that is, he depicts individual mental states without any regard to their causes, now he goes into these. But in the latter case too he is consistent insofar as he does not give a scientifically *exact* derivation a single time. The proofs of this have been adduced in abundance in the first part of my critique, and they can be multiplied at will. Thus it is surely no attempt to explain the origin of the so-called *common image* from its causes when it is said in the new addition (p. 275): “The image has universality *immediately* through its existence in spirit, because universality is essential to spirit.” Instead of causes we have here, rather, the absolute freedom of spirit once again. But he continues: “Universality does not arise only gradually through repetition of the same intuition, as if, e.g., in order to possess the presentation house as an abstract schema under which every house can be subsumed, the intuition of many houses had to come first.” Whence, then, the astonishment with which a peasant boy who until now has seen only straw roofs or broad-leaved trees, and has also heard only of such, sees for the first time a [p. II.94] copper roof, conifers, or even palms? Was his previous schema of house or tree really already so constituted that every house and every tree could be subsumed under it without difficulty? This is neither a faithful apprehension of experience nor that conscientious research which permits itself to reject views hitherto prevailing only after mature examination and for good reasons. — Since someone might raise the question, not unimportant for the psychological theory of thinking, how it comes about, then, that spirit *distinguishes* from one another the *presentations* which it possesses, attention may be drawn to the passage (p. 275): “The subject distinguishes not only the intuition from the presentation, but *also, in the simple breadth of its intelligence*, presentation from presentation.” This simple breadth of intelligence is surely a splendid expression. The *breadth* is presumably that infinite space in intelligence which we have already come across elsewhere; the *simple* breadth is something quite special, a concept to which our mathematicians, with their tough understanding, will not easily soar; but the whole evidently contains not the *cause* of that distinguishing, but the speculative ground of the absolute freedom of spirit. — On the origin of *signs* we are taught (p. 295) that phantasy in its development passes gradually from crudeness into beauty, and from there into over-refinement, immoderation, fantastic dreaming, whereby its content and form separate. “That severance of content and form brings about the possibility that phantasy posits a content of presenting, an intuition, as the *form of a content* which it *gives* to the intuition, but which is not present in it in itself. The intuition arising in this way through the mediation of free phantasy is the *sign*.” Here, then, we have a going into causes, but what a one! Where has the origin of signs waited until a people had *gone through* all the *stages of art* from the beginning to the decline? And again, what an apprehension of experience, when (p. 314) it is assured of our musical *notation*, in a new addition to the second edition: “This [p. II.95] script of signs represents neither a symbolic reflex of the presentation nor a sound, but embryonically balls whole masses of presentations together arbitrarily into one intuition.” Does Prof. R. know, I wonder, what a note under the treble clef on the top line means, and what a musician would say to it if he heard that this note embryonically balls whole masses of presentations together arbitrarily into one intuition! Among the ancients everything was permitted to painters and poets; among us philosophers claim this privilege. They very probably become bad thinkers by it; that they would at the same time become good poets has not yet been heard of, despite a few attempts.

The Hegelian psychology contains a section in which it is developed *that* and *why* man *must* apprehend external things in exact *descriptions* and seek out the *causes and laws* of their changes; namely the section that deals with perceiving consciousness and the consciousness of the understanding. This, however, as is further asserted, is a subordinate standpoint, because on it the differences are held fast in their being-different and opposition, whereas on the higher standpoint of rational thinking it is shown that they are not absolutely different, and that in general nothing truly is but the eternal process of transition in which the moments of the absolute Idea are comprehended. But what sense, then, does this expression have: a *subordinate* standpoint? The categories of substance, cause, interaction, law pass over into other categories, but *every* category does this. Even the absolute Idea becomes its otherness, nature, and absolute spirit lowers itself to empty being. If a moment were *untrue* because it passes over, then *all* would be so, for nothing is but the transition alone. Rather, one must say with Prof. R. (p. 401): “In and for itself every moment of the Idea has *equal worth*.” If a moment is richer in itself than its predecessor, it nevertheless persists just as little, and *in this respect*, which is precisely what matters here, there is only *earlier* and *later*, not lower and higher. The philosopher, however, must survey the moments of the circular movement without partisan preference [p. II.96] and subject them all to his consideration with equal interest. Now as regards *external nature*, I do not believe that Prof. R. would be inclined to deny it causes and laws, since he too (pp. 211–219) has developed that standpoint of the consciousness of the understanding. *Hegel* calls *Kepler’s* and *Galileo’s* laws of planetary motion and of falling bodies immortal discoveries, which he would surely not do if they were dead from the outset for the philosopher. Now, however, *mental states* form a domain of inner experience, as external things do of outer experience, and it is precisely for the domain of *experience* that Prof. R. has formed his standpoint of the consciousness of the understanding. How, then, can he assert, in contradiction with his own doctrine, that for mental states there are neither causes nor laws?

But if there is anywhere in the domain of cognition a standpoint on which the categories of cause and law have validity, then it too, like every other, must be *worked out scientifically*. Now where is this to happen, inside or outside philosophy? *Erdmann* (“Logic and Metaphysics,” pp. 69, 178) declares himself in favor of its having to happen, with regard to the objects of outer experience, in the natural sciences. If Prof. R. agrees with this view, I have nothing against it; only, in a more exact investigation of the relation of the natural sciences to the philosophy of nature, he might run into the difficulties indicated earlier, whose solution will not be easy without giving up essential points of the system. Analogously to this procedure, the same standpoint would then have to find its working-out, with regard to the objects of inner experience, in an empirical psychology which would be no part of philosophy. Now I do not know what Prof. R. thinks on this point, but I doubt that he will advise philosophers henceforth to give wholly out of their hands this part of the science of spirit, in whose undisputed possession they have been until now. But according to Prof. R.’s own judgment it can by no means be assumed that this empirical psychology, insofar as it has to show the causes and laws of the states of the soul, has already sufficiently solved its task [p. II.97] ; hence we are entitled to put to the psychologists of the Hegelian school the question by *what right* they wholly *neglect*, indeed *ignore*, a *principal part* of the *science of spirit*, as though it were no concern of theirs at all? The question becomes weightier through the following consideration. No Hegelian will assert that the philosophy of nature which the school presently gives could have been set up just as well 500 years ago, or that at present a philosopher could put it forward, just as it is, who empirically knew no more of electricity and magnetism, of light and heat, of mechanics and chemistry than, say, a wholly uninstructed countryman. Experience has taught how easily the philosopher of nature makes himself ridiculous when he scorns the instruction of experience, and people have at least become more cautious. Now if the necessity is acknowledged of relying, in the *philosophy of nature*, on developed natural sciences, will this necessity not also arise for the *philosophy of spirit* with respect to what we have provisionally called empirical psychology? It is not possible to answer this question in the negative with reasons, and if this has so far been done without reasons, but in fact, the cause lay in this, that one can more easily make words about mental states, and at the same time be refuted less easily by ocular evidence, than with the palpability of the external world. This was precisely no advantage for the philosophy of spirit. But if the relation indicated between it and that science of spirit which grows up on the so-called standpoint of the consciousness of the understanding, i.e. which investigates the grounds and laws of mental objects, is correct, then the Hegelian psychology is guilty of a twofold grave neglect. This, it seems to me, is how the matter presents itself when one considers it from this school’s own standpoint.

Supposing the Hegelian speculation were perfectly justified, the philosophy of spirit, *going beyond* the standpoint of the consciousness of the understanding, would have to dissolve the causal connection of mental states as empirical psychology has presented it, and put the dialectic of the concept in its place. [p. II.98] I have called the presentation of the states according to their causal connection, because it shows their origin, the genetic method. The Hegelian school would therefore have to declare with decisiveness that the *dialectical method is not the genetic one*, indeed *cannot be it at all*. Even so, a greater and also a lesser divergence of the two methods from each other would still be possible. The dialectical method has the concept of the mental moment B issue from the concept of the moment A. In doing so it does not regard A as the cause of B, for it is beyond this category; nevertheless it would be possible for it to have B develop dialectically from the same A which according to the genetic method is the cause of B. If the same relation now continued through the remaining moments, the *series* of dialectically developed moments would coincide perfectly with the *series* of genetically connected ones, which is at the same time the factual temporal series occurring in actuality. The difference would lie only in this, that what in the one series would be cause would in the other be a lower moment of the concept. But it could also be that, while genetically B issues from A, dialectically the reverse took place; or else that B developed from some third concept M, in which case the two series would have a quite different arrangement. In the case of the coincidence of the two series it could now, after all, likewise be said that the dialectical method is also the genetic one; for although this would *not* be true in the above *proper* sense, because the causal connection is not demonstrated, it would nevertheless give a development of the states of the soul which corresponded perfectly to their factual development; the dialectical connection would *copy the real* connection of empirical occurrence exactly, and only the causal linkage of the members would have been replaced by the dialectical one. I had emphatically brought out the question of the relation of the dialectical method to the genetic in my critique; here, where we are discussing the conception of the causal relation in the Hegelian psychology, is the place to appraise the more detailed answer given by Prof. R.

[p. II.99] What led me to raise that question was the observation that the dialectical method, if it was *not* at the same time to *be the genetic one*, would leave us wholly uninstructed about the highly important temporal and causal succession of mental states, and would thereby become useless for the practical sciences; and that, on the other hand, if it *were at the same time genetic*, it would give evidently false developments, in that it would dialectically generate conceptual series of states which in fact could never appear in such an order and develop out of one another. Prof. R.’s “Refutation” devotes a section of its own to this subject (p. 402), with the heading “The Dialectical and the Real Genesis.” After the unhappy wavering with which, as has been shown above, he now expressly rejects the causal connection of the mental moments, now admits it again, or expressly asserts it, it is not to be expected that he will be especially clear here. A more exact determination of the concept of the genetic method does not, accordingly, enter his mind either; instead, after stating by way of preface that I miss in the Hegelian psychology the genetic course which alone could give true enlightenment, he begins his instruction: “This objection rests on a failure to recognize the necessity of scientific *exposition* to *lay out* the *moments* of the matter *separately* in the form of particular concepts. Science must here proceed from the abstract to the concrete. The all-sided totality of the matter unfolds itself according to all its sides before it gathers itself together as a whole into its unity.” After a small digression he continues: “*Actuality* takes quite *the same* path, but since in it *being-at-once and being-side-by-side* exists, one must not expect a *copy* of the *systematic* exposition.” There follows a reproach concerning the way in which I conceive the agreement of the dialectical and the real genesis, and thereupon he continues: “It has been set forth with the greatest possible clarity that at all *stages* of development it is the whole spirit with which science has to do, but that the logical course makes it impossible to imitate the coexistence of the determinations in immediate virtuality. [p. II.100] But this impossibility by no means signifies that this very course, the logical one, would not be in *harmony* with reality. Were it not so, it would simply not be logical. In psychology the course of the concepts is also that of the thing itself.”

Is not the first, Prof. R. thereupon asks, the captivity of spirit in its naturalness, the second the clarity of consciousness, the last the further development of intelligence into thinking and willing? “What in this course is not genetic? What in it does not correspond to actuality? What in it is a determination merely of the concept, as of an abstraction, to which no living pulse-beat of reality corresponded?” Now follows the asseveration that the Hegelian psychology is far more genetic than those of *Tetens*, *Carus*, *Kant*, *Vorländer*, and with this, though not the section, yet the discussion of that important question comes to a close. — That the dialectical method is at the same time genetic is thus decidedly *asserted*; it remains undecided, however, for want of a more exact determination of the genetic method, whether the causal relation is thereby taken in or excluded, whether genetic development is to be taken in the narrower or the wider meaning noted above. Yet reality is supposedly unable to be a *copy* of the systematic exposition, because the thing has a coexistence of determinations, which can also develop as a being-simultaneous and being-side-by-side, whereas the logical course must appear in the form of succession. It is quite correct that one cannot write or read two lines, two pages of a book at the same time; what in reality is *side by side*, therefore, one can in science only say or think *one after the other*. But one can surely say *whether* what comes up for discussion one after the other *is* in reality one after the other or else simultaneous; and if one says it, one must *prove* it. Suppose the thing A has two determinations, a and α, which develop simultaneously into series, the former into a, b, c, the latter into α, β, γ; then science cannot speak of both series at the same time. But it can and must show that and why these series, discussed one after [p. II.101] the other, run side by side in reality, and why others, *likewise* discussed one after the other, also appear one after the other in reality. If it does not do this — if it perhaps even joins them to one another in such a way that the first member of the one develops dialectically out of the last of the other — then we shall have to take the two no longer as two series but as one single series of succession, provided, that is, that dialectical development really is also genetic development. All the more does it hold that, where there is talk of only one single dialectical series at all, its members are to be regarded as appearing in reality one after the other; not, indeed, as if the preceding member had to vanish upon the entry of the following one, but still in such a way that the following one is a later one in time and factually proceeds from the former.

In this respect, now, it may sound harsh, but it is true, that Prof. R. must have counted on a thoughtlessness in his readers bordering on imbecility if he believed he could justify his psychology by his reply against the objections now under discussion. For one looks around in vain for a scientific application of the distinction he made between series running side by side and series running one after the other, by which the defects censured would be removed; against Prof. R.’s bald assertion, however, that he proceeded just as genetically as dialectically, nothing more is needed than to open the *table of contents* of his book and survey the series of concepts whose members follow one another dialectically in one and the same series. Even the feeblest understanding will thereupon declare it untrue that in reality the alternation of the times of day develops out of the alternation of the seasons, the white race of men out of the yellow, the choleric temperament out of the sanguine, antipathy out of apathy, sympathy out of antipathy, man out of woman, the stages of life out of the difference of the sexes, sleeping and waking out of the stages of life, the senses of smell and taste out of the sense of touch, derangement out of imbecility, physiognomic expression out of mimic expression, the craniological out of the [p. II.102] physiognomic, observing out of describing, intuiting out of rational self-consciousness, stimulus and drive out of thinking, etc., etc. If one adds to this all the examples of unsuitable and failed derivations of mental states which have been adduced and dissected partly in this little work, partly and especially in the first part, then the demand that one recognize in such series of concepts the course which the thing itself takes in its reality will appear too strong in the face of any public whatever, however large the doses of this kind may be to which its organism has perhaps already grown accustomed. Admittedly, pieces of series of concepts also occur in which the dialectical sequence fairly well corresponds to the genetic one. Insisting on one such, Prof. R. exclaims in a new addition to the 2nd ed. (p. 245): “And this sequence is not merely a makeshift of the *exposition*, but also the necessity of the *thing*. Without intuiting no presenting, without presenting no phantasy, without phantasy no memory, without memory no thinking, and so forth.” Why does he break off here? The very next step would have led him to the statement: without thinking no stimulus nor drive — against which, to be sure, experience would protest strongly if thinking is taken as it is taken here. That was precisely my reproach: that dialectical development now agrees with genetic development and now again does not; and on this I demanded an explanation. Prof. R. gives it by declaring that the agreement is found everywhere. And this he does in the face of such a book — his own, namely! — As a particular case in which dialectical and genetic development come into contradiction, I had adduced that the practical spirit proceeds dialectically out of the theoretical, whereas genetically the developments of both, as is well known, run parallel from childhood on. To remove this difficulty, Prof. R. makes a special effort in the 2nd ed. He says (p. 247) in a new addition: “In and for itself, therefore, spirit is free of the necessary one-sidedness of its configuration as theoretical or practical. Thinking and willing are equally essential moments in the totality of spirit, and it is a [p. II.103] thoughtlessness to pass off the one *or* the other of them as the true nature of spirit; for not only is *self-determination* contained in thinking, but the *concept* of that to which spirit determines itself is also contained in willing.” Accordingly we would have here the case mentioned above, where two determinations of A, an a and an α, develop into series that run on side by side, and thereby the difficulty would admittedly be removed. Unfortunately we read immediately afterward (pp. 248 ff.) the following passage, carried over from the first edition: “Theoretical intelligence can be comprehended without mixing the practical into it. Only the universal concept of self-determination is necessary for this. *But practical spirit cannot be comprehended without presupposing the forms of the theoretical*.” Thus the fine new expedient is destroyed again. Incidentally, one can gather from this with what care Prof. R. worked on the second edition of his work; and there were already some earlier occasions for this. New, however, that expedient certainly is, since until now the proposition “thinking is the essence of spirit” has generally been held to be one of the most important in the Hegelian school. “Spirit in its whole essence is nothing other than thinking,” says *Gabler* (p. 149). This, however, yields at most one split more, and Prof. R. does not seem to shrink from such a thing. For let us note that, just as decidedly as he defends the dialectical method as the one that is at the same time the genetic, just as decidedly is the contrary asserted by *Erdmann* — a difference which may well be called an essential one for the working out of the philosophy of nature and of spirit.

The question of the relation of the dialectical method to the genetic has yet another side, for whose sake it is important. If the moments of spirit developed with dialectical necessity are also genetically necessary, then, I had objected, every human being *must* on his way from birth to the grave be at one time a clairvoyant, at one time sick in soul, at one time exclusively dominated by a passion. If one denies the latter, one must give up the former. Against this Prof. R. comes forward and says [p. II.104] (p. 405): “But to return once more to the relation of the dialectical and the real process, one must consider not only what was already said earlier, namely that a distinction is to be made between ground as condition and end, between external and ideal *dependence*, but also that every determination of a systematic whole sublates itself within it as an *accidental* moment, and therefore *appears* as something quite other, *inverted*, as soon as it *fixes* itself, against its concept, against its absolute *valuation*, as the *sole substance*. Science, now, has to let each moment have its way in its whole detail. It has, then, to show how far the one-sided further development of the moment can go, but it must not thereby forget that the moment can attain such validity only within *its relative isolation*. The progress of its development must unveil the semblance of its absoluteness. The course of the whole must give it back its limitation and *reduce* it again to accidentality.” By this, plainly, nothing is refuted; for what has been said, precisely because it holds of all moments of spirit, teaches nothing about the exceptional character of the states named above. Prof. R. continues: “What science thus accomplishes in an ordered course, actuality accomplishes too, but in the confusion of the most manifold *relations of measure*. All possibilities realize themselves. Everything now depends on the degree to which reality extends itself so as to let the individual, as a real appearance, grow out of its subordination to the whole up to the *extreme* of a *self-dependence* which behaves *negatively* toward *change*, toward the *fusion* with other moments that lies implicitly in its concept, toward *relative vanishing*. Then, as always, the quantitative difference leads to a qualitative alteration. The diseases of self-feeling, the struggle of self-consciousness for its recognition, the malformation of phantasy, the fury of the desires and passions are in constant readiness, in constant possibility, of being *fixed*. But so-called healthy life consists precisely in the power of negating such oscillations, such [p. II.105] incipient tendencies, of bringing the *abnormal*, *while it is striving to come to be*, back under its affirmative superior power. The abyss of physical, psychical and ethical disease constantly gapes open within us. That which science pursues with all its sharpness to its uttermost, and from which, when it appears as such in life as disease, as insanity, as wickedness, the abstract understanding turns away as from something *merely negative* — that is present in the healthy, the rational and the good itself; a concept which the understanding usually interprets as speculation’s *identification with the positive*, as its *making-one-and-the-same* of good and evil. The harmony of *appearing* existence can at any moment unleash the disharmony overcome within it. *Diabolical distortion* can turn its gruesome visage out of features that were smiling only a moment ago, as suddenly as the unexpected flash of lightning darts down out of a clear sky.” I cannot find that by this, or even by what still belongs here from the next section of the refutation, my argument has been refuted.
