Let the disease of the soul consist in whatever it may: if its concept follows with dialectical *necessity* from the concept of self-consciousness, and if the dialectical connection is at the same time the *genetic* one, then it must factually occur in every human being whose self-consciousness has developed. Both premises are expressly conceded; how does one propose to deny the conclusion? *Erdmann*, for whom the second premise does not hold, may do so; Prof. R., however, has caught himself in his own snares, and words do not set him free. Against both, however, the question holds how it is *possible* that a *single moment of spirit* should exclusively *fix* itself, when after all *spirit* is the absolute negativity of all its moments, and “it depends on it whether it will persist in one of its states or not, whether this state is to be for it or not” (p. 335); when it is the free power which nothing can withstand, and “to which victory is guaranteed in advance?” When Prof. R. (p. 163) says: “The conception of the spiritual genesis of derangement through the dialectic of the individual moments of intelligence [p. II.106] is very difficult. The health of spirit *demands* that they mutually *interpenetrate*; the abstraction of the one moment from the rest *must* therefore not become a brittle isolation of it, but subjectivity *must*, as *Hegel* puts it, *remain* the *ruling genius* of all the individual acts, and constantly *subordinate* their difference from one another, as well as their relation to one another, to itself as the free power, in and for itself, of the *whole movement*”; then we see that he was well acquainted with the difficulty indicated, but not that he removed it. And when he continues thereupon: “Each moment for itself is *therefore* the possibility of falling ill,” one could almost feel pity for this “innocent” *therefore*. What really lies at the bottom here is that “impotence of nature” to hold fast the concept, by virtue of which reason finally runs out into pure unreason, into blind chance. Thus we hear absolute knowing, which knows everything, speak of the absolutely *incomprehensible* precisely where the other sciences go on and on finding problems for their research. The ground lies deeper, in the impossibility of getting from pure thinking to being. The diseases of the soul, the idiosyncrasies, the passions, evil, are indeed *dialectically derived*, but they are *no longer necessary*, but *abnormal*; the dream, on the other hand, is even (p. 117) not only not necessary but also *no* longer abnormal. For what, then, it after all is, not even words seem any longer to be found. Thus one sees quite clearly how the philosopher is running ever more short of thoughts, and is compelled to set down naked *facts* as *uncomprehended* and *incomprehensible*, because they mock his dialectic as escaped birds mock the net.

Take from our physical sciences their descriptions of natural phenomena and the concept of cause, and less remains than the physics of a village maidservant. Give them pure thinking instead, and one obtains an astronomy which asserts that the planets move in circles because the circle is the most perfect line; that no more than 10 planets are possible because of the wonderful properties of the number 10; a [p. II.107] physiology which teaches that the liver is being-for-itself seeking its center, that tubercles of the lung are lung become liver, and such things, which it would be more the business of jest than of scientific seriousness to enumerate. Do the same with regard to psychology — which I called a natural science for the reason that, like natural science, it has to apprehend what is given through experience and to explain it from its causes — and one obtains — Hegelian psychology. This fulfills neither the first nor the second of the principal demands made of it: it does not say exactly *what* the states of the soul are, nor *how* and *from what* they arise. Instead, as it lies before us in Prof. R.’s work, it contains a multitude of *mutually contradictory propositions* about the relation in which it stands to those demands; it is itself completely *unclear* on this point; finally it turns out that the method by which it is governed makes the fulfillment of those demands *impossible*. But more remains to be said about this method; with this we arrive at the third part of my principal objections.

After my critique had shown that the demands mentioned above, which are to be made of every psychology, remain unfulfilled in the Hegelian psychology, it proceeded to inquire what, then, this psychology puts in place of the descriptions and explanations of the states of the soul. The answer was: dialectical development. Its application was examined in a large number of examples, and the following results emerged: 1) The dialectical method does not obtain the concepts of the individual moments of spirit through itself, but through continual taking-up from outside, through the furtive insertion of experience. 2) In order that what is given through experience may fit into the dialectical connection, one proceeds with complete arbitrariness in the use of the predetermined method, and 3) frequently disfigures the concepts of experience to the point of unrecognizability, indeed to the point of absurdity, so that only the names still let one recognize what is being talked about. The first and third results are established; the factual proofs were supplied by the first part, and what Prof. R. has recently brought forward in defense of pure thinking has been [p. II.108] assessed above. In order, however, to avoid even the appearance that something has been evaded on so important a point, let us consider what Prof. R. (pp. 386 ff.) brings forward directly against the reproach of taking from outside.

He says: “Instead of its being experience on which thinking in speculation — not, which is surely to be distinguished from it, in knowing in general — were dependent, it is rather the concept of the absolute, in its *a priori descent* from itself, that is *in advance* the *master* and regulator of all concepts of experience.” The latter may be true, though perhaps not quite in the sense in which it is said here. But does it follow from this that the concept of the absolute is also the *creator* of the other concepts? Does the servant let himself be *developed a priori* from the master, the regulated from the regulator? That, after all, was the question. He continues: “The Platonic *Idea*, the Aristotelian *entelechy*, the Spinozist *substance*, the Leibnizian *monad*, the Fichtean I are concepts which absolutely cannot be drawn from experience, even if they must be confirmed by it.” Quite right! But who has said that *all* concepts are drawn from experience? Does it follow from the contrary that *none* is? not even the concept of the yellow race of men, of the *viola tricolor*, or of desire? He continues: “What does not go back to the absolute as its ground is no philosophy in the true sense of the word.” Quite right again! But must every philosophy go back to the *Hegelian* absolute? He concludes: “Whoever does not comprehend in the concept of the absolute the principle of the self-formation and self-development of speculation cannot do otherwise than assume that the so-called *movement* of concepts is only a concept borrowed from the intuition of sensuous movement, and the so-called transition from concept to concept only an *intentional supplementing* of the one by the other.” Certainly quite right again. Whoever *admits* the possibility of the a priori construction of all concepts will not *deny* it; but precisely because I do *not* comprehend that principle of the self-development of speculation, I assume this intentional supplementing. I have also proved it, [p. II.109] by more than one fact. *And empty words*, nothing but empty words, have been set against it, as one sees, which at most bring it about *ut aliquid dixisse videatur* [that he may seem to have said something].

Shortly before (p. 385), however, Prof. R. also goes into a special case, the derivation of the concept of *nature* from the concept of the logical *Idea*. He says on this: “*Trendelenburg*, *Schelling*, *Exner* would have to show, in order to refute that transition: 1) that the abstract concept of the Idea as the unity of the concept and its reality is false; 2) that the concept of the logical Idea, as that of original reason, is not opposed by nature, which is likewise impersonal; 3) or that between the concept of the logical Idea and that of nature another concept necessarily steps in as middle term; 4) that spirit is not the subject which sublates within itself both nature and reason — as appearing spirit conditioned in its existence by nature, but free of it in itself, and raising itself in its formation to self-conscious cognition of reason, as of nature and of itself, but as eternal spirit the free productive ground, in and for itself, of reason and nature.” It is strange that Prof. R. here, instead of the psychological transitions I examined, chooses an example from general logic; it is stranger still that he ignores what has already actually been brought forward from the most diverse sides, namely by *Trendelenburg* and *Schelling*, in refutation of that transition, and, as if it did not exist, demands new refutations. If he continues in this manner, he does admittedly remain irrefutable, at least for himself. As far as I am concerned, I shall make some remarks on the concept of the logical Idea further below, in a more suitable context; but as regards his second point, I do not at all deny that impersonal *nature* is opposed to impersonal *reason*. But just as little will Prof. R. deny that *personal* reason is opposed to *impersonal* reason, and that it therefore appears merely a matter of arbitrariness when he passes from the Idea to *nature* and not at once to *spirit*. But this transition would go against a well-known relation which obtains between [p. II.110] nature and spirit, and which one knows precisely from *experience* — namely against the dependence of subjective spirit on nature; and for this reason, but not because of any necessity lying in the concept of the logical Idea, the other transition has been made. So at least it will appear until the contrary is proved. Further: although impersonal nature is opposed to impersonal reason, it by no means yet follows that the latter concept *develops dialectically* out of the former. The negation of reason is non-reason, an emptiness that contains nothing of the fullness of the positive determinations of nature, and yet is actually “its” negation, i.e. the negation of the concept of reason. Whoever finds more in it, say an asunderness, has smuggled it in. The yellow race of men too is opposed to the black, yet Prof. R. himself does not venture (p. 396) to derive the former dialectically from the latter; so *more* must after all be required for such a derivation than merely an *opposition*. — My stubborn understanding would gladly let itself be taught better on this important point, had Prof. R. only been willing to contribute something to it. As it is, I must console myself in the face of his reason, as before, with the thought that I do not, after all, stand alone. *Feuerbach* too holds in the work cited (p. 52): “Thinking negates everything, but only in order to posit everything in itself. It *no longer has a limit in anything outside* itself, but thereby it itself steps *outside its immanent, its natural limit*. Thus reason, the Idea, becomes *concrete*, i.e. what *intuition* is supposed to *give is appropriated to thinking*; *what is the function, the affair of sense, of sensation, of life*, is made into a function, an affair of *thinking*, i.e. the *concrete* is made into a *predicate of thought*, being into a mere *determinacy of thought*; for the proposition: *The concept is concrete*, *is identical* with the proposition: *Being is a determinacy of thought*. What in the Neoplatonists is presentation, phantasy, *Hegel* has merely transformed into concepts, rationalized. *Hegel* is not the ‘German or Christian Aristotle’ [p. II.111] — he is the German *Proclus*. The *absolute philosophy* is the *reborn Alexandrian philosophy*.”

Of the *arbitrariness in the application of the dialectical method*, as the second result, mentioned above, of the inquiry into what is peculiar to the Hegelian psychology, I had written the following in the first part of my critique (p. 107): “Not-A is indeed allowed to be the negation of A, but all sorts of things besides. Thus spirit is indeed a within-one-another, and hence the negation of asunderness, but on the side it is also thinking. Or Not-A is a negation that is not yet one, an intermediate negation. Thus the soul is the negation of nature, yet not yet the proper one, which only spirit is. Or it is a partial negation, like the changeable natural determinacies alongside the permanent ones. Or it is merely another concept altogether, like sex alongside age. Or it stands to A in the relation of the particular to the universal, as is mostly the case in the first part, or also in the relation of the universal to the particular, as is the case in the second and third parts. Or the later moment is for once also a lower one, spirit ‘falls down,’ whereas it ought properly to be a higher one, as with the diseases of the soul, etc. Further, B need not always be the strict unity of A and Not-A. It is occasionally also merely the mixing of the two, like the dream; or their sum, like subjectivity; or some other combination of them, like the disposition, which is taken as self-consciousness *of* feeling, not as self-consciousness *and* feeling; or it is any third concept one likes, like the symbolism of the dream, the restoration of the diseased self-consciousness; or the third member is left out altogether, and one contents oneself with two, as with the theoretical and practical spirit, etc. If, finally, all this does not help to obtain at least the semblance of a dialectical development, then in its place a superficial assemblage of a certain material in three members is offered, and declared to be an innocent trichotomy.” To this, now, something had to be replied, for the heart of the Hegelian system, the method, was at stake. On the first reproach, the [p. II.112] indeterminacy of Not-A, a separate section of the refutation (pp. 399 and 400) therefore speaks. I had earlier remarked on this subject that the strict negation of the concept A is only Not-A, which, however, leads to no new concept; that if, as frequently happens, one posits in place of Not-A some particular to which A stands opposed as the universal, the method does not decide to *which* particular the transition is to be made; that finally, if one does not take the negation strictly, any concept whatever could be regarded as the negation of any other. In this I had found the indeterminacy in the doctrine of the transition of a concept into its negation. Now in that defensive section of Prof. R.’s, all sorts of things are discussed at the beginning and at the end which decide nothing. In the dialectical process all particular logical forms are supposed to be sublated, by which, presumably, it is meant to be justified that Not-A is now the particular, now again the universal of A. Nevertheless it is further asserted that on the whole a law prevails by virtue of which the categories of being hold in the first part of psychology, those of essence in the second, those of the concept in the third; and so that one should not be too greatly astonished at this rule, set against that sublatedness of every such rule, it is at once added that every other category could after all also appear in each part. Prof. R. evidently does not wish, and not unwisely, to bind himself in advance by a strict rule, since one cannot know what freedom may yet be good for. The main passage occurs in the middle of the section, and runs: “Herr *Exner*... thinks that *anything possible* could be posited *as the negative*. The *what* of negation, he says, is indeterminate. If A is *thinking*, and B as the negation of A is to be merely Not-A in general, then B could, e.g., also be a circle, but not, as is of course said, *willing*. Herr *Exner* here takes negation merely as otherness in general, not, as the method demands, as the *other of the other*, so that A in B has over against itself not merely an existence different from it, but its *own* difference, *its otherness*.” Naturally one must now ask [p. II.113] which negation of A, then, is its *own*, *its otherness*? Is age the own negation of sex, sleep that of age? But let us hear further: “In which particular logical categories the dialectical process presents itself depends on the *constitution of the content* in each case.” With this we have once again been sent round in a circle. To which negation a determinate content passes over, the dialectical method teaches; but what the dialectical method demands, the determinate content teaches. If one speaks in earnest of the *own* negation of the concept A, which is precisely *only* its own, then this is only the *contradictory Not-A*, which, as is acknowledged, gets one no further. *Besides*, one can pass over from the content of A not merely to its contradictory but also to its contrary opposite, and not merely to one but to many; one can just as well pass over to a particular and subordinate one, and again not only to one but to many; one can pass over to a superordinate one, and again to more than one, since there are series of superordinations, and within them again coordinations; one can finally pass over from the content of the concept A to any other concept one likes, from thinking to the circle just as well as from sex to age. Where, then, does there lie in the content of a concept a principle of necessity for advancing in a *determinate direction*? All this I have already asked, but Prof. R. has replied nothing. I will therefore remark in his place that the school asserts that the concept A contains a contradiction, and that this drives to a transition in a determinate direction.

But is it true that *every* concept contains a contradiction? And *how*, *why* does the contradiction drive onward in a *determinate direction*? This is nowhere discussed, and yet method is supposed to be a firmly determined, universal form of advance. Instead of a universal determination we are referred to the application, in which the method is supposed to show itself and at the same time prove itself. But it was precisely its application to psychology that *was examined this time*, and here there appeared transitions of the most manifold kinds, to be brought under no general rule, [p. II.114] whose necessity was nowhere evident. Thus, then, the method of transition into the negation is *left indeterminate in general*, and *handled arbitrarily in application*.

Let us see whether what is said further about the nature of the dialectical method is suited to refute the reproach of the arbitrariness that prevails in it. I had remarked that the use of dichotomies must seem strange in a system which insists throughout on trichotomies, namely a concept, its negation, and the unity of both. Prof. R. replies (p. 398): “*Herr Exner* must bear in mind in general that the triplicity of the concept cannot exclude the *forms of reflection* for the determination of individual moments. It is precisely through the acknowledgment of such forms that free speculation distinguishes itself from *servile schematism* and its fanaticism of forcing everything into the same external order.” By virtue of the forms of reflection, then, he believes himself entitled to turn any triad without hesitation into a tetrad or pentad, since, as he says, it depends only on how one counts. On the next page, however, it says: “The presupposition must of course not be abandoned that the triplicity of the concept is to be carried through more and more, as time goes on, in the detail of philosophy as well. But its discovery requires, like everything human, time, and the period of the first enthusiasm, which in the joy of the insight attained anticipates everything, is followed in philosophical schools too by a period of hesitant deliberation.” Are the dyads, tetrads and the like, then, *faults* or *virtues* of the system? If Prof. R. himself has no view on this, and if the whole explanation is not perhaps merely a turn in favor of convenience and arbitrariness, then his opponents may well lay claim to indulgence. — What I objected against the *third member of the trilogies*, B as the unity of A and Not-A, has been cited above. Prof. R. replies (p. 396): “But if my critic, as to the designation of the third member of a genuine trichotomy, one really burdened with the guilt of necessity, takes offense at my using for the expression *identity* once *sum*, another time [p. II.115] *combination*, a third time *mixture*, a fourth time *unity*, a fifth time *negative identity*, a sixth time *concrete synthesis*, and so forth, then I can assure him that this was done after mature consideration, and that no harm is thereby done to the method. If Herr *Exner* will reflect on this a little, he may well come to see that identity in all those forms always determines itself precisely as the one which is precisely the only possible one.” But is a sum, then, an identity, a mixture an identity? Is five the identity of two and three, water the identity of hydrogen and oxygen, atmospheric air the identity of nitrogen and oxygen? This is a play upon the word, but not a thinking of the concept. And now let one recall how the transition to the last member of the trilogy is justified in general. A, it is said, shows itself on closer consideration to be self-contradictory, to be a Not-A; it therefore *is* Not-A; and precisely because it is this, the *identity* of A and Not-A, B, is what in truth is there. And now I ask, what sense does identity have here, what sense *must* it have? This: that A as A is in itself at the same time Not-A, but by no means that it is summed with Not-A, combined or mixed with it. Were the latter the case, the first examination of A could not have yielded a contradiction; this is present only where A is at the same time and in the same respect Not-A, but by no means where it remains beside Not-A as a summand, or as a member of a combination or mixture. So completely has Prof. R. forgotten his own principles here that I cannot believe any other Hegelian will adopt this new theory from him. The theory, I say; for with the practice the case may easily be otherwise, since it has been shown that with the strict unity of A and Not-A one likewise cannot arrive at new concepts. Prof. R. thinks one must always take that identity which is precisely the only possible one; just as it was asserted earlier that one must pass over to that negation of A to which the content of A leads. But whence, then, is one to know which in the present case [p. II.116] is the only possible one? What can be mixed can also be combined, summed; what yields a concrete synthesis also yields a negative identity. But if the content of the concepts cannot determine the nature of the identity, who will do it? *Experience*, the *taking from outside*. Through experience one knows a concept as the goal one wishes to reach, and the method is the movable rudder, which can be turned this way and that and steers the little boat into harbor without great exertion. So it lies in the nature of the matter, and so it was shown in fact. And so that this may proceed all the more easily, that identity has now been conceived so indeterminately, even against its original meaning. The arbitrariness of the method has here come to confession in a striking manner. — That taking from outside is, as was already shown in the first part, the reason why the Hegelian psychologies are so very detailed in their first part, anthropology, in which, as they themselves admit, they have the least strict knowledge, but where the material flows to them most abundantly from countless books. In Prof. R.’s 2nd ed., of the 22 pages by which it exceeds the first, again 18 fall to the first part, and only 4 to the second and third. — If one adds in thought to that *arbitrariness of the method* the demonstrated *disfigurement* of the *concepts of experience*, the *neglect* of *descriptions* and of *causal connection*, and finally the *arrogance* of a supposedly *pure thinking*, one obtains a picture of a science which, to repeat a comparison already used, looks roughly like an empty skin that has been stuffed with shavings and clippings of all things and set up as an idol. *Hegel* himself well knew and described these caricatures of genuine science. Prof. R. cites a passage from the works of his master about the bacchantic whirl of eternal becoming, in order, as he says, to vex my understanding. Not in order to vex him, which as a rule only boys take pleasure in, but in order, if possible, to prompt him to reflect on his psychology, I will cite another passage [p. II.117] which is to be read in the Phenomenology (p. 12): “As regards content, the others do sometimes make it easy enough for themselves to have a great expanse. They draw a mass of material onto their ground, namely what is already known and ordered, and since they busy themselves chiefly with oddities and curiosities, they seem all the more to possess the rest, with which knowledge in its own way was already finished, and at the same time also to master what is still unregulated, and thus to subject everything to the absolute Idea, which therewith seems to be cognized in everything and to have matured into a widely extended science. But when this extension is considered more closely, it shows itself not to have come about through one and the same thing having shaped itself in different ways; rather it is the shapeless repetition of one and the same thing, which is merely applied externally to the different material and obtains a tedious semblance of diversity. The Idea, true enough by itself, in fact always remains only at its beginning if the development consists in nothing but such a repetition of the same formula. The one unmoved form led round by the knowing subject over what is at hand, the material dipped from outside into this resting element — this is as little as arbitrary notions about the content the fulfillment of what is demanded, namely the wealth springing from itself and the self-determining difference of the shapes. It is rather a monochrome formalism, which arrives at the difference of the material only, and indeed only because this is already prepared and known.” When *Hegel* wrote this, he took himself for a prophet looking backward; but he was also a prophet for his own school.

Stepping beyond the special domain of psychology, and therefore only cursorily, I had remarked that the Hegelian philosophy, because it begins from contradictions and, moving round in a circle, does not get out of them, could not contain the solution of the speculative problems. Prof. R. sets against this (pp. 387 ff.) a more detailed section, to which I will reply with some general considerations.

[p. II.118] Every philosophy, as metaphysics, seeks the absolute. What compels it to do so? For what and why must it seek it? For if it did not have to, it would be a game to pass the time, not a business for men, for “wise men.” It lives only in thoughts; what compels it must be thoughts; what it is compelled to is in the first place an advance in thoughts. Compulsion to advance is the impossibility of standing still, and this impossibility — not, say, as a subjective one, but as a scientific one, lying in the thoughts — is present only where a thought contradicts itself. Contradictory concepts are therefore the starting point of speculation; and indeed concepts of experience, because only they contain that universal compulsion which subjective cobwebs of the brain lack, and because other concepts would already be speculation. What is sought is a thought that resolves those contradictions without itself contradicting itself; the thought of an ultimate in which all thinking finds rest, the thought of the absolute. For *experience*, then, the *absolute* is sought as something *free of contradiction*, *in order that it may* finally and completely *resolve* the contradictions lying in those concepts. — *This* is also *Hegel’s* doctrine. He starts from alteration, the change of here and there, as it presents itself in experience, or else from empty being. Empty being is indeterminate being, which is obtained by abstraction from the determinations given through experience. The one concept of experience, like the other, is contradictory to him, and with the second too it is really the first that he thinks in concealment and to which he at once openly passes over. But whether the concept of alteration is the only one to which speculation has to link itself is a question that is surely not unimportant, but is unheeded by him.

From those beginnings speculation must *advance*; advance in the course of a development necessary through the concepts themselves, not leap. The driving powers remain continually the contradictions, whether because the first ones, being not yet resolved, still continue to act, or because concepts newly generated from them have also brought new contradictions. The development must not end before the absolute has actually been reached. — That [p. II.119] contradiction is continually the driving power of speculation, and indeed in such a way that the newly generated concepts always contain new contradictions, is *Hegel’s* and also *Gabler’s* doctrine, and *Erdmann* has carried out his psychology consistently according to this thought. Only *Rosenkranz* wavers, here as everywhere. He wants to deny the restlessness of thinking not merely for the absolute but also for the subordinate moments. Thus he says in the section last cited (p. 388): “But it is quite wrong to take the *unrest of the movement* as abstract *restlessness*. According to *Hegel*, rest exists in unrest, because each moment of the whole, in its unity with the whole, is at the same time *free*.” Very well; but as moments they are not the whole, any more than every stone is the Hegelian God, and it is precisely in this respect that they are being considered. The restless rest of the whole, moreover, is the rest of a spinning top, which turns and yet stays on the same spot. It rests, only everything about it moves; whoever wants to derive subtleties from this will find models enough among ancients and moderns. Prof. R. goes still further, in that he adduces from the dialectical series of concepts of his system a very important concept as an example of one that does not pass over, and so also contains no contradiction: the concept of nature. He continues the above passage thus: “Nature, e.g., is in complete *correspondence* with the concept of the Idea as the unity of the concept and its reality, and yet it is in *contradiction* with it in that it is this unity only in an *unthinking*, *non-self-conscious* way. But this contradiction is not present at all *for nature* itself. It is *sufficient unto itself*. It draws immortal life out of itself, as the Platonic *Timaeus* says, and is in itself utterly indifferent to its *being thought* and to the *history* of spirit. Hence it is not nature as such that goes out beyond itself; rather the *Idea* goes, in it, *out beyond itself as nature*.” This passage might arouse some astonishment among Hegelians themselves. The concept of nature is supposed not to contradict itself because nature has no self-consciousness! Have the concepts of being, becoming, determinate being, etc., then, a self-consciousness? [p. II.120] That, further, the *Idea* passes over as nature is quite correct, for the Idea does everything; but because it passes over *as nature*, it is precisely nature that passes over and *does not rest*. With the above passage one may compare another from *Erdmann* (“Logic and Metaphysics,” 2nd ed., p. 176): “This transition (of the Idea) to nature is *not* a transition of the Idea, for the Idea, as completed within itself, is satisfied within itself, has no longer, as hitherto, to complete itself; nor does it any longer *become* something other, because no lack and no limit is contained in it; rather, the science of logic passes over to the science of nature, or *we* pass over from the Idea to nature.” Here, then, the Idea does not pass over at all; there it alone passes over. This passage is moreover very remarkable in that it teaches that the thing at which *Hegel’s* opponents took so much offense — namely the transition of the Idea into nature — does not exist at all. Rather, it says, the dialectical connection here breaks off clean, and in its place steps the contingent act of subjective thinking. With this, the taking from outside is conceded. We see, then, that with regard to speculative progression our philosophers are neither agreed among themselves nor each of them clear with himself.

It was further said above that the absolute must not be reached by *leaps*, but only in a development necessary through the concepts themselves. So one may also not make unproved *presuppositions* about the constitution of the absolute in order to arrange the progress according to them. One may therefore not presuppose that the absolute is a *unity*, even if it were true that there lies in us a drive to reduce the many to a unity; for a drive is no guarantee of a warrant. It might perhaps after all be possible that there is a plurality of the absolute; in any case science must not arbitrarily block the paths of inquiry. — But the Hegelian system and all systems related to it make that presupposition without hesitation, and *Gabler* says in the work mentioned (p. 127): “Dialectic does indeed ascend to the last and highest oppositions, and then *demands* their unity, in which what is distinguished is also supposed to be something not distinguished.” Indeed, he assures us [p. II.121] (p. 120): “That such a One (from which everything has its origin, as well as its derivation and explanation) exists, it is superfluous to want to prove specially as well; for every thinking and every consciousness already has it, and is unable to reject it from itself, since it is most intimately bound up with the very substantial life of spirit.” — Still less may one presuppose, as happens in what immediately precedes the passage just quoted, and elsewhere too so frequently in the kindred systems, that this One is *God*. Along with this presupposition the other one usually forces its way in at once, namely that the objects of the contradictory empirical concepts which gave occasion to speculation are related to the object of the last concept, up to which the development advances, as the created is to its creator. But it might turn out that the absolute at which inquiry arrives from the empirical concepts by strict development is far from worthy of being designated by the name of God. This *Schleiermacher* did not fail to recognize, however much he otherwise loves the unscientific presupposition of unity. — Just as little may one presuppose that this One is the *unity of thinking and being*. The assertion of this unity resounds at us from all sides in the Hegelian school; but that it is only a presupposition, lacking all proof, has also been shouted back at it from all sides. Or would one seriously put it forward as a proof when it is said: Thinking is, therefore thinking and being are one? Ordinary logic resolves the judgment: Thinking is, into: Thinking has being, and holds that the propositions: Thinking *has* being, thinking *is* being, are very different; but this logic will accomplish little here. Let us note, then, that only of *subjective* thinking can it be said, in the meaning of being presupposed here, that it *is*, whereas there thinking *in itself* is taken as concept, so that the proposition thus contains a little amphiboly. That proposition is also sometimes interpreted to mean that being is “the nature of beings, the concept, the universality of the thing” (*Rosenkranz* p. 389), where it thus has the sense: The universal [p. II.122] is the universal. Against this presupposition there would surely be nothing to object; it is quite innocent, does neither good nor harm — if only one does not, where one speaks of the unity of subjectivity and objectivity in reason, of the unity of the concept and reality in the Idea, again slip in a quite different meaning, about which one gets very heated indeed, whereas the former will surely never arouse any particular interest. The presuppositions noted in the Hegelian school are a taking-from-outside of another kind, but one no less unscientific.

If speculation has really come as far as the absolute, it must have reached a concept that no longer contains any contradiction, and with which it therefore finds rest. That this is not the case in the Hegelian speculation I asserted; Prof. R., however, has contradicted me. Before I answer, the question will not be inappropriate: what, then, is *Hegel’s* absolute? Herewith we come upon a split in the school which has caused sensation enough. Here the *Idea* is named, there *absolute spirit*. With the former view are connected the becoming of absolute spirit, its existence only in human spirits, the perishing of individuals; with the latter the personality of God, individual immortality, and much else that is well known and important. The public’s confidence in the Hegelian system suffered its first severe blow through this difference. The first opinion seems to have consistency on its side, and it looks most peculiar when beside it the other appears with so ingenuous a mien, as though it had no inkling at all that anyone could do it any harm. *Gabler* declares himself decidedly for the second; Prof. R. also agrees with it, though, as usual, not without some wavering. So, e.g., p. 330, where “the Idea alone is true being;” or p. 386, where the absolute is called “the eternal unity of being and thinking,” a definition which, as is well known, is otherwise given of the Idea; or p. 389, where the Idea is substituted for absolute spirit. Yet he writes to *Leroux* (p. 21): “*Hegel’s* absolutely-absolute is absolute spirit, which, as the self-mediating unity of absolute [p. II.123] substantiality and absolute subjectivity in its world-free, and therefore world-creating, personality, produces being, thinking and willing only as moments of its eternally self-same totality,” and here and there in his psychology he speaks of God and immortality in a thoroughly Christian sense. Whether, now, the absolute Idea or absolute spirit be *Hegel’s* true absolute, we must ask whether *contradiction-free* concepts have in fact been gained thereby.

In the absolute Idea all categories, in absolute spirit all oppositions, are sublated. They are still there, because preserved; but they are also no longer there, because the truth of the oppositions is their unity. So it is taught. But the *unity of opposites* was *rejected* above, and if all oppositions are *preserved* in the absolute, then the latter is precisely *the very greatest contradiction*. Prof. R. says in the open letter to *Leroux* (p. 38): “Every stage of the system contains a definition of God. But to the absolute concept of God it belongs precisely to think the *totality* of the different definitions as *identity*.” We know well enough so far how one has to go about *calling* that totality identity, but not how one could *think* it as such. *Erdmann* remarks (p. 160) very rightly that analysis easily finds contradictions in the Idea; but it will be in vain to forbid science this analysis. We will not insist that the Hegelian view of God is a completely pantheistic one; were it scientifically justified, science would have to bear it. All the same, where people appeal to the universal human, nay to the Christian consciousness in confirmation of it, or even as proof that one is entitled to *presuppose* it, we could indeed express our astonishment that right beside such appeals we read (*Gabler* p. 163): “Spirit in its infinite ideality and universality, whose moving pulse is negativity or vitality itself, is and *becomes everything*, and is the *ideal activity* of everything, as much as the differentiation as the unification of everything.” But here it suffices that, if one gathers all contradictions together into one [p. II.124] heap, the mere assurance that there they now all are one does not suffice to sublate them. Rather, one must say with *Feuerbach* (p. 37): “Just as little as the faith restored again out of unbelief is a true faith, because it is always encumbered with its opposite, just so little is the God who restores himself again out of his negation a true God; he is rather a *self-contradicting*, an *atheistic* God.” — Let us look more closely at how the absolute is characterized. According to *Gabler* it is *activity*, absolute self-activity, thinking. Well, activity is a *becoming*, and becoming is, according to *Hegel* himself, a *contradiction*! The same contradiction which was present on the lowest stage of speculation, and was supposed to be sublated already on the second — that same contradiction, then, is found again on the highest, in the concept of the absolute, unresolved; speculation has *not come one step closer* to its goal. It is pure arbitrariness in the use of the word, by which the concept is not touched at all, when Prof. R. (p. 389) wants to restrict becoming to the domain of appearances. It is rather present quite as well wherever something *differentiates* itself within itself, wherever thinking *posits* being, whether it remains with itself at the same time or not. That restriction is moreover a contradiction of the Hegelian speculation, which sublates the distinction between appearance and thing in itself; it is also a contradiction of the other assertion, that it is the Idea which passes over *as nature*. It is precisely by this that the whole group of philosophical systems to which the Hegelian one too belongs is characterized: that absolute becoming is the pervasive principal thought. If he asserts that this movement is at the same time rest, he has only produced a new contradiction, which would be present even if movement, becoming in itself, did not contradict itself. *Hegel* defines: “Absolute spirit is identity that is *eternally* within itself as much as it is identity *returning* and *returned* into itself; the one and universal substance as *spiritual*, the judgment *into itself* and *into a knowing for which* it is as such.” Now if *eternal being* and *returning*, *returning* and *returned*, *substance* and *knowing about* [p. II.125] the *substance* not oppositions whose unifications yield *contradictions*? — Were God, moreover, the metaphysical absolute, then his concept would not merely have to be set up quite differently from the way it is done here, i.e. free of contradictions, but it would also have to possess complete clarity and determinateness for science, since through it all the speculative difficulties of our entire circle of thought were supposed to be removed. On the contrary, however, the history of speculation teaches that it has from time immemorial itself been one of the *hardest problems* of science, so hard that it was frequently declared insoluble, and it was left to faith to penetrate to where the strict knowledge of man seemed unable to reach.

If the absolute that is offered us here is not free of inner contradictions, then thinking too can find no rest in it; it must stride onward once again. This *Hegel* acknowledged when he taught that absolute spirit *lowers* itself again to empty being; in doing so he implicitly acknowledged at the same time the existence of contradictions in absolute spirit, because only in them does a necessity of progress lie. If Prof. R. asserts (p. 390): “The externalization into the breadth of nature, into the length of history, are indeed determinations which are in and for themselves inappropriate to the concept of God,” and if he therefore interposes the Idea of the Logos, then the externalization has merely turned from an immediate into a *mediate* one. The same necessity of progress is acknowledged also by all who, like Prof. R. (p. 403), teach with *Hegel*: “The truth is only the whole in its self-movement, which circles through all the moments of the matter;” or: what is there is the eternal process. That it goes round in a circle and not forward in a straight line changes nothing; if it is process, and eternal process, it can be so only by *each* moment, as self-contradictory, driving beyond itself. When Prof. R. (p. 389) declares that the progress in a circle is a *being rounded off in itself*, he only offers, as has already been remarked, one empty word more, or one contradiction more. If he asks whether I conceive the rest in absolute spirit as the rest [p. II.126] of the churchyard, I say: No. But precisely for that reason I declare that God, as the absolute of the *world*, is not the absolute of *science*, i.e. that his concept itself still contains a compulsion to further inquiry, is itself a *problem* of science. I am far from believing that the power of the Godhead is more comprehensible than the power with which the magnet attracts iron. — It is interesting to see how the might of contradiction drives beyond the assumed absolute in *different* directions. *Gabler* had declared God, self-conscious absolute thinking, the absolute knowing of himself and of his being, to be the absolute. In this there obviously lies a multiplicity of determinations which, taken as a strict unity, becomes a contradiction. Therefore it is asserted that the being in God is itself posited by the thinking in God. But herewith it is no longer the self-conscious *God*, knowing about his being, but *thinking* that is the *true prius*, the *absolute* proper, and *Gabler* has drawn near to those who declare the *Idea* to be the absolute. Whether one can stop at thinking, an activity, a becoming, has been discussed above.

When speculation has reached its absolute as the last necessary presupposition through which the contradictions in the empirical concepts are removed and experience itself becomes comprehensible, it can attempt to descend by the reverse path from the last conditions to experience; and if it has succeeded in this, then it has passed its test. Thus *experience*, sharply apprehended, is its *basis*; and its *task* is solved when the *contradictions* are not merely shifted but really *removed*. To have accomplished this completely, no philosophical system may well boast to this day; but it can stand nearer to its goal or farther from it. The Hegelian school has not exactly distinguished itself hitherto by a faithful apprehension of experience; the concepts it mixes into one another; the difficulties in them it rolls upward from stage to stage and into the absolute, and declares that there they all no longer exist. What, pray, do we learn about the nature of intuiting when we are told that its ground is [p. II.127] presenting, and still further up the absolute negativity of absolute spirit — nay, that it is itself in truth properly presenting, and this thinking, and this absolute spirit? “To set this one knowledge,” says *Hegel*, “that in the absolute everything is the same, against differentiating and fulfilled cognition, or cognition seeking and demanding fulfillment — or to pass off one’s *absolute* as the night in which, as the saying goes, all cows are black, is the naivety of emptiness of cognition.”

So stands it with the Hegelian speculation; and from this one may see whether science can be content when, in psychology, instead of exact descriptions of the states of the soul and their derivation from their causal connection, it is offered as a substitute a dialectical development of the kind described in the foregoing. *I believe I am entitled to the conclusion that all the principal objections which I raised against the psychology of the Hegelian school have remained unrefuted.*

I too, for that matter, have not answered everything that Prof. R. brings forward against me in his “Refutation,” though I have answered everything that seemed of significance for the matter in question. But I believe that by what I have said I have earned the right that no one should assent to any reproach of my opponent that I have left unmentioned before he has himself looked at the passage of my critique to which the censure applies. On that basis I can calmly await the verdict.

Prof. R. says at the close of his refutation that he by no means hopes it will make the impression of a refutation on this person or that, and continues: “But that I have not shrunk from looking my opponent in the face, and have done my duty toward him, people will at least have to attest, whether they share my view or not.” Will people have to attest this of him? He has formed a grimace out of contemptible insinuations and set it before the public as my countenance; it, not me, he has looked in the face. Where reasons were called for, he has answered with the haughty reproach of obstinate understanding-mindedness and of misunderstanding, and where he ought to have refuted, [p. II.128] he has often kept silent, or evaded the question under the semblance of answering it. I had made against his work the grave reproach of dilettantism, which plays on the surface and does not know the depths, and not seldom the still graver one of drivel, which, as he himself says, thinks without connection and leaps from half-measure to half-measure; and now it has been shown anew that exact apprehension of concepts, sharpness of judgment, rigor of progressive thinking are not to be found in it, that it gropes about in the dark on the vital questions of its science, and, where views conflict within and outside its own school, leaps helplessly from one side to the other. If by such conduct one does one’s duty in science, then it has been done here; I, however, cannot take back my reproaches. Science itself will decide which of us is right. — Since I have nothing essential to add to what has been said, this polemic will now rest forever on my part.

But I do not wish to close with such harsh dissonances. Let something rather stand here on which even opponents in science can agree. It is no trifle that is at issue between us, but, in the last instance, truths with which the welfare and the glory of our common fatherland are intimately bound up. This may excuse some zeal in the polemic. The Hegelian system is the most fully elaborated form of the thought of a supreme, speculative unity in connection with the absolute becoming inseparable from it. This thought is as old as philosophy and as widespread as it; it reaches back into Greek antiquity and was, as it seems, at home among the sages of India. As the one of the two possible principal metaphysical views, it claims the greatest attention and must be defended to the utmost. At the moment when its adherents grew weak and began to conduct its defense badly, its opponents would be obliged to take it over, until every little fold of it has been spread open and the worth or worthlessness of its content lies spread out in the light of the sun. The same holds of the [p. II.129] opposite view, which sets being over against becoming, plurality over against unity. And at some point it must surely come to this, that we have a metaphysics; at some point, surely, abiding convictions must take the place of the humiliating change of systems, and philosophers be able to unite their forces in concord, not merely for tearing down but for building further in common. Let no one assert that the change of systems is in this field a necessity for all time; it is only a weakness, and to declare it necessary means renouncing knowledge. But concord is impossible as long as the struggle between those two fundamental views is not decided; therefore it is a duty for both sides. And yet we already have so much of struggle on all sides that the honor of science and of persons seems scarcely able to bear any more of it. It is true that it is not very long since mathematicians and physicists lay in no less violent strife among themselves than the philosophers still do now, although in their present good fortune they easily forget the sufferings of their past. If they arrived at agreement earlier, their business was precisely in this respect the easier one. Over against this agreement, the strife of the philosophers is a twofold evil: it is a weakness of the systems, and it diminishes the respect of the public, which no science can do without. Under these circumstances, however, there is nothing to do but to bear it with patience, like any other evil, and to fight nowhere without need, but, where it must be, *conscientiously*.

[scan 260]
